EDIC Part II — Comparative & Bedside Simulations (CBS)

Weighted Blueprint & Consultant-Level Question Bank

150 stations · 50 imaging / 50 curves / 50 biochemistry ESICM EDIC Guidelines 2023–2026 CoBaTrICE-mapped Phase 1 — Sample Release
Phase 1 sample. This file currently contains the full blueprint, usage guide, and CoBaTrICE mapping, plus three sample stations — one imaging, one curves/waveforms, one biochemistry/data — built to the final structure and styling. Imaging and waveform stations use a hybrid image approach: an original schematic SVG diagram built to match the described findings (works offline, no licensing dependency), plus a "Real reference images" box with search-link(s) only to Radiopaedia and/or LITFL, so you can view an actual patient image or tracing of the same pattern. Direct/topic-page links were tried in an earlier draft and dropped after review — one pointed at a generic library index rather than the relevant content, and one (Deranged Physiology) was dead — so only each site's real search endpoint is used, which reliably surfaces relevant real cases without needing this session's blocked network access to verify a specific link. This may be revisited later if a more reliable way to link/verify individual cases becomes available. Remaining stations will be added in batches of 25–30, respecting the blueprint distribution below, after review of these samples.

How to use this resource

This bank contains 200 EDIC Part II–style stations:

  • 50 CCS (20 long, 30 short)
  • 150 CBS (50 imaging, 50 curves/waveforms, 50 biochemistry/data)

All stations are consultant-level, aligned with the EDIC Guidelines 2023–2026 and the CoBaTrICE syllabus.

Suggested approach

CCS stations

  • Long cases: allow yourself 10 minutes to read the opening vignette, then 25 minutes to answer out loud in structured bullet points, as in the real exam.
  • Short cases: 5 minutes reading, 12–13 minutes answering.
  • Answer out loud first, then write your bullet points. If you cannot say it in structured order in 60 seconds, you do not truly have it.
  • Before revealing the model answer, set your confidence: Confident / Unsure / Guessing. Do not adjust it afterwards.
  • Then reveal the model answer and mark yourself as an examiner would. Partial credit is allowed; benefit of the doubt is not.

CBS stations

  • Treat each as a 12-minute station with 8–12 expected answers.
  • Go through the images/curves/data in order, speaking your answers out loud, then write them down.
  • Set confidence before revealing, then mark yourself strictly.

Tracking your progress

  • For each station, record: confidence, marks awarded, and total marks.
  • Periodically review your performance by domain (respiratory, cardiovascular, neuro, etc.) to identify weak areas.
  • Re-sit selected stations after 2–4 weeks without re-reading the model answers first; the delta is the only measure that matters.

Blueprint coverage

  • The distribution of stations follows the EDIC Part II blueprint derived from CoBaTrICE:
    • Heavy emphasis on respiratory, cardiovascular, neurology, and infection.
    • Substantial coverage of renal/metabolic, GI/hepatic, haematology/oncology, endocrine/metabolic-toxic, obstetric, trauma, and end-of-life/ethics.
  • CBS stations emphasise data interpretation (imaging, curves, biochemistry) across all systems.

Use this bank as a calibration and gap-diagnostic tool, not just a question list. The goal is to expose guideline drift, strengthen structured verbal answers, and build exam-ready fluency.

Mapping to CoBaTrICE domains

The 12 CoBaTrICE domains (plus basic sciences) underpin all stations:

  1. Resuscitation & initial management of the acutely ill patient
  2. Diagnosis: assessment, investigation, monitoring & data interpretation
  3. Disease management
  4. Therapeutic interventions
  5. Practical procedures
  6. Peri-operative care
  7. Comfort & recovery
  8. End-of-life care
  9. Paediatric care
  10. Transport
  11. Patient safety & health management system
  12. Professionalism & communication
  • CCS stations primarily map to domains 1, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 11, 12.
  • CBS stations primarily map to domains 2, 3, 4, 5.

Each station's metadata lists the primary CoBaTrICE domains it targets.

Sample Stations

CBS_IMG_01 — Portable Chest Radiograph in Severe Hypoxaemic Respiratory Failure

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 61-year-old woman, ventilated for 36 hours for severe community-acquired pneumonia, has a supine portable AP chest radiograph performed for worsening oxygenation (PaO2/FiO2 95 mmHg). Endotracheal tube and a right internal jugular central line are in place.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "ARDS chest x-ray" (cases)
  • LITFL — search: "ARDS chest x-ray"

Search links only (direct/topic-page links were dropped — less reliable and not verifiable from this session). Individual images on these sites are typically CC BY-NC-SA — check the licence on the specific case before reusing or redistributing it yourself.

Structured questions

  1. Describe this radiograph systematically, as you would present it to an examiner.
  2. What is your differential diagnosis for this pattern, and which single bedside investigation would most efficiently narrow it in this patient?
  3. Relate the appearance to a formal diagnostic framework for this patient's respiratory failure.
  4. Name three further tests or manoeuvres you would perform in the next hour and what each would add.
Reveal model answer

1. Systematic description

  • Confirm patient details, projection (AP, supine, portable), rotation and inspiratory effort — adequacy should be caveated for a portable supine film.
  • Lines/tubes: endotracheal tube tip appropriately positioned above the carina (roughly 3–5 cm above, mid-trachea with neck neutral); right internal jugular central line with tip projecting appropriately at the cavoatrial junction — state you would confirm on a magnified view.
  • Lung fields: bilateral, fairly symmetric, diffuse airspace (alveolar) opacification with visible air bronchograms, sparing no zone — consistent with diffuse alveolar filling rather than a focal process.
  • Heart size: cannot be reliably assessed on a supine AP film (cardiothoracic ratio not valid), but no gross cardiomegaly apparent; no obvious upper-lobe diversion or peribronchial cuffing to strongly favour cardiogenic oedema, though supine positioning limits this assessment.
  • Pleura: no obvious pneumothorax or large effusion; costophrenic angles not fully assessable supine.
  • Soft tissues/bones: no surgical emphysema, no obvious fracture.

2. Differential diagnosis and bedside investigation

  • Differential: ARDS (from pneumonia/sepsis as here), cardiogenic pulmonary oedema, diffuse alveolar haemorrhage, aspiration, acute interstitial pneumonia/acute eosinophilic pneumonia, less likely miliary infection given acuity.
  • The single most efficient bedside test to narrow this is focused critical care echocardiography — assessing LV systolic function, filling status, and excluding significant cardiogenic contribution, which is central to the diagnostic requirement to exclude cardiac failure as the sole/main explanation.

3. Formal diagnostic framework

  • Apply the 2023 Global Definition of ARDS (Matthay et al.), which updates the 2012 Berlin Definition: acute onset (within 1 week of a known insult — here, pneumonia), bilateral opacities not fully explained by effusion/collapse/nodules — now explicitly acceptable on chest X-ray, CT, or lung ultrasound (Berlin recognised only X-ray/CT) — and respiratory failure not fully explained by cardiac failure or fluid overload (requires objective assessment, e.g. echo, if no clear ARDS risk factor — though a clear risk factor is present here). The 2023 update also removed Berlin's requirement for invasive ventilation or NIV/CPAP ≥5 cmH2O, permitting diagnosis in patients on high-flow nasal oxygen ≥30 L/min using SpO2/FiO2 ≤315 (SpO2 ≤97%) where an arterial gas is unavailable — not directly relevant to this already-intubated patient, but a key distinction for less severe presentations.
  • With PaO2/FiO2 95 mmHg on adequate PEEP, this represents moderate ARDS (101–200 mmHg band; note 95 mmHg would be at the moderate/severe boundary — confirm the PEEP level at which this was measured, as the ratio is only valid at PEEP/CPAP ≥5 cmH2O). This severity banding is unchanged by the 2023 update for intubated patients.

4. Further tests/manoeuvres in the next hour

  • Focused echocardiography — as above, to exclude a significant cardiogenic contribution and assess RV function/strain from high intrathoracic pressures.
  • Repeat/confirm ETT and line position on a magnified view or by direct laryngoscopy if any doubt, given the implications of a malpositioned airway device in a hypoxaemic patient.
  • Lung ultrasound at the bedside — rapid, radiation-free assessment for B-lines (interstitial/alveolar syndrome), effusion, and to help exclude pneumothorax; complements the radiograph and can be repeated serially without transport risk.
Marking guide

Award up to 10 marks across: systematic ABCDE-style description (3), correct identification of ETT/line position as first priority (1), a coherent differential diagnosis (2), correct application of the current (2023 Global) definition with correct severity band (2), sensible next steps with justification (2).

Common error / drift trap: Candidates frequently attempt to comment on cardiothoracic ratio or use classic upright-film cardiomegaly criteria on a supine AP portable film — this is invalid and should be explicitly flagged as a limitation rather than quantified. Similarly, candidates should not state the PaO2/FiO2 ratio is diagnostic in isolation without confirming it was measured on PEEP/CPAP ≥5 cmH2O.
Guideline-drift trap: Citing only the 2012 Berlin Definition, unqualified, is itself a drift trap. The 2023 Global Definition of ARDS (Matthay et al.) superseded Berlin, notably by accepting lung ultrasound as an imaging modality and by permitting diagnosis in patients on high-flow nasal oxygen rather than requiring invasive/NIV support — candidates should cite the current definition, not Berlin alone.

References

  1. Matthay MA, Arabi Y, Arroliga AC, et al. A New Global Definition of Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2024;209(1):37–47.
  2. ARDS Definition Task Force, Ranieri VM, Rubenfeld GD, et al. Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome: the Berlin Definition. JAMA. 2012;307(23):2526–2533. (superseded by the 2023 Global Definition above; retained here for historical/exam-recall context)
  3. Lichtenstein DA, Mezière GA. Relevance of lung ultrasound in the diagnosis of acute respiratory failure: the BLUE protocol. Chest. 2008;134(1):117–125.
  4. Expert Round Table on Echocardiography in ICU. International consensus statement on training standards for critical care ultrasonography. Intensive Care Med. 2011;37:1077–1083.
  5. European Society of Intensive Care Medicine (ESICM). EDIC Guidelines 2023–2026. Available at: esicm.org/edic

CBS_IMG_02 — Saddle Pulmonary Embolus on CT Pulmonary Angiogram

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old woman collapses with sudden hypotension (BP 82/54 mmHg) and hypoxaemia. CT pulmonary angiogram is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CTPA findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "saddle pulmonary embolus CTPA" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the filling defect and its location.
  2. What additional measurement on this same study is essential to risk-stratify her, and why?
  3. What other CT signs would you actively look for?
  4. How does this correlate with her clinical risk category and management?
Reveal model answer
  • A large filling defect straddles the pulmonary artery bifurcation and extends into both main pulmonary arteries — a saddle embolus.
  • Essential additional assessment: the RV:LV diameter ratio on the same study — a ratio >1.0 indicates right ventricular strain and is associated with worse prognosis, independent of the clot burden itself.
  • Other signs of RV strain to seek: interventricular septal bowing/flattening toward the LV, reflux of contrast into the IVC/hepatic veins, and a dilated main pulmonary artery.
  • Given her hypotension (high-risk/"massive" PE by ESC classification), the priority is immediate reperfusion consideration rather than anticoagulation alone — see CCS_S05 for the full management discussion, including the tension around thrombolysis in specific bleeding-risk contexts.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/location of the filling defect (3), correct emphasis on RV:LV ratio as essential (4), other strain signs named (2), correct link to clinical risk category (1).

Common error / drift trap: Focusing only on identifying the clot without assessing RV strain is a common omission — the RV:LV ratio and secondary strain signs are as important as the embolus itself for risk-stratifying and guiding therapy.

References

  1. Konstantinides SV, Meyer G, Becattini C, et al. 2019 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of acute pulmonary embolism. Eur Heart J. 2020;41(4):543–603.

CBS_IMG_03 — Aortic Dissection on CT Aortogram

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old hypertensive man presents with sudden tearing chest pain radiating to the back. CT aortogram is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT aortogram findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "aortic dissection CT angiogram" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the key finding and how you distinguish true from false lumen.
  2. Classify this dissection and explain why the classification matters.
  3. What complications would you actively look for on this study?
  4. How does management differ by classification?
Reveal model answer
  • An intimal flap divides the aortic lumen into a true and false lumen. The true lumen is typically smaller, higher-attenuation, and continuous with the undissected aorta; the false lumen is often larger and crescentic, sometimes showing a "cobweb" sign or slower flow.
  • Stanford classification: Type A involves the ascending aorta (surgical emergency); Type B is confined to the descending aorta distal to the left subclavian artery (generally managed medically unless complicated). This distinction fundamentally changes management.
  • Actively look for: pericardial effusion/tamponade, malperfusion of branch vessels (coronary, renal, mesenteric, limb), and aortic regurgitation.
  • Management: Type A is a surgical emergency requiring urgent cardiothoracic surgery. Uncomplicated Type B is managed medically with aggressive heart rate/blood pressure control ("impulse control" therapy); complicated Type B (malperfusion, rupture, refractory pain/hypertension) requires intervention, typically TEVAR.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct true/false lumen distinction (3), correct Stanford classification and rationale (3), complications named (2), correct management distinction by type (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming all aortic dissections require emergency surgery is incorrect — uncomplicated Type B dissections are generally managed medically; conversely, a Type A dissection is a surgical emergency and should never be managed medically alone.

References

  1. Erbel R, Aboyans V, Boileau C, et al. 2014 ESC Guidelines on the diagnosis and treatment of aortic diseases. Eur Heart J. 2014;35(41):2873–2926.

CBS_IMG_04 — Pneumothorax on a Supine ICU Chest Radiograph

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A ventilated ICU patient desaturates suddenly. A supine portable chest radiograph is obtained (an erect film is not possible).

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "deep sulcus sign pneumothorax" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Why are supine films insensitive for pneumothorax, and what subtle sign is shown here?
  2. What other supine-specific signs would you look for?
  3. If this patient had tension physiology, would you wait for this film before treating?
  4. How would you confirm the diagnosis if it remained uncertain and the patient was stable?
Reveal model answer
  • Supine films are insensitive for pneumothorax because free air rises anteriorly and medially rather than apically as on an erect film, so the classic apical lucent rim with a visible visceral pleural line may not be seen. The key subtle sign here is the deep sulcus sign — an abnormally deep, lucent costophrenic angle.
  • Other supine signs: a hyperlucent upper quadrant, an unusually sharply outlined cardiac border/ mediastinum, a visible anterior costophrenic sulcus, and the "double diaphragm" sign.
  • No — with tension physiology, treat immediately on clinical grounds without waiting for imaging (see CCS_S13); imaging should never delay decompression in a haemodynamically compromising tension pneumothorax.
  • If stable and diagnosis uncertain, bedside lung ultrasound is more sensitive than a supine film — look for absent lung sliding and the "barcode"/"stratosphere" sign on M-mode.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct explanation of supine insensitivity and deep sulcus sign (4), other supine signs named (2), correct "treat, don't wait for imaging" answer for tension physiology (2), correct ultrasound confirmation answer (2).

Common error / drift trap: Relying on classic erect-film pneumothorax signs on a supine ICU film can miss the diagnosis — the deep sulcus sign and other subtle supine-specific signs must be actively sought, and bedside ultrasound is more sensitive than a supine film in this setting.

References

  1. Lichtenstein DA, Mezière GA. Relevance of lung ultrasound in the diagnosis of acute respiratory failure: the BLUE protocol. Chest. 2008;134(1):117–125.

CBS_IMG_05 — Massive Pleural Effusion vs Lobar Collapse: Mediastinal Shift

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Two ICU patients each have a dense, near-complete "white-out" of the left hemithorax on chest radiograph. Compare the two.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagrams constructed to represent the described radiographic patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "massive pleural effusion vs lung collapse chest x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. State the single most useful discriminating sign between the two panels, and apply it.
  2. What other features support each diagnosis?
  3. How would you confirm and further characterise each?
  4. How does management differ between the two?
Reveal model answer
  • The key discriminator is the direction of mediastinal shift: a large space-occupying process (effusion) pushes the mediastinum away from the white-out (Panel A); volume loss (collapse) pulls the mediastinum toward the white-out (Panel B).
  • Supporting features — effusion: a meniscus sign, relatively uniform density, costophrenic angle blunting. Collapse: volume-loss signs such as crowded ribs, an elevated hemidiaphragm, and displaced fissures.
  • Confirm with bedside ultrasound (rapid, distinguishes fluid from consolidation/collapse, guides thoracocentesis) or CT if diagnosis remains unclear.
  • Management differs fundamentally: a large symptomatic effusion requires drainage; collapse requires identifying and treating the cause (e.g. mucus plug — consider bronchoscopy, physiotherapy).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification of shift direction as the key sign, applied correctly to both panels (5), supporting features named (2), sensible confirmatory imaging (1), correct, contrasting management (2).

Common error / drift trap: Not actively assessing mediastinal shift direction is a common oversight that can lead to misdiagnosis between a large effusion and lobar/lung collapse — two conditions with opposite management implications (drainage vs bronchoscopy/physiotherapy).

References

  1. Corcoran JP, Wrightson JM, Belcher E, et al. Pleural procedures and thoracic ultrasound: British Thoracic Society Pleural Disease Guideline 2010. Thorax. 2010;65(Suppl 2):ii61–ii76.

CBS_IMG_06 — Subarachnoid Haemorrhage on Non-Contrast CT Head

Imaging Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old woman presents with sudden "thunderclap" headache. Non-contrast CT head is performed within 3 hours of onset.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "subarachnoid haemorrhage non-contrast CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern shown and state the diagnosis.
  2. How would you grade this using a validated scale, and why does grading matter?
  3. How does CT sensitivity change with time from symptom onset, and what does this mean for a negative scan?
  4. What is your next investigation?
Reveal model answer
  • Star-shaped hyperdensity filling the basal cisterns and proximal sulci is the classic appearance of subarachnoid haemorrhage (SAH).
  • Grade using the modified Fisher scale, which correlates blood quantity/distribution with the risk of subsequent vasospasm/delayed cerebral ischaemia — a thick, diffuse pattern like this one places her at higher risk, informing surveillance intensity (see CCS_S06).
  • CT sensitivity for SAH is very high (~95–98%) within the first 6 hours of onset, but declines substantially over the following days as blood is resorbed. A negative CT beyond this window, especially with a convincing thunderclap headache history, does not exclude SAH and warrants further investigation.
  • Next step: CT angiography to identify a causative aneurysm and guide urgent neurosurgical/ interventional referral.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern description and diagnosis (3), correct grading scale and its purpose (3), correct time-dependent sensitivity point (2), correct next investigation (2).

Common error / drift trap: A negative CT is sometimes assumed to definitively exclude SAH regardless of timing. Sensitivity is very high within the first 6 hours but declines significantly thereafter — a negative scan beyond this window with a convincing history still warrants further investigation (LP for xanthochromia or CT angiography), not reassurance alone.

References

  1. Hoh BL, Ko NU, Amin-Hanjani S, et al. 2023 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Aneurysmal Subarachnoid Hemorrhage. Stroke. 2023;54(7):e314–e370.
  2. Frontera JA, Claassen J, Schmidt JM, et al. Prediction of symptomatic vasospasm after subarachnoid hemorrhage: the modified Fisher scale. Neurosurgery. 2006;59(1):21–27.

CBS_IMG_07 — Extradural vs Subdural Haematoma

Imaging Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Two trauma patients each have an extra-axial haematoma on CT head. Compare their appearances.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagrams constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "extradural vs subdural haematoma CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the shape difference between the two and explain why it occurs.
  2. What is the typical mechanism/vessel for each?
  3. How does appearance change with age of the haematoma, and what pitfall does this create?
  4. What determines the threshold for surgical evacuation?
Reveal model answer
  • Extradural haematoma (EDH): biconvex/lentiform, does not cross suture lines — limited by dural attachment at the sutures. Subdural haematoma (SDH): crescentic, crosses suture lines but is limited by dural reflections (falx, tentorium).
  • EDH classically arises from middle meningeal artery injury, often with a "lucid interval" following initial loss of consciousness. SDH arises from bridging vein rupture, more common in elderly or anticoagulated patients or those with brain atrophy (more vein stretch/vulnerability).
  • Acute blood is hyperdense on CT; a subacute SDH becomes isodense to brain parenchyma over roughly 1–3 weeks and is easily missed if not specifically sought (subtle signs: sulcal effacement, midline shift without an obviously visible hyperdense collection, ventricular asymmetry); chronic SDH becomes hypodense.
  • Surgical evacuation thresholds depend on size/midline shift, GCS trajectory, and local neurosurgical guidance (e.g. Brain Trauma Foundation-informed criteria) — small, asymptomatic collections may be managed conservatively with serial imaging.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct shape distinction and mechanism (suture-limited vs falx-limited) (4), correct vessel/mechanism for each (2), correct subacute-isodense pitfall (2), sensible surgical threshold reasoning (2).

Common error / drift trap: Subacute SDH can become isodense to brain (roughly 1–3 weeks post-injury) and is easily missed if not specifically looked for — a "normal-looking" scan in a patient with a compatible history and appropriate timing should prompt closer scrutiny or MRI if suspicion remains.

References

  1. Carney N, Totten AM, O'Reilly C, et al. Guidelines for the Management of Severe Traumatic Brain Injury, 4th Edition. Neurosurgery. 2017;80(1):6–15.

CBS_IMG_08 — Pneumoperitoneum on Erect Chest Radiograph

Imaging GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 70-year-old man presents with sudden severe abdominal pain and peritonism. An erect chest radiograph is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic finding for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "pneumoperitoneum erect chest x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name it.
  2. What alternative or adjunct radiographic signs exist if an erect film is not possible?
  3. Is free air always due to perforation? What must you consider?
  4. What is your immediate management?
Reveal model answer
  • A crescent of lucent free gas beneath the right hemidiaphragm, above the liver — pneumoperitoneum, indicating a perforated viscus until proven otherwise.
  • If an erect film cannot be obtained: a lateral decubitus film (air rises to the non-dependent side), Rigler's sign on a supine film (air outlining both sides of the bowel wall), or the "football sign" (large-volume free air outlining the entire peritoneal cavity).
  • Free air is not always due to perforation — recent abdominal surgery (open or laparoscopic) can produce visible free air for up to 7–10 days postoperatively as a normal finding. Clinical correlation (new peritonism, timing relative to any recent surgery) is essential.
  • In a clinically clear picture of perforation with peritonism, this is a surgical emergency — proceed to urgent laparotomy after appropriate resuscitation, without delaying for further imaging.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/naming of the finding (3), alternative signs named (2), correct recognition that recent surgery is a normal-finding pitfall (3), correct urgent management (2).

Common error / drift trap: Free intraperitoneal air is not always due to visceral perforation — recent abdominal surgery can produce visible free air for up to 7–10 days postoperatively as a normal finding; clinical correlation is essential before assuming perforation in a post-surgical patient.

References

  1. Chen SC, Yen ZS, Wang HP, et al. Pneumoperitoneum following laparoscopic surgery: incidence, resolution, and clinical implications. Am J Surg and general surgical radiology reference texts.

CBS_IMG_09 — Gas in Soft Tissue on CT: Necrotising Fasciitis

Imaging Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old diabetic man has rapidly progressive leg pain and swelling. CT is obtained for diagnostic clarification while theatre is arranged.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "necrotising fasciitis CT gas" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings shown.
  2. What is the value — and the danger — of obtaining this scan in this clinical scenario?
  3. How does this differ from simple cellulitis or a localised abscess?
  4. Given this scan, what is your immediate next step?
Reveal model answer
  • Small gas locules tracking along the deep fascial planes between muscle compartments, with fascial thickening and surrounding fat stranding — findings consistent with necrotising fasciitis.
  • Value: imaging can support the diagnosis and assist surgical planning (extent of involvement). Danger: it must never delay surgical exploration/debridement when clinical suspicion is already high — the same principle established for LRINEC in CCS_L05 applies equally to imaging.
  • Simple cellulitis shows no gas and no fascial-plane tracking, remaining more superficial; a localised abscess shows a discrete fluid collection without gas tracking along fascial planes.
  • Given a clinically convincing presentation, proceed directly to urgent surgical exploration/debridement — imaging findings here support but do not replace the urgency of the clinical decision already made.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct description of gas/fascial findings (4), correct value-vs-danger balance regarding imaging (3), correct differentiation from cellulitis/abscess (2), correct urgent next step (1).

Common error / drift trap: Ordering CT to "confirm" necrotising fasciitis before surgery in a clinically obvious case is a dangerous delay — imaging should support operative planning in equivocal cases, never substitute for or delay urgent surgical exploration when the clinical picture is convincing.

References

  1. Stevens DL, Bryant AE. Necrotizing Soft-Tissue Infections. N Engl J Med. 2017;377:2253–2265.

CBS_IMG_10 — Traumatic Aortic Injury: Widened Mediastinum

Imaging Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 30-year-old man sustains a high-speed deceleration motor vehicle collision. A supine trauma chest radiograph is obtained.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "traumatic aortic injury widened mediastinum" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. List the signs shown and their significance.
  2. How sensitive/specific is this film for the underlying injury?
  3. What is the typical mechanism and anatomical site of injury?
  4. Outline your management approach, including blood pressure control.
Reveal model answer
  • Signs of possible traumatic aortic injury: widened mediastinum (>8 cm on a supine AP film), loss of the normal aortic knob contour, a left apical pleural cap, and tracheal deviation to the right — all raise concern for great vessel injury.
  • This chest radiograph is a screening tool only — insensitive and non-specific (many false positives from rotation/technique, and it can miss injury). CT aortography is the definitive diagnostic test and should follow whenever suspicion exists, regardless of the plain film appearance.
  • Mechanism: rapid deceleration. Injury typically occurs at the aortic isthmus, just distal to the left subclavian artery origin, where the relatively mobile aortic arch meets the fixed descending aorta tethered by the ligamentum arteriosum.
  • Management: grade of injury determines urgency — free rupture is immediately life-threatening, while contained/minor injury may allow permissive hypotension and delayed repair if other injuries take immediate priority. TEVAR is now generally preferred over open repair. Regardless of repair timing, blood pressure/heart rate control (e.g. beta-blockade) to reduce aortic wall stress is important while awaiting definitive management.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct signs and significance (3), correct sensitivity/specificity limitation and need for CT aortography regardless of CXR appearance (3), correct mechanism/anatomical site (2), correct management including BP/HR control (2).

Common error / drift trap: A normal-appearing supine trauma chest X-ray is sometimes taken as reassurance against traumatic aortic injury. CXR is only a screening tool with limited sensitivity/specificity for this injury — CT aortography is required for definitive exclusion whenever the mechanism is compatible, regardless of CXR appearance.

References

  1. Fox N, Schwartz D, Salazar JH, et al. Evaluation and management of blunt traumatic aortic injury: a practice management guideline from the Eastern Association for the Surgery of Trauma. J Trauma Acute Care Surg. 2015;78(1):136–146.

CBS_IMG_11 — Placental Abruption: Retroplacental Haematoma on Ultrasound

Imaging Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A woman at 34 weeks' gestation develops sudden severe abdominal pain and vaginal bleeding, with a tense, "woody hard" uterus and an abnormal CTG. Ultrasound is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described ultrasound findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "placental abruption ultrasound" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding shown and name it.
  2. How sensitive is ultrasound for this diagnosis, and what does this mean practically?
  3. What major complication must you actively consider, even with modest visible bleeding?
  4. Outline your management given fetal compromise.
Reveal model answer
  • A hypoechoic/heterogeneous collection between the placenta and uterine wall — a retroplacental haematoma, consistent with placental abruption.
  • Ultrasound has limited sensitivity for abruption — it can be normal in a significant abruption, particularly if acute (fresh blood can be isoechoic to placenta). Placental abruption is primarily a clinical diagnosis (pain, bleeding, uterine tenderness/tone, fetal distress); a normal scan should never delay delivery when the clinical picture is convincing.
  • Concealed haemorrhage — significant blood loss can occur behind the placenta with minimal visible vaginal bleeding; also consider consumptive coagulopathy/DIC, a recognised major complication of abruption.
  • With fetal compromise, proceed to urgent delivery (usually emergency caesarean section), correct coagulopathy, and have the major obstetric haemorrhage protocol ready (see CCS_L09).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/naming (3), correct sensitivity limitation and clinical-diagnosis emphasis (3), concealed haemorrhage/DIC awareness (2), correct urgent management (2).

Common error / drift trap: A normal or equivocal ultrasound is sometimes taken as reassurance against abruption. Ultrasound has limited sensitivity for this diagnosis — a normal scan should not delay delivery in a patient with a compatible clinical picture and fetal compromise.

References

  1. Royal College of Obstetricians and Gynaecologists. Green-top Guideline No. 63: Antepartum Haemorrhage.

CBS_IMG_12 — HELLP Syndrome: Hepatic Subcapsular Haematoma

Imaging Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A woman at 32 weeks' gestation with known pre-eclampsia develops sudden severe right upper quadrant pain and hypotension. CT/ultrasound of the liver is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described imaging findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "hepatic subcapsular haematoma HELLP" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and its clinical significance.
  2. What is the major risk, and what signs would suggest it is occurring?
  3. Outline your management if she is stable and unruptured versus ruptured/unstable.
  4. What specific bedside caution applies to this patient?
Reveal model answer
  • A crescentic fluid collection beneath the liver capsule — a subcapsular haematoma, a rare but life-threatening complication of HELLP syndrome (see CCS_S27/BIO_11 for the broader HELLP picture).
  • The major risk is rupture, causing catastrophic haemoperitoneum — suggested by worsening pain, hypotension, and free intraperitoneal fluid.
  • Stable, unruptured: conservative management with close monitoring and urgent delivery planning. Ruptured/unstable: emergency laparotomy or embolisation with massive transfusion protocol readiness.
  • Specific caution: avoid abdominal palpation or unnecessary invasive procedures (e.g. liver biopsy) — mechanical pressure/trauma to the liver can precipitate catastrophic rupture.

Common error / drift trap: Abdominal palpation or unnecessary invasive procedures in a patient with a suspected hepatic subcapsular haematoma can precipitate catastrophic rupture — gentle handling and avoidance of unnecessary abdominal pressure is an easily overlooked safety point.

References

  1. Sibai BM. Diagnosis, controversies, and management of the syndrome of hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelet count (HELLP). Obstet Gynecol. 2004;103(5 Pt 1):981–991.

CBS_IMG_13 — Splenic Laceration Grading on CT Abdomen

Imaging Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 25-year-old man sustains blunt abdominal trauma in a road traffic collision. He is haemodynamically stable. CT abdomen with contrast is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "splenic laceration CT grading" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What grading system would you apply, and what features does it consider?
  2. Given he is stable, what is your management approach?
  3. Does the finding of contrast blush change your approach even though he is stable?
  4. What would change if he were haemodynamically unstable?
Reveal model answer
  • The AAST (American Association for the Surgery of Trauma) organ injury scale grades splenic injury (I–V) based on laceration depth, subcapsular haematoma size, vascular involvement, and degree of devascularisation.
  • In a haemodynamically stable patient, non-operative management (NOM) is now standard for most grades, with close monitoring and interval imaging as needed.
  • Yes — a contrast blush (active extravasation) on CT, even in a stable patient, should prompt consideration of angioembolisation to reduce the risk of NOM failure, rather than simply continuing observation alone.
  • If haemodynamically unstable: proceed directly to emergency laparotomy/ splenectomy without delay for further imaging or an embolisation attempt.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct grading system named with key features (3), correct NOM-as-standard answer for stable patients (3), correct angioembolisation consideration for contrast blush (2), correct unstable- patient pathway (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming all splenic injuries require surgery is outdated — non-operative management is now standard for haemodynamically stable patients regardless of grade, with angioembolisation as an important adjunct for active extravasation; an unstable patient, however, should proceed directly to surgery without delay.

References

  1. Coccolini F, Montori G, Catena F, et al. Splenic trauma: WSES classification and guidelines for adult and pediatric patients. World J Emerg Surg. 2017;12:40.

CBS_IMG_14 — Superior Vena Cava Obstruction on CT Chest

Imaging Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old man with known lung cancer develops facial and neck swelling with distended neck veins. CT chest with contrast is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "superior vena cava obstruction CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and correlate with the clinical syndrome.
  2. Is this always a treatment emergency requiring immediate radiotherapy? Explain.
  3. What treatment options exist, and how does tumour type influence the choice?
  4. What features would make this a genuine emergency?
Reveal model answer
  • A mediastinal mass narrows/occludes the SVC, with dilated chest wall collateral veins — correlating with SVC obstruction syndrome (facial/neck/upper limb swelling, distended neck veins, sometimes stridor or altered consciousness if severe).
  • No — in most stable patients, obtaining a tissue diagnosis before starting definitive treatment is preferred, since histology fundamentally changes management (e.g. chemo-sensitive lymphoma vs other tumours responding better to radiotherapy or stenting).
  • Options: stenting (rapid symptom relief), radiotherapy, chemotherapy (tumour-type dependent), and anticoagulation if thrombus is present (balanced against bleeding risk); steroids have limited evidence outside specific chemo-sensitive scenarios (e.g. lymphoma).
  • Genuine emergencies overriding the "get tissue first" approach: stridor/airway compromise or severe cerebral oedema/reduced consciousness — these mandate immediate treatment without waiting for biopsy.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct description/clinical correlation (3), correct "usually not an immediate emergency" answer (3), correct treatment options with tumour-type dependency (2), correct true-emergency red flags (2).

Common error / drift trap: SVC obstruction is sometimes treated as an absolute emergency requiring immediate radiotherapy before any diagnosis is obtained. In most stable patients, obtaining a tissue diagnosis first is preferred, since it changes management — true emergency treatment without biopsy is reserved for life-threatening airway compromise or severe cerebral oedema.

References

  1. Wilson LD, Detterbeck FC, Yahalom J. Superior vena cava syndrome with malignant causes. N Engl J Med. 2007;356(18):1862–1869.

CBS_IMG_15 — Pituitary Apoplexy on MRI

Imaging Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old man with a known pituitary macroadenoma develops sudden severe headache, worsening visual field loss, and ophthalmoplegia, then collapses with hypotension. MRI is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described MRI findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "pituitary apoplexy MRI" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and name the diagnosis.
  2. What is the most immediately life-threatening concern, distinct from the mass effect itself?
  3. What is your immediate management of that concern?
  4. When is urgent neurosurgical decompression indicated?
Reveal model answer
  • Haemorrhage into a pituitary macroadenoma, compressing the optic chiasm — pituitary apoplexy, correlating with the clinical triad of sudden headache, visual disturbance/ ophthalmoplegia, and potential acute hypopituitarism.
  • The most immediately life-threatening concern, easily overshadowed by the dramatic neuro- ophthalmic presentation, is acute secondary adrenal insufficiency from ACTH deficiency — this can be fatal if unrecognised.
  • Give empirical IV hydrocortisone urgently on clinical suspicion, without waiting for confirmatory cortisol results or imaging if the patient is unstable — the same principle as adrenal crisis management (see CCS_S26).
  • Urgent neurosurgical decompression is indicated for severe or progressive visual loss or reduced consciousness; milder cases with stable/improving vision can be managed conservatively with close monitoring.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis (2), correct identification of adrenal crisis risk (4), correct urgent hydrocortisone answer (2), correct decompression threshold (2).

Common error / drift trap: Focusing only on the neurosurgical/visual aspect of pituitary apoplexy while forgetting the acute adrenal insufficiency risk is a dangerous, recognised error — empirical hydrocortisone should be given urgently on clinical suspicion, because delayed steroid replacement can be fatal even if the mass effect itself is manageable.

References

  1. Fleseriu M, Bodach ME, Tumialan LM, et al. Congress of Neurological Surgeons Systematic Review and Evidence-Based Guideline on Pretreatment Endocrine Evaluation of Patients With Nonfunctioning Pituitary Adenomas (includes apoplexy guidance). Neurosurgery. 2016;79(4):E527–E529.

CBS_IMG_16 — Bilateral Hydronephrosis: Obstructive Uropathy

Imaging Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 75-year-old man with known prostatic enlargement presents anuric with a rising creatinine. Renal ultrasound is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described ultrasound findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "bilateral hydronephrosis ultrasound" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and the category of AKI they represent.
  2. What levels of obstruction should you consider, and which is most likely here?
  3. What is your immediate management priority?
  4. What must you anticipate and actively monitor for after relieving the obstruction?
Reveal model answer
  • Dilated renal pelvis and calyces bilaterally — hydronephrosis, consistent with post-renal (obstructive) AKI.
  • Consider obstruction at the level of the bladder outlet (BPH/prostate cancer — most likely given this patient's demographics and bilateral symmetry), ureters (stones, malignancy, retroperitoneal fibrosis), or urethra (stricture).
  • Priority is urgent decompression — a urethral or suprapubic catheter for bladder outlet obstruction, or nephrostomy for higher ureteric obstruction — relief of obstruction takes priority over extensive further work-up.
  • Anticipate post-obstructive diuresis — a large-volume diuresis can follow decompression, causing significant fluid and electrolyte loss (dehydration, hypokalaemia, hyponatraemia). This requires careful monitoring and matched fluid/electrolyte replacement, not fixed restriction.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/AKI category (3), correct level-of-obstruction reasoning (2), correct urgent decompression priority (3), correct post-obstructive diuresis monitoring point (2).

Common error / drift trap: After relieving obstruction, failing to anticipate post-obstructive diuresis can lead to under-replacement of large ongoing urinary losses and dehydration/ AKI recurrence — careful monitoring and matched replacement, not fixed restriction, is required.

References

  1. Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury. Kidney Int Suppl. 2012;2:1–138.

CBS_IMG_17 — Left Ventricular Free Wall Rupture on Echocardiography

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old woman, day 3 post anterior STEMI (delayed presentation, no reperfusion given), suddenly collapses in PEA arrest. Bedside echocardiography is performed during resuscitation.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described echocardiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "left ventricular free wall rupture echo" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and name the mechanical complication.
  2. What other mechanical complications of MI would you also consider, and how do they differ on echo?
  3. What is your immediate management?
  4. Why is bedside echo specifically important in this PEA arrest scenario?
Reveal model answer
  • A large pericardial effusion with diastolic RV collapse (tamponade physiology) and a visible discontinuity in the LV free wall — free wall rupture, a mechanical complication of MI classically occurring days 1–5 post-MI, especially with delayed/failed reperfusion, first anterior MI, elderly or hypertensive patients.
  • Other mechanical complications: VSD (flow across the septum on colour Doppler, new pansystolic murmur) and papillary muscle rupture (acute severe MR, flail leaflet) — all can present with sudden haemodynamic collapse days post-MI.
  • This is a surgical emergency. Pericardiocentesis may provide temporary stabilisation as a bridge, but definitive treatment is emergency surgical repair; mortality remains extremely high even with treatment.
  • In post-MI PEA arrest, a mechanical complication should be specifically and actively sought with bedside echo — standard ALS drugs and compressions alone will not address underlying tamponade physiology, and echo directly identifies a reversible cause.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis/timing (3), other mechanical complications named with echo distinction (3), correct emergency management (2), correct rationale for actively using bedside echo in PEA (2).

Common error / drift trap: PEA arrest post-MI is sometimes managed with standard ALS algorithms alone without considering a mechanical complication as the reversible cause — free wall rupture/tamponade should be specifically sought with bedside echo, since standard resuscitation alone will not address the underlying tamponade physiology.

References

  1. Figueras J, Alcalde O, Barrabés JA, et al. Changes in hospital mortality rates in 425 patients with acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction and cardiac rupture over a 30-year period. Circulation. 2008;118(25):2783–2789.

CBS_IMG_18 — Cerebral Venous Sinus Thrombosis on CT Venogram

Imaging Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 28-year-old postpartum woman, recently restarted on a combined oral contraceptive, presents with severe headache, papilloedema, and a focal seizure. CT venogram is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT venogram findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "cerebral venous sinus thrombosis empty delta sign" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name the sign.
  2. What risk factors does this patient have, and what else would you screen for?
  3. What is first-line treatment, and how does the presence of haemorrhagic venous infarction affect this?
  4. When would you escalate beyond anticoagulation?
Reveal model answer
  • A triangular filling defect within the superior sagittal sinus — the classic "empty delta" sign of cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (CVST).
  • This patient has two prothrombotic risk factors: the postpartum state and combined oral contraceptive use. Also screen for thrombophilia, dehydration, infection (e.g. mastoiditis), and malignancy.
  • First-line treatment is anticoagulation (typically therapeutic LMWH/heparin) — importantly, this remains indicated even in the presence of haemorrhagic venous infarction (common in CVST), unless the haemorrhage is massive/life-threatening, because the underlying thrombotic process — not the anticoagulant — drives ongoing venous infarction.
  • Escalate to endovascular thrombectomy/thrombolysis if the patient deteriorates despite adequate anticoagulation; also manage seizures and raised ICP as needed.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct sign identification (2), correct risk factor recognition and further screening (2), correct anticoagulation-despite-haemorrhage answer (4), correct escalation criteria (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Many clinicians withhold anticoagulation in CVST when haemorrhagic venous infarction is present, fearing worsening bleeding. Current evidence supports anticoagulation even with haemorrhagic transformation (unless massive/life-threatening) — withholding it in this setting is a recognised, harmful error.

References

  1. Ferro JM, Bousser MG, Canhão P, et al. European Stroke Organization guideline for the diagnosis and treatment of cerebral venous thrombosis. Eur J Neurol. 2017;24(10):1203–1213.

CBS_IMG_19 — Traumatic Diaphragmatic Rupture on Chest Radiograph

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 30-year-old man sustains a high-speed lateral-impact road traffic collision. Chest radiograph is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "traumatic diaphragmatic rupture chest x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and state the diagnosis.
  2. How sensitive is CXR for this injury, and what would you do next?
  3. What happens if this injury is missed?
  4. What specific bedside procedural hazard must you avoid here?
Reveal model answer
  • An elevated left hemidiaphragm with a visible bowel gas pattern and a nasogastric tube curling upward into the thoracic cavity ("coiled NG tube in chest" sign) — findings of traumatic diaphragmatic rupture with herniation of abdominal contents.
  • CXR is often subtly abnormal or missed initially (up to around half of cases on the first film) — CT with sagittal/coronal reconstructions is more sensitive, and a high index of suspicion is needed given the mechanism (severe blunt or penetrating trauma, especially lateral impact).
  • If missed, progressive herniation can lead to bowel strangulation/obstruction and respiratory compromise from mass effect, sometimes presenting late (months to years later) if not recognised acutely.
  • Critical hazard: never insert a chest drain blindly into an opacified hemithorax in a trauma patient without imaging correlation — if herniated bowel is mistaken for a haemothorax/ pneumothorax, blind drain insertion risks catastrophic bowel perforation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct description/diagnosis (3), correct sensitivity limitation and next step (CT) (3), correct late-complication awareness (2), correct chest-drain safety point (2).

Common error / drift trap: A chest drain inserted blindly for a presumed haemothorax/ pneumothorax in a trauma patient with an undiagnosed diaphragmatic rupture and herniated bowel risks catastrophic bowel perforation — imaging correlation before drain insertion is essential when the chest X-ray appearance is atypical.

References

  1. Petrone P, Asensio JA, Marini CP. Diaphragmatic injuries and post-traumatic diaphragmatic hernias. Curr Probl Surg. 2017;54(1):11–32.

CBS_IMG_20 — Blast Lung Injury on CT Chest

Imaging Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A survivor of an industrial explosion develops respiratory distress. CT chest is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "blast lung injury CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern and correlate it with the mechanism of injury.
  2. Classify the categories of blast injury this patient may have sustained.
  3. What specific ventilation caution applies here, distinct from typical ARDS?
  4. What associated injuries would you screen for?
Reveal model answer
  • Bilateral, central/perihilar, patchy consolidation and ground-glass opacity in a "butterfly/batwing" distribution — primary blast lung injury, from barotrauma caused by the blast overpressure wave.
  • Classify: primary (overpressure wave effect on gas-containing organs — lung, ear, bowel), secondary (penetrating injury from projectiles/debris), tertiary (blunt trauma from being thrown), quaternary (burns, inhalation, crush, psychological).
  • Caution: avoid excessive PEEP/high airway pressures where possible — blast lung carries a very high risk of pulmonary barotrauma and systemic (including cerebral/coronary) air embolism from alveolar-pulmonary venous fistulae created by the primary blast injury, a potentially fatal, under-recognised complication. Maintain a low threshold to suspect air embolism (sudden neurological or cardiac event) during positive pressure ventilation in this population.
  • Screen for tympanic membrane rupture (marker of blast exposure severity and predictor of blast lung), bowel perforation (primary blast effect on gas-containing bowel), and traumatic limb amputation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern/mechanism (3), correct blast injury classification (3), correct barotrauma/air embolism caution (3), associated injuries named (1).

Guideline-drift trap: Applying standard high-PEEP ARDS ventilation without caution in blast lung injury risks precipitating barotrauma and potentially fatal systemic air embolism — ventilation strategy should be more cautious regarding peak/plateau pressures than in typical ARDS.

References

  1. Sasser SM, Sattin RW, Hunt RC, Krohmer J. Blast Lung Injury. Prehosp Emerg Care. 2006;10(2):165–172.

CBS_IMG_21 — Central Venous Catheter Tip Position on Chest X-ray

Imaging General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·4·5·11 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A right internal jugular central line has just been inserted. A post-procedure chest radiograph is obtained.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent correct catheter position for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "central venous catheter tip position chest x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the correct tip position and how you identify it on this film.
  2. What malposition patterns would you specifically look for?
  3. What other complication must you actively exclude on the same film, and when might it appear late?
  4. Is a line that aspirates blood freely necessarily correctly placed in a vein?
Reveal model answer
  • Correct position: tip at the cavoatrial junction, roughly at the level of the carina or the lower SVC, with the catheter following a smooth expected course from the IJV into the SVC.
  • Malposition patterns: tip too high in the SVC (long-term vessel wall erosion risk), tip in the right atrium (arrhythmia/perforation/tamponade risk), or looping into the contralateral brachiocephalic vein, back up the IJV, or into the azygos vein — all require repositioning.
  • Actively exclude pneumothorax on the same film — this can be immediate or delayed up to 24 hours, so a repeat film may be needed if delayed symptoms develop; also check for haemothorax.
  • No — a line that aspirates blood freely is not necessarily venous; arterial cannulation can also aspirate freely. Confirmation (blood gas from the line showing venous values, ultrasound, or waveform) alongside imaging is needed, particularly before infusing vesicant/hypertonic solutions or high-dose vasopressors through a newly inserted line.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct position description (3), malposition patterns named (3), correct pneumothorax/ delayed-presentation point (2), correct arterial-cannulation caution (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming any line that "aspirates blood easily" is definitely correctly positioned in a vein is a recognised error — arterial cannulation can also aspirate freely, and confirmation is needed before infusing vesicant/hypertonic solutions or vasoactive drugs through a newly inserted line.

References

  1. Frykholm P, Pikwer A, Hammarskjöld F, et al. Clinical guidelines on central venous catheterisation. Acta Anaesthesiol Scand. 2014;58(5):508–524.

CBS_IMG_22 — Splenic Infarction in Sickle Cell Disease

Imaging Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 24-year-old man with known HbSS sickle cell disease has acute left upper quadrant pain during a vaso-occlusive crisis. CT abdomen is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "splenic infarction sickle cell CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and correlate it with the underlying pathophysiology.
  2. Distinguish this from acute splenic sequestration crisis, and comment on age-related likelihood.
  3. Outline your management if sequestration were to occur instead.
  4. What long-term functional implication does repeated splenic infarction carry?
Reveal model answer
  • A wedge-shaped, peripherally based, non-enhancing area within the spleen — splenic infarction, from sickling and vaso-occlusion in the low-oxygen, sluggish-flow splenic microvasculature.
  • Acute splenic sequestration crisis — rapid splenic enlargement with trapped blood, a precipitous Hb drop, and hypovolaemic shock risk — is more common in children before autosplenectomy occurs. By adulthood, the spleen in HbSS disease is often already small and infarcted ("autosplenectomised") from repeated crises, so simple infarction pain (as here) is the more likely picture in an adult, managed supportively rather than as a sequestration emergency.
  • If sequestration does occur: urgent, judicious transfusion (avoid over-transfusion, since sequestered splenic blood may auto-transfuse back once the crisis resolves, risking hyperviscosity), fluid resuscitation, and consideration of splenectomy if recurrent.
  • Repeated infarction leads to functional asplenia even without surgical splenectomy — increased risk of infection with encapsulated organisms, need for vaccination/ prophylactic penicillin, and a low threshold to treat fever as potential sepsis.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/mechanism (3), correct age-related sequestration-vs-infarction distinction (3), correct, cautious sequestration management (2), correct functional asplenia implication (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming splenic pathology in sickle cell disease always means an enlarged, sequestering spleen requiring urgent intervention is incorrect — in older children/ adults, the spleen is often already small and autosplenectomised, and the clinical concern shifts to functional asplenia and infection risk.

References

  1. Brousse V, Buffet P, Rees D. The spleen and sickle cell disease: the sick(led) spleen. Br J Haematol. 2014;166(2):165–176.

CBS_IMG_23 — Adrenal Haemorrhage (Waterhouse-Friderichsen Syndrome)

Imaging Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A young adult with fulminant meningococcal septicaemia and a purpuric rash remains in refractory shock despite fluids and escalating vasopressors. CT abdomen is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "Waterhouse-Friderichsen adrenal haemorrhage CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name the syndrome.
  2. Explain the pathophysiology linking this to the patient's refractory shock.
  3. Would you wait for this scan before treating? Justify your answer.
  4. Is this syndrome specific to meningococcal sepsis?
Reveal model answer
  • Bilateral adrenal enlargement with high-attenuation haemorrhagic content — Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome (bilateral adrenal haemorrhage complicating fulminant sepsis).
  • DIC and severe sepsis-related microvascular injury within the adrenal glands cause haemorrhagic destruction, precipitating acute adrenal insufficiency that compounds the septic shock — a "double hit" on haemodynamics that helps explain catecholamine-resistant shock.
  • No — suspect this in any patient with fulminant sepsis and refractory/vasopressor- resistant shock and give empirical IV hydrocortisone without waiting for imaging (see general principles in CCS_S01).
  • No — while classically described with meningococcaemia, bilateral adrenal haemorrhage causing acute adrenal insufficiency can occur with other causes of fulminant bacterial sepsis and severe DIC (e.g. pneumococcal, staphylococcal) — the underlying mechanism is not organism-specific.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis (2), correct pathophysiology linking to shock (3), correct "treat, don't wait" answer (3), correct non-meningococcal-specificity point (2).

Common error / drift trap: Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome is often taught as exclusively linked to meningococcal sepsis. The underlying mechanism is not organism-specific — it can occur with other causes of fulminant bacterial sepsis and severe DIC.

References

  1. Guarner J, Paddock CD, Bartlett J, Zaki SR. Adrenal Gland Hemorrhage in Patients with Fatal Bacterial Infections. Mod Pathol. 2008;21(9):1113–1120.

CBS_IMG_24 — Toxic Megacolon on Abdominal Radiograph

Imaging GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with a known severe ulcerative colitis flare develops abdominal distension, fever, and tachycardia. Abdominal radiograph is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "toxic megacolon abdominal x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and state what additional criterion makes this "toxic" rather than simple megacolon.
  2. What underlying causes would you consider?
  3. Outline your immediate management, including a specific prescribing caution.
  4. What would prompt urgent surgical referral?
Reveal model answer
  • Colonic dilation >6 cm (often affecting the transverse colon predominantly) with loss of haustral pattern. Toxic megacolon requires this radiological finding plus systemic toxicity (fever, tachycardia) — dilation alone without toxicity is simple megacolon.
  • Consider IBD flare (ulcerative colitis more than Crohn's), infectious colitis (especially C. difficile), and ischaemic colitis.
  • Immediate management: bowel rest, nasogastric decompression, IV fluid/electrolyte correction, and targeted therapy for the cause (IV corticosteroids for IBD, or targeted antimicrobial therapy for C. difficile). Avoid anti-motility agents, opioids, and anticholinergics — these can precipitate or worsen toxic megacolon.
  • Urgent surgical referral for perforation, uncontrolled bleeding, or failure to improve within 24–72 hours of maximal medical therapy — do not delay surgical involvement in a deteriorating patient given the high mortality risk with perforation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding and toxicity criterion (3), causes named (2), correct management including the prescribing caution (3), correct surgical threshold (2).

Common error / drift trap: Anti-motility agents, opioids, and anticholinergic drugs are sometimes given for symptomatic relief in colitis without recognising they can precipitate or worsen toxic megacolon — these should be avoided in any patient with severe colitis at risk of this condition.

References

  1. Sheth SG, LaMont JT. Toxic megacolon. Lancet. 1998;351(9101):509–513.

CBS_IMG_25 — COVID-19 Pneumonia on CT Chest

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An unvaccinated patient, day 8 of COVID-19 symptoms, has worsening hypoxaemia. CT chest is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "COVID-19 pneumonia CT ground glass" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the distribution and pattern shown.
  2. Is this pattern diagnostic of COVID-19 in isolation?
  3. How does the extent of involvement relate to prognosis and its typical time course?
  4. What else should this imaging prompt you to actively exclude?
Reveal model answer
  • Bilateral, peripheral, subpleural, lower-zone-predominant ground-glass opacities sparing the central lung — a typical/high-probability pattern for COVID-19 pneumonia per structured reporting systems.
  • No — this pattern is suggestive but not specific; other viral pneumonias, organising pneumonia, and eosinophilic pneumonia can look similar. Always correlate with PCR and clinical context.
  • Extent of parenchymal involvement correlates with severity/ICU admission risk; disease typically peaks in extent around day 10–12 from symptom onset before improving in survivors.
  • Actively exclude a co-existing or alternative diagnosis — secondary bacterial pneumonia (new focal consolidation) and pulmonary embolism (given the elevated VTE risk in severe COVID-19) — rather than attributing all deterioration to COVID-19 pneumonia alone.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern/distribution (3), correct "not diagnostic alone" answer (3), correct severity/time-course correlation (2), correct exclusion of secondary bacterial pneumonia/PE (2).

Common error / drift trap: A "typical COVID pattern" on CT is sometimes treated as diagnostic in isolation. Maintain a lower threshold to look for a co-existing or alternative diagnosis rather than attributing all findings/deterioration to COVID-19 pneumonia alone.

References

  1. Simpson S, Kay FU, Abbara S, et al. Radiological Society of North America Expert Consensus Statement on Reporting Chest CT Findings Related to COVID-19. Radiol Cardiothorac Imaging. 2020;2(2):e200152.

CBS_IMG_26 — Usual Interstitial Pneumonia Pattern on HRCT

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old man has progressive exertional dyspnoea, dry cough, and fine bibasal crackles. HRCT is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described HRCT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "usual interstitial pneumonia HRCT honeycombing" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern and name it.
  2. Does this pattern require a surgical lung biopsy for a confident diagnosis of IPF?
  3. What differentials would you consider, and how is a confident diagnosis reached?
  4. Why is this baseline imaging relevant to an ICU admission?
Reveal model answer
  • Subpleural, basal-predominant reticulation with honeycombing and traction bronchiectasis, and no features suggesting an alternative diagnosis — a typical usual interstitial pneumonia (UIP) pattern.
  • No — current guidelines support a confident clinical-radiological diagnosis of IPF based on a typical UIP pattern in the appropriate clinical context, avoiding the need for surgical lung biopsy, which carries real procedural risk in this often physiologically fragile population.
  • Differentials mimicking UIP: chronic hypersensitivity pneumonitis, connective tissue disease- associated ILD, asbestosis, drug toxicity — confident diagnosis is reached through multidisciplinary discussion integrating clinical, radiological, and (when needed) pathological data, not radiology in isolation.
  • This baseline is important context for interpreting acute deterioration on ICU — distinguishing acute exacerbation of IPF, superimposed infection, or an alternative acute process (see CCS_L15 for the ventilation/ECMO decision-making this informs).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern identification (3), correct "biopsy not required for typical UIP" answer (3), differentials and MDT process (2), correct ICU relevance (2).

Common error / drift trap: A "honeycombing" pattern is sometimes assumed to require surgical lung biopsy for definitive IPF diagnosis. Current guidelines support a confident clinical- radiological diagnosis based on a typical UIP pattern via multidisciplinary discussion, reserving biopsy for indeterminate or atypical patterns.

References

  1. Raghu G, Remy-Jardin M, Richeldi L, et al. Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (an Update) and Progressive Pulmonary Fibrosis in Adults: An Official ATS/ERS/JRS/ALAT Clinical Practice Guideline. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2022;205(9):e18–e47.

CBS_IMG_27 — Infective Endocarditis Vegetation on Transoesophageal Echocardiography

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with suspected infective endocarditis has an equivocal transthoracic echo. Transoesophageal echocardiography (TOE) is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described echocardiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "infective endocarditis vegetation TOE" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and its diagnostic significance.
  2. Why was TOE necessary given an equivocal TTE?
  3. What additional TOE findings would you specifically look for?
  4. Which specific complication is a strong independent indication for surgery?
Reveal model answer
  • A mobile, irregular echodensity attached to a valve leaflet with motion independent of the valve itself — a vegetation, satisfying a modified Duke major criterion.
  • TOE has significantly higher sensitivity than TTE, particularly for smaller vegetations, prosthetic valves, and posterior structures (e.g. mitral valve, aortic root abscess) — TTE alone has a significant false-negative rate.
  • Also look for: valve destruction/perforation, abscess formation, new valvular regurgitation, and prosthetic valve dehiscence.
  • Abscess formation is a strong independent indication for surgery given the risk of conduction abnormality/fistula formation (see CCS_L16 for the broader surgical timing discussion).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/significance (3), correct TOE-over-TTE rationale (3), additional findings named (2), correct abscess-as-surgical-indication point (2).

Common error / drift trap: A normal/equivocal TTE is sometimes taken as sufficient to exclude endocarditis in a patient with ongoing high clinical suspicion. TOE should be pursued when clinical suspicion remains high despite a negative/equivocal TTE, given TTE's significant false-negative rate.

References

  1. Delgado V, Ajmone Marsan N, de Waha S, et al. 2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of endocarditis. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(39):3948–4042.

CBS_IMG_28 — Severe Aortic Stenosis on Echocardiography

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·6 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 78-year-old woman with exertional breathlessness and syncope has an ejection systolic murmur. Echocardiography is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described echocardiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "severe aortic stenosis echocardiography" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and the quantitative thresholds defining severity.
  2. What important diagnostic pitfall exists in patients with reduced LV function?
  3. What are the perioperative/ICU haemodynamic management principles for severe AS?
  4. What treatment options exist depending on urgency and risk profile?
Reveal model answer
  • Severely restricted leaflet opening with heavy calcification. Standard severity thresholds: valve area <1.0 cm2, mean gradient >40 mmHg, peak velocity >4 m/s.
  • Pitfall: "low-flow, low-gradient" severe AS — in patients with reduced LV function/low stroke volume, gradients can be misleadingly low despite truly severe AS. This requires additional assessment (dobutamine stress echo, calcium scoring) to clarify true severity when gradient/ velocity and LV function are discordant.
  • Severe AS significantly increases risk with general anaesthesia/positive pressure ventilation/ vasodilation (fixed afterload, reduced coronary perfusion reserve): maintain preload, avoid excessive afterload reduction, and maintain sinus rhythm/avoid tachycardia (which reduces diastolic coronary filling time).
  • Options: surgical AVR, TAVI (depending on risk profile), or balloon valvuloplasty as a temporising bridge in an unstable patient not immediately fit for definitive intervention.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/thresholds (3), correct low-flow, low-gradient pitfall (3), correct haemodynamic principles (2), treatment options named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Relying solely on mean gradient/peak velocity to assess AS severity can be misleading in "low-flow, low-gradient" severe AS with reduced LV function — a seemingly modest gradient does not necessarily mean AS is not severe.

References

  1. Vahanian A, Beyersdorf F, Praz F, et al. 2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease. Eur Heart J. 2022;43(7):561–632.

CBS_IMG_29 — Cerebral Abscess: Ring-Enhancing Lesion

Imaging Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 40-year-old man with a recent dental infection develops fever, headache, and a new focal neurological deficit. Contrast MRI is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described MRI findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "cerebral abscess ring enhancing MRI" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and your differential for a ring-enhancing lesion.
  2. What specific MRI sequence best discriminates abscess from tumour, and why?
