  3. What sources/risk factors would you consider given this history?
  4. Outline your management, including a nuanced point about corticosteroids.
Reveal model answer
  • A well-defined ring-enhancing lesion with surrounding vasogenic oedema. Differential: cerebral abscess, tumour (typically thicker, irregular enhancement), toxoplasmosis (in HIV), resolving haematoma, and demyelination.
  • Diffusion-weighted imaging (DWI) is the key discriminator — an abscess typically shows restricted diffusion (high DWI signal) due to viscous pus, unlike most tumours, which generally do not restrict as strongly.
  • Sources: contiguous spread (sinusitis, otitis media, dental infection — as here), haematogenous spread (endocarditis, right-to-left cardiac shunts bypassing pulmonary filtration, pulmonary AVMs), or direct inoculation (trauma/neurosurgery).
  • Management: neurosurgical aspiration/drainage for diagnosis (culture) and treatment, especially if >2.5 cm or causing significant mass effect, plus prolonged targeted antimicrobial therapy. Corticosteroids should be used cautiously — reserved for significant mass effect/ oedema with herniation risk, since steroids can reduce antibiotic penetration and mask the clinical response to treatment.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/differential (3), correct DWI discriminator (3), sources/risk factors named (2), correct management including steroid caution (2).

Common error / drift trap: Diagnosing a ring-enhancing lesion purely on standard contrast appearance without considering DWI risks misclassification — restricted diffusion is a key, relatively specific discriminator favouring abscess over most neoplasms.

References

  1. Brouwer MC, Tunkel AR, McKhann GM, van de Beek D. Brain Abscess. N Engl J Med. 2014;371:447–456.

CBS_IMG_30 — Cavitating Lung Lesion: Differential Diagnosis

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man with chronic cough and weight loss has a thick-walled cavitary lesion in the right upper lobe on CT chest.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "cavitating lung lesion CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Give a structured differential for a cavitating lung lesion.
  2. Does wall thickness reliably distinguish malignant from infective causes?
  3. What ICU-relevant complications can arise from a cavitating lesion?
  4. Outline your immediate priorities if this patient presents with massive haemoptysis.
Reveal model answer
  • Infective: TB, bacterial (often anaerobic/aspiration-related) abscess, fungal (aspergilloma — "air crescent sign"), coccidioidomycosis. Malignant: squamous cell carcinoma (the most common cavitating primary lung cancer), metastases. Vasculitic: granulomatosis with polyangiitis (often multiple, bilateral).
  • No — while thick, irregular walls tend to favour malignancy and thin walls tend to favour infection/benign disease, this distinction is not reliable enough alone to guide management. Tissue diagnosis (bronchoscopy or CT-guided biopsy) is required whenever malignancy is a realistic possibility, regardless of wall thickness.
  • Haemoptysis (potentially massive if a cavitating malignancy erodes a vessel), secondary infection within the cavity, and pneumothorax if subpleural rupture occurs.
  • For massive haemoptysis: protect the airway (lateral decubitus positioning, bleeding side down), urgent CT angiography/bronchial artery embolisation, rigid bronchoscopy if available for large-volume bleeding, and avoid intubating/ventilating without an airway-protection strategy given the risk of blood flooding the contralateral lung.
Marking guide

10 marks: structured differential across categories (4), correct wall-thickness caveat (2), ICU complications named (2), correct massive haemoptysis priorities (2).

Common error / drift trap: Thin-walled cavities are sometimes assumed to always be benign/infective and thick-walled always malignant — this distinction is not reliable enough alone to guide management; tissue diagnosis is required regardless of wall thickness when malignancy is realistically possible.

References

  1. Gadkowski LB, Stout JE. Cavitary pulmonary disease. Clin Microbiol Rev. 2008;21(2):305–333.

CBS_IMG_31 — Bronchopleural Fistula on Chest Radiograph

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A ventilated patient with necrotising pneumonia has a persistent large air leak from the chest drain and subcutaneous emphysema. Chest radiograph is repeated.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described radiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "bronchopleural fistula chest x-ray" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and their significance.
  2. How does a large bronchopleural fistula (BPF) affect your ventilation strategy?
  3. Outline your management approach.
  4. What specific hazard applies to chest drain suction in this context?
Reveal model answer
  • Persistent pneumothorax despite drainage, with subcutaneous emphysema — findings of a bronchopleural fistula (BPF), an abnormal communication between the bronchial tree and pleural space.
  • Positive pressure ventilation worsens the air leak through the fistula — much of the delivered tidal volume can escape through the fistula rather than ventilating the lung, and risks tension physiology. Consider lower-pressure/lung-protective strategies, selective lung ventilation (double-lumen tube) or high-frequency oscillation in severe cases, and minimise PEEP where possible.
  • Management: adequate, correctly positioned chest drainage, bronchoscopic or surgical closure (endobronchial valves, glue, surgical repair) once stable, and treatment of the underlying infection.
  • Excessive suction on the chest drain can, in some cases, perpetuate the leak rather than promote healing — use the minimal effective suction required.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct findings/significance (3), correct ventilation strategy modification (4), management approach (2), suction caution (1).

Common error / drift trap: Applying standard high-PEEP lung-protective ventilation without modification in a patient with a large bronchopleural fistula can significantly worsen air leak and cause inadequate ventilation — strategy must be individualised, sometimes requiring lower pressures, selective lung ventilation, or high-frequency approaches.

References

  1. Lois M, Noppen M. Bronchopleural fistulas: an overview of the problem with special focus on endoscopic management. Chest. 2005;128(6):3955–3965.

CBS_IMG_32 — Constrictive Pericarditis: Pericardial Calcification on CT

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with elevated JVP, Kussmaul's sign, and peripheral oedema has CT chest showing pericardial calcification.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "constrictive pericarditis CT calcification" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and correlate it with the clinical syndrome.
  2. List causes of constrictive pericarditis.
  3. How do you differentiate this from restrictive cardiomyopathy?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Thick, calcified pericardium encasing the heart — consistent with constrictive pericarditis, correlating with the clinical picture of predominantly right heart diastolic failure.
  • Causes: TB (historically the most common cause worldwide), post-cardiac surgery, post-radiotherapy, idiopathic/viral, connective tissue disease.
  • A classic, difficult differential from restrictive cardiomyopathy: pericardial thickening/ calcification favours constriction; echocardiographic septal bounce and discordant respiratory variation in LV/RV filling favour constriction; myocardial strain imaging — preserved longitudinal strain favours constriction, reduced strain favours restrictive cardiomyopathy/infiltrative disease.
  • Management: pericardiectomy is definitive treatment for symptomatic chronic constriction; diuretics for symptom control while awaiting surgery or in milder cases; treat the underlying cause where identifiable (e.g. anti-TB therapy).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/correlation (2), causes named (2), correct differentiation from restrictive cardiomyopathy (4), correct management (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming pericardial calcification on imaging is diagnostic of constrictive pericarditis in isolation is a common error — calcification can be present without haemodynamically significant constriction, and constriction can occur without calcification; diagnosis requires correlation with haemodynamic/functional findings, not imaging appearance alone.

References

  1. Welch TD. Constrictive pericarditis: diagnosis, management and clinical outcomes. Heart. 2018;104(9):725–731.
  2. Adler Y, Charron P, Imazio M, et al. 2015 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of pericardial diseases. Eur Heart J. 2015;36(42):2921–2964.

CBS_IMG_33 — Spontaneous Coronary Artery Dissection on Angiography

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 38-year-old postpartum woman with no traditional cardiac risk factors presents with chest pain and a troponin rise. Coronary angiogram is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described angiographic findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "spontaneous coronary artery dissection angiogram" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name the diagnosis.
  2. What risk factors/associations does this patient have?
  3. How does management differ from typical atherosclerotic ACS?
  4. What longer-term issues would you address?
Reveal model answer
  • A long, smooth, hazy coronary narrowing without atherosclerotic plaque — spontaneous coronary artery dissection (SCAD), from spontaneous separation of the arterial wall layers creating a false lumen/intramural haematoma.
  • Predominantly affects women; associated with the pregnancy/postpartum period (as here), fibromuscular dysplasia, connective tissue disorders, and emotional/physical stress triggers.
  • Conservative management is generally preferred for stable SCAD rather than routine PCI/stenting — the dissection often heals spontaneously, and PCI carries a higher complication risk (propagating the dissection) in this specific pathology. PCI is reserved for ongoing ischaemia, haemodynamic instability, or high-risk anatomy.
  • High recurrence risk — screen for fibromuscular dysplasia in other vascular beds (renal, cerebral), provide psychological support given the young patient population and diagnostic rarity/uncertainty.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis (2), risk factors/associations (2), correct conservative-management answer with rationale (4), longer-term issues (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Managing SCAD identically to typical atherosclerotic STEMI/ NSTEMI (routine PCI/stenting, standard aggressive antiplatelet escalation) is a significant, dangerous error — conservative management is generally preferred given the risk of iatrogenic dissection propagation with PCI in this distinct pathology.

References

  1. Hayes SN, Kim ESH, Saw J, et al. Spontaneous Coronary Artery Dissection: Current State of the Science. Circulation. 2018;137(19):e523–e557.

CBS_IMG_34 — Diffuse Axonal Injury on MRI

Imaging Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe TBI has a relatively unremarkable initial CT but persistently low GCS disproportionate to the imaging. MRI with susceptibility-weighted imaging (SWI) is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described MRI findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "diffuse axonal injury MRI SWI" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the findings and name the diagnosis.
  2. Why can CT be relatively normal despite severe injury here?
  3. How does lesion location relate to prognosis?
  4. What are the management implications?
Reveal model answer
  • Multiple small, punctate haemorrhagic foci at the grey-white matter junction, corpus callosum, and brainstem — diffuse axonal injury (DAI), from rotational/shear forces during rapid acceleration-deceleration.
  • The primary pathology is microscopic axonal shearing injury, often below CT resolution; small haemorrhagic foci may be subtle or absent on CT. MRI — particularly SWI/GRE, and DWI in the acute phase — is far more sensitive.
  • Grading systems correlate lesion location with prognosis (grey-white junction only → additionally corpus callosum → additionally brainstem, roughly worsening in that order) — more central/deep lesions generally carry worse prognosis, though this correlation is imperfect and should be combined with clinical trajectory.
  • DAI has no specific surgical treatment (diffuse, not a mass lesion) — management is supportive neurocritical care (ICP/CPP optimisation, avoiding secondary injury, see CCS_L04); prognostication should be cautious and multimodal, avoiding premature pessimism from imaging alone.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct findings/diagnosis (3), correct CT-vs-MRI sensitivity explanation (3), grading/ prognostic correlation (2), correct management implications (2).

Common error / drift trap: A relatively unremarkable initial CT despite severe clinical impairment can lead to underestimation of injury severity if MRI is not pursued — DAI is frequently missed on CT because the primary pathology is often below CT resolution.

References

  1. Skandsen T, Kvistad KA, Solheim O, et al. Prevalence and impact of diffuse axonal injury in patients with moderate and severe head injury. J Neurosurg. 2010;113(3):556–563.

CBS_IMG_35 — Psoas Abscess on CT

Imaging Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old man with diabetes has back pain and fever. CT abdomen/pelvis is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "psoas abscess CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name the diagnosis.
  2. Distinguish primary from secondary causes, and how this changes your work-up.
  3. Outline your management priorities.
  4. What associated conditions would you specifically screen for?
Reveal model answer
  • A low-attenuation, rim-enhancing collection within the psoas muscle — a psoas abscess.
  • Primary (haematogenous spread, more common in immunocompromised/diabetic patients, often S. aureus) versus secondary (contiguous spread from adjacent structures — vertebral osteomyelitis/discitis, Crohn's disease, appendicitis, urinary tract infection — more likely polymicrobial). This distinction guides the search for a source.
  • Management: image-guided percutaneous drainage first-line where feasible, targeted antibiotics (empirical broad-spectrum initially), and surgical drainage if percutaneous drainage fails, the collection is loculated, or the underlying source needs addressing (e.g. spinal instability from osteomyelitis).
  • Screen for vertebral osteomyelitis/discitis (MRI spine if back pain is prominent), bacteraemia, and underlying immunocompromise/diabetes control.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis (2), correct primary/secondary distinction (3), correct source-control- based management (3), associated conditions named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Treating a psoas abscess with antibiotics alone without attempting source control (drainage) risks treatment failure, particularly for larger or loculated collections — drainage is a key component of management, analogous to other abscess source-control principles.

References

  1. Mallick IH, Thoufeeq MH, Rajendran TP. Iliopsoas abscesses. Postgrad Med J. 2004;80(946):459–462.

CBS_IMG_36 — Renal Vein Thrombosis in Nephrotic Syndrome

Imaging Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with membranous nephropathy and nephrotic-range proteinuria develops sudden flank pain and haematuria. CT venogram is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "renal vein thrombosis nephrotic syndrome CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and explain why nephrotic syndrome predisposes to this.
  2. What presentation can mask the true underlying diagnosis?
  3. Outline your management.
  4. What broader screening would you undertake?
Reveal model answer
  • A filling defect within the renal vein — renal vein thrombosis (RVT). Nephrotic syndrome is a major risk factor: urinary loss of antithrombin III and other anticoagulant proteins, hepatic overproduction of clotting factors in response to hypoalbuminaemia, and platelet hyperaggregability. Membranous nephropathy carries the highest RVT risk among nephrotic-range proteinuric conditions.
  • RVT can be asymptomatic or present with flank pain/haematuria — but can also present with pulmonary embolism as the initial feature, a recognised, sometimes overlooked scenario in nephrotic patients.
  • Therapeutic anticoagulation is standard, typically continued for as long as nephrotic syndrome/ hypoalbuminaemia persists given the ongoing prothrombotic state; some experts also support prophylactic anticoagulation in nephrotic patients with severe hypoalbuminaemia (<20 g/L, particularly membranous nephropathy) even without confirmed thrombosis.
  • Screen for concurrent PE, consider thrombophilia work-up if presentation is atypical, and treat the underlying nephrotic syndrome cause.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/mechanism (3), correct PE-as-presenting-feature point (3), correct anticoagulation approach (2), broader screening (2).

Common error / drift trap: A patient with nephrotic syndrome presenting with unexplained PE is sometimes worked up purely as a "provoked/unprovoked PE" without specifically considering renal vein thrombosis as the source — RVT should be specifically imaged for in this context.

References

  1. Kerlin BA, Ayoob R, Smoyer WE. Epidemiology and pathophysiology of nephrotic syndrome-associated thromboembolic disease. Clin J Am Soc Nephrol. 2012;7(3):513–520.
  2. Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO). 2021 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Glomerular Diseases. Kidney Int. 2021;100(4S):S1–S276.

CBS_IMG_37 — Ischaemic Bowel: Pneumatosis Intestinalis and Portal Venous Gas

Imaging GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An elderly patient with untreated atrial fibrillation has sudden severe abdominal pain out of proportion to examination findings, with a rising lactate. CT abdomen is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "pneumatosis intestinalis portal venous gas CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe both findings and distinguish portal venous gas from pneumobilia.
  2. What does this combination, in this clinical context, indicate?
  3. Outline your management.
  4. Is pneumatosis always a surgical emergency? Explain.
Reveal model answer
  • Gas within the bowel wall — pneumatosis intestinalis; branching lucencies extending to within 2 cm of the liver capsule — portal venous gas (as opposed to pneumobilia, which is more centrally located within the biliary tree).
  • Together, in this context — pain out of proportion to examination, atrial fibrillation as an embolic source, rising lactate — this strongly suggests transmural bowel ischaemia/infarction.
  • Urgent surgical review/laparotomy given signs of transmural ischaemia (this combination carries very high mortality and typically indicates non-viable bowel requiring resection), alongside resuscitation, broad-spectrum antibiotics, and correction of the underlying cause where possible.
  • No — isolated pneumatosis without portal venous gas or clinical severity can be a benign, incidental finding in some contexts (COPD, certain medications, connective tissue disease, or mild self-limiting ischaemia). The correct interpretation requires integrating the imaging with the clinical picture, not treating either in isolation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct findings/distinction from pneumobilia (3), correct clinical correlation (3), correct urgent management (2), correct "not always an emergency in isolation" nuance (2).

Common error / drift trap: Pneumatosis intestinalis is not always a surgical emergency in isolation — the correct answer requires integrating the imaging finding with the clinical picture (pain out of proportion, lactate, embolic risk factor) rather than treating either alone as diagnostic.

References

  1. Wiesner W, Mortelé KJ, Glickman JN, et al. Pneumatosis intestinalis and portomesenteric venous gas in intestinal ischemia. AJR Am J Roentgenol. 2001;177(6):1319–1323.
  2. Bala M, Kashuk J, Moore EE, et al. Acute mesenteric ischemia: guidelines of the World Society of Emergency Surgery. World J Emerg Surg. 2017;12:38.

CBS_IMG_38 — Diffuse Alveolar Haemorrhage on CT

Imaging Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A therapeutically anticoagulated patient develops a falling haemoglobin and rapidly worsening bilateral infiltrates. CT chest is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram constructed to represent the described CT findings for teaching purposes.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "diffuse alveolar haemorrhage CT" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and the clinical triad it correlates with.
  2. Does the absence of visible haemoptysis exclude this diagnosis?
  3. How would you confirm the diagnosis?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Diffuse, bilateral, central/perihilar ground-glass opacity/consolidation — can be radiologically indistinguishable from other causes of diffuse alveolar filling. Correlates with the clinical triad of haemoptysis, falling haemoglobin, and diffuse infiltrates — diffuse alveolar haemorrhage (DAH).
  • No — haemoptysis is absent in up to a third of cases at presentation (blood can be absorbed/redistributed within the alveolar space before appearing in the airway). The diagnosis should still be considered with falling haemoglobin plus diffuse infiltrates even without visible haemoptysis, particularly in a high-risk patient.
  • Confirm with bronchoscopy and sequential bronchoalveolar lavage — a progressively bloodier return on sequential aliquots is the key confirmatory finding.
  • Correct coagulopathy/reverse anticoagulation urgently, provide supportive respiratory care (often requiring lung-protective ventilation as for ARDS), and treat the underlying cause (immunosuppression/ pulse steroids ± cyclophosphamide/rituximab for vasculitic causes, plasma exchange for anti-GBM disease).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct finding/triad (3), correct "haemoptysis not required" answer (3), correct confirmatory test (2), correct management (2).

Common error / drift trap: The absence of visible haemoptysis is sometimes used to exclude diffuse alveolar haemorrhage. It is absent in up to a third of cases and should not be relied upon to rule out the diagnosis.

References

  1. Lara AR, Schwarz MI. Diffuse alveolar hemorrhage. Chest. 2010;137(5):1164–1171.

CBS_IMG_39 — Complicated Parapneumonic Effusion vs Empyema: When to Drain

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·3 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 58-year-old man, day 5 of community-acquired pneumonia, remains febrile on appropriate antibiotics. Chest ultrasound shows a large, septated, echogenic right pleural collection. Pleural fluid is aspirated: pH 7.05, glucose 1.8 mmol/L, LDH 1800 U/L, frank pus.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): thoracic ultrasound showing a septated, echogenic right pleural collection overlying consolidated lung.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "empyema ultrasound" (cases)
  • LITFL — search: "pleural effusion ultrasound"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Classify this pleural fluid using standard criteria. Does it meet criteria for tube drainage?
  2. What organisms most commonly cause empyema in this setting, and how does this affect antibiotic choice?
  3. If simple chest-tube drainage fails, what are the evidence-based next steps?
Reveal model answer
  • Classification: pH <7.2, glucose <2.2 mmol/L, LDH >1000 U/L, frank pus, or organisms on Gram stain/culture all define a complicated parapneumonic effusion/empyema requiring prompt tube drainage — this fluid meets multiple criteria (pH 7.05, glucose 1.8, pus).
  • Microbiology: Streptococcus milleri group, Streptococcus pneumoniae, Staphylococcus aureus (including MRSA), and anaerobes (aspiration-associated) predominate; empirical cover should include anaerobic activity (e.g., co-amoxiclav or piperacillin-tazobactam, or metronidazole added to a cephalosporin) pending cultures.
  • If drainage alone fails (persistent sepsis, loculated collection, inadequate output): intrapleural fibrinolytic plus DNase combination therapy (tPA + DNase, per the MIST2 trial — Rahman et al., NEJM 2011) improves drainage and reduces surgical referral/hospital stay versus either agent alone or placebo; if this fails, video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery (VATS) decortication is the next step, ideally before the empyema organizes (best outcomes when performed within ~2 weeks of presentation).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct classification with cited pH/glucose/LDH thresholds (3), correct organism/antibiotic reasoning (3), correct escalation pathway including named trial (4).

Guideline-drift trap: Intrapleural tPA alone (without DNase) showed no significant benefit over placebo in MIST2 and increased bleeding risk without added benefit; candidates who answer "give intrapleural tPA" without specifying the tPA+DNase combination are demonstrating an outdated or incomplete understanding of the evidence.

References

  1. Rahman NM, Maskell NA, West A, et al. Intrapleural use of tissue plasminogen activator and DNase in pleural infection. N Engl J Med. 2011;365(6):518–526.
  2. Chapman SJ, Davies RJO. British Thoracic Society guideline for pleural disease. Thorax. 2010;65(Suppl 2):ii1–ii76 (updated pleural infection recommendations 2023 consolidation).

CBS_IMG_40 — Post-Intubation Tracheal Stenosis

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 4·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 44-year-old woman, extubated 6 weeks ago after 3 weeks of mechanical ventilation for COVID-19 pneumonia, presents with progressive stridor and dyspnoea on exertion. CT neck with 3D airway reconstruction is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): CT airway reconstruction showing focal "hourglass" tracheal narrowing at the level of the former endotracheal tube cuff.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "tracheal stenosis post intubation" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What is the mechanism of this complication, and which cuff-management practices reduce its risk?
  2. What degree of luminal narrowing typically correlates with symptoms at rest vs on exertion?
  3. Outline the management options, from least to most invasive.
Reveal model answer
  • Mechanism: cuff overinflation causes tracheal mucosal ischaemia (capillary perfusion pressure is ~25–30 mmHg; cuff pressure should be kept at 20–30 cmH2O, i.e. below this threshold) leading to circumferential mucosal injury, chondritis, and later cicatricial fibrotic stenosis, typically presenting weeks after extubation once the lumen narrows below the symptomatic threshold.
  • Symptom correlation: narrowing to roughly 8mm diameter (or >50% cross-sectional area loss) begins to cause dyspnoea on exertion; <5mm typically causes stridor at rest — but "silent" compensation is common, so imaging/spirometry (flattened flow-volume loop) should be pursued for any exertional dyspnoea after prolonged intubation, not just stridor.
  • Management ladder: conservative/observation for very mild narrowing; endoscopic balloon dilatation ± laser/electrocautery scar release for short simple stenoses (may need repeated sessions); tracheal resection with end-to-end anastomosis is the definitive procedure for symptomatic, anatomically favourable stenoses; silicone or self-expanding metal stents are reserved for non-surgical candidates (metal stents avoided if resection is later planned, due to granulation/fibrosis complicating subsequent surgery).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct mechanism with cuff-pressure figures (3), correct symptom/lumen correlation (3), correct management ladder (4).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming stridor is required before considering this diagnosis. Compensated patients can present with exertional dyspnoea alone weeks to months after extubation; a normal chest X-ray does not exclude tracheal stenosis, which requires dedicated airway imaging or bronchoscopy.

References

  1. Sarper A, Ayten A, Eser I, et al. Tracheal stenosis after tracheostomy or intubation. Tex Heart Inst J. 2005;32(2):154–158.
  2. Wain JC. Postintubation tracheal stenosis. Semin Thorac Cardiovasc Surg. 2009;21(3):284–289.

CBS_IMG_41 — "Crazy-Paving" Pattern: Pulmonary Alveolar Proteinosis

Imaging Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 39-year-old former foundry worker presents with progressive exertional dyspnoea over 6 months and mild hypoxaemia. HRCT chest is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): HRCT showing geographic ground-glass opacity superimposed with interlobular septal thickening ("crazy-paving") pattern.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "pulmonary alveolar proteinosis" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. List three other conditions producing a "crazy-paving" pattern on HRCT, to illustrate why this sign is not diagnostic in isolation.
  2. What is the underlying pathophysiology of alveolar proteinosis, and what serum test supports the diagnosis?
  3. What is the definitive treatment for symptomatic disease?
Reveal model answer
  • Differential for "crazy-paving": Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, acute respiratory distress syndrome/diffuse alveolar damage, lipoid pneumonia, and mucinous adenocarcinoma can all produce the same radiological pattern — it is a nonspecific sign of combined airspace and interstitial disease and always needs clinical correlation.
  • Pathophysiology: autoimmune (in ~90% of adult cases) neutralising anti-GM-CSF antibodies impair alveolar macrophage clearance of surfactant, leading to accumulation of lipoproteinaceous material within alveoli; elevated serum anti-GM-CSF antibody titre supports the diagnosis, with confirmation typically via bronchoalveolar lavage (milky, PAS-positive fluid) or biopsy.
  • Definitive treatment: whole-lung lavage (sequential lavage of each lung under general anaesthesia with double-lumen tube) remains the standard for symptomatic patients; inhaled or subcutaneous GM-CSF therapy is an emerging adjunct/alternative with growing evidence (e.g., Tazawa et al., NEJM 2019, inhaled GM-CSF trial) but whole-lung lavage remains first-line for significant hypoxaemia.
Marking guide

10 marks: three valid differentials (3), correct pathophysiology and test (4), correct treatment (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating "crazy-paving" as pathognomonic for alveolar proteinosis. In an unwell, immunocompromised or critically ill patient the same pattern more often reflects PCP pneumonia or ARDS — the occupational history (foundry work, silica/dust exposure can cause secondary alveolar proteinosis) and chronicity here are what point toward the primary diagnosis.

References

  1. Trapnell BC, Nakata K, Bonella F, et al. Pulmonary alveolar proteinosis. Nat Rev Dis Primers. 2019;5:16.
  2. Tazawa R, Ueda T, Abe M, et al. Inhaled GM-CSF for pulmonary alveolar proteinosis. N Engl J Med. 2019;381(10):923–932.

CBS_IMG_42 — Takotsubo Cardiomyopathy: Apical Ballooning

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 67-year-old woman develops acute chest pain and pulmonary oedema hours after learning of her husband's sudden death. ECG shows anterior ST elevation; troponin is modestly elevated. Emergency coronary angiogram is normal. Left ventriculogram/echocardiogram is shown (systole).

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): apical ballooning with basal hyperkinesis, giving the classic "octopus pot" (tako-tsubo) silhouette in systole.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "takotsubo cardiomyopathy" (cases)
  • LITFL — search: "takotsubo"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What diagnostic criteria distinguish this from acute coronary syndrome?
  2. Is Takotsubo cardiomyopathy a benign, self-limiting condition? Justify your answer with outcome data.
  3. What two complications require specific early surveillance in the acute phase?
Reveal model answer
  • Diagnostic criteria (InterTAK/Mayo-based): transient regional wall-motion abnormality extending beyond a single epicardial coronary territory (classically apical ballooning with basal sparing), absence of obstructive coronary disease or acute plaque rupture explaining the wall-motion abnormality, new ECG changes (ST elevation, T-wave inversion, or QTc prolongation) or modest troponin rise, and absence of pheochromocytoma/myocarditis; a preceding emotional or physical stressor is common but not required for diagnosis.
  • Not benign: in-hospital mortality (~4–5%) and complication rates approach or equal those of acute coronary syndrome in registry data (InterTAK Registry, Templin et al., NEJM 2015); cardiogenic shock occurs in ~10%, and long-term mortality is also increased compared to age/sex-matched controls — describing it as "benign" or a "reassure and discharge" diagnosis is an exam-level error.
  • Early complications to surveil for: left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction (from basal hyperkinesis with systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve — worsened by inotropes, which are relatively contraindicated if LVOT obstruction is present) and acute mitral regurgitation; also monitor for LV apical thrombus (from stasis in the akinetic apex, warranting anticoagulation) and malignant arrhythmia from QTc prolongation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnostic criteria (3), correct "not benign" answer with cited mortality data (4), two correct complications (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Reflexively starting an inotrope (e.g., dobutamine) for Takotsubo-associated hypotension without first excluding LVOT obstruction on echocardiography. If LVOT obstruction is present, inotropes worsen the gradient and shock; management should shift toward cautious fluid, beta-blockade or phenylephrine (pure alpha-agonist), and mechanical support (e.g., IABP/Impella) if needed.

References

  1. Templin C, Ghadri JR, Diekmann J, et al. Clinical features and outcomes of Takotsubo (stress) cardiomyopathy. N Engl J Med. 2015;373(10):929–938.
  2. Ghadri JR, Wittstein IS, Prasad A, et al. International Expert Consensus Document on Takotsubo Syndrome. Eur Heart J. 2018;39(22):2032–2046.

CBS_IMG_43 — Acute Severe Aortic Regurgitation: Premature Mitral Valve Closure

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 52-year-old man with untreated infective endocarditis develops sudden pulmonary oedema and cardiogenic shock. Transthoracic echocardiogram (M-mode across the mitral valve) is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): M-mode echocardiography showing premature mitral valve closure (leaflets converge well before the QRS) due to rapid diastolic LV pressure rise from severe acute aortic regurgitation.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "premature mitral valve closure" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Explain the physiology behind this M-mode finding.
  2. Why is intra-aortic balloon counterpulsation contraindicated here?
  3. Outline definitive management and its timing.
Reveal model answer
  • Physiology: in acute severe AR the non-compliant, non-dilated LV cannot accommodate the large regurgitant volume; LV diastolic pressure rises very rapidly and can exceed left atrial pressure before end-diastole, causing the mitral valve to close prematurely — this is both a marker of haemodynamic severity and a protective mechanism limiting retrograde pressure transmission to the pulmonary circulation (though pulmonary oedema still occurs from the elevated LV/LA pressures achieved).
  • IABP contraindicated: counterpulsation inflates during diastole, which augments aortic diastolic pressure and would directly worsen the volume of regurgitant flow back into the LV — the same physiological reason IABP is contraindicated in any significant native or prosthetic aortic regurgitation.
  • Definitive management: this is a surgical emergency — emergency aortic (± mitral) valve surgery, source-control of the underlying endocarditis where feasible in the same operation, guided by the 2023 ESC endocarditis guideline criteria for urgent surgery (heart failure, uncontrolled infection, high embolic risk); medical therapy (vasodilators, inotropes) is only a bridge to theatre, and delay increases mortality.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct physiology (4), correct IABP contraindication reasoning (3), correct definitive management with urgency (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Delaying surgery to "complete a full course" of antibiotics before valve replacement in endocarditis-related acute severe AR with heart failure. The 2023 ESC endocarditis guideline (Delgado et al., Eur Heart J. 2023;44(39):3948–4042) recommends urgent/emergency surgery (within days, not after antibiotic completion) when heart failure, uncontrolled infection, or high embolic risk are present — waiting for a full antibiotic course is an outdated and dangerous practice in this scenario.

References

  1. Delgado V, Ajmone Marsan N, de Waha S, et al. 2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of endocarditis. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(39):3948–4042.
  2. Stout KK, Verrier ED. Acute valvular regurgitation. Circulation. 2009;119(25):3232–3241.

CBS_IMG_44 — Obstructive Prosthetic Mitral Valve Thrombosis

Imaging Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 61-year-old woman with a mechanical mitral valve, subtherapeutic INR for 2 weeks (missed clinic visits), presents with acute pulmonary oedema. Fluoroscopy of the prosthetic valve and transoesophageal echocardiogram are shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): fluoroscopy/TOE showing markedly restricted mechanical valve occluder disc motion due to thrombus.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "prosthetic valve thrombosis" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What are the two principal treatment options, and what factors determine the choice between them?
  2. What is the evidence-based first-line therapy for a left-sided obstructive mechanical valve thrombosis in a critically unstable patient versus a stable one?
  3. What bedside imaging test rapidly quantifies the severity of obstruction?
Reveal model answer
  • Two options: emergency surgery (valve rethrombectomy/replacement) versus fibrinolysis. Choice depends on thrombus burden/mobility, surgical risk, NYHA class, availability of surgery, and haemodynamic stability.
  • Evidence-based approach: per ESC valvular heart disease guidance, surgery is generally preferred for left-sided obstructive thrombosis with large/mobile thrombus or high surgical candidacy; fibrinolysis (e.g., low-dose slow-infusion tPA) is a reasonable alternative when surgery is high-risk/unavailable or for right-sided prosthetic thrombosis, and may also be used as a bridge in a critically unstable patient when surgery cannot be mobilised immediately, accepting a higher risk of systemic embolism and bleeding compared with a stable, elective surgical approach.
  • Bedside imaging: fluoroscopy (cine) rapidly quantifies occluder disc excursion and is often faster than TOE to obtain; TOE/TTE with continuous-wave Doppler gradient across the valve complements this by quantifying the haemodynamic severity of the obstruction.
Marking guide

10 marks: two correct options with correct discriminating factors (4), correct evidence-based approach (4), correct bedside imaging (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming fibrinolysis is always contraindicated because "it's a mechanical valve, high bleeding risk." Fibrinolysis is an established option, particularly for right-sided prosthetic thrombosis or when surgery is not immediately available — it is a risk-stratified decision, not an absolute contraindication.

References

  1. Vahanian A, Beyersdorf F, Praz F, et al. 2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease. Eur Heart J. 2022;43(7):561–632.
  2. Lengyel M, Horstkotte D, Völler H, Mistiaen WP. Recommendations for the management of prosthetic valve thrombosis. J Heart Valve Dis. 2005;14(5):567–575.

CBS_IMG_45 — Hypoxic-Ischaemic Brain Injury on DWI-MRI After Cardiac Arrest

Imaging Neurological CoBaTrICE 1·2, 4·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man remains comatose 5 days after out-of-hospital cardiac arrest, off sedation. Diffusion-weighted MRI is performed as part of multimodal neuroprognostication. The image is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): DWI-MRI showing bilateral restricted diffusion in the basal ganglia and diffuse cortical "ribboning," typical of global hypoxic-ischaemic injury.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "hypoxic ischaemic brain injury MRI" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Within the 2021 ERC-ESICM neuroprognostication algorithm, where does MRI fit, and what is its optimal timing?
  2. What critical timing pitfall can cause a false-negative (or falsely reassuring) DWI-MRI?
  3. What is the multimodal principle underpinning current neuroprognostication guidance, and why does it matter?
Reveal model answer
  • Placement/timing: the 2021 ERC-ESICM guideline recommends MRI with DWI/ADC mapping performed at 2–5 days post-arrest as one component of a multimodal assessment (alongside clinical exam off sedation, bilaterally absent pupillary/corneal reflexes, bilaterally absent N20 on SSEP, and highly malignant EEG patterns), used no earlier than 72 hours post-arrest (and ideally after confirming absence of confounders — sedation, hypothermia, neuromuscular blockade, metabolic derangement) to support a poor-prognosis decision.
  • Timing pitfall: DWI changes evolve over time and can "pseudonormalise" if imaging is performed too early (before restricted diffusion becomes apparent, typically most pronounced around day 2–5) or too late (as diffusion abnormalities can resolve toward isointensity after roughly 1–2 weeks) — imaging outside the optimal window can be falsely reassuring in either direction and must be interpreted with its timing explicitly in mind.
  • Multimodal principle: no single test is considered sufficiently sensitive and specific in isolation to guide withdrawal of life-sustaining therapy; current guidance requires at least two concordant poor-prognosis findings from different modalities (clinical, electrophysiological, biochemical/biomarker [e.g., neuron-specific enolase], and radiological) before a decision to withdraw is made, explicitly to minimise the risk of a self-fulfilling prophecy from over-reliance on any one test.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct algorithm placement/timing (4), correct pseudonormalisation pitfall (3), correct multimodal principle (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Using a single "malignant-looking" MRI at an unspecified or suboptimal time point in isolation to justify withdrawal of care. The 2021 ERC-ESICM guideline (Nolan et al., Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(4):369–421) is explicit that prognostication must be multimodal and delayed at least 72 hours, with confounders excluded — a single early or borderline-timed test result is never sufficient grounds alone.

References

  1. Nolan JP, Sandroni C, Böttiger BW, et al. European Resuscitation Council and European Society of Intensive Care Medicine Guidelines 2021: Post-resuscitation care. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(4):369–421.
  2. Wijdicks EF, Hijdra A, Young GB, et al. Practice parameter: prediction of outcome in comatose survivors after cardiopulmonary resuscitation. Neurology. 2006;67(2):203–210 (historical — largely superseded by 2021 multimodal ERC-ESICM approach).

CBS_IMG_46 — Spinal Epidural Abscess: An Incomplete Triad

Imaging Neurological CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 63-year-old man with diabetes and a recent skin infection presents with two days of worsening back pain and new bilateral leg weakness; he is afebrile. MRI spine with contrast is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): sagittal contrast-enhanced MRI showing a dorsal epidural rim-enhancing collection compressing the thecal sac.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "spinal epidural abscess MRI" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. The classic triad for this diagnosis is fever, back pain, and neurological deficit. What proportion of patients present with all three, and what is the exam-relevant implication?
  2. What is the time-critical management priority once this diagnosis is suspected?
  3. What is the most common causative organism, and what predisposing factors increase risk?
Reveal model answer
  • Triad prevalence: the complete classic triad (fever, back pain, neurological deficit) is present in only around 10–15% of patients at initial presentation; back pain is the most consistent feature (present in the large majority), while fever is absent in a substantial minority — as in this vignette. The exam-relevant implication is that the diagnosis must not be excluded on the absence of fever or a full triad; a high index of suspicion with risk factors (diabetes, IV drug use, recent bacteraemia/skin infection, spinal instrumentation) plus new back pain and any neurological change should trigger urgent MRI.
  • Time-critical priority: once neurological deficit is present, this is a neurosurgical emergency — urgent surgical decompression (laminectomy and drainage) plus targeted antibiotics is required, ideally within hours, as neurological outcome correlates strongly with the duration and severity of deficit before decompression; rapid deterioration to irreversible paralysis can occur within 24 hours.
  • Microbiology/risk factors: Staphylococcus aureus (including MRSA) is the most common organism (~two-thirds of cases); risk factors include diabetes mellitus, intravenous drug use, recent spinal surgery/epidural instrumentation, immunosuppression, and a contiguous or haematogenous source of infection (e.g., skin/soft-tissue infection, as in this vignette, or endocarditis).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct triad prevalence and implication (4), correct urgency/management (4), correct microbiology/risk factors (2).

Common error / drift trap: Excluding spinal epidural abscess because the patient is afebrile. Fever is absent in a meaningful proportion of confirmed cases; relying on the full classic triad delays diagnosis and is a well-documented cause of preventable permanent paralysis.

References

  1. Davis DP, Wold RM, Patel RJ, et al. The clinical presentation and impact of diagnostic delays on emergency department patients with spinal epidural abscess. J Emerg Med. 2004;26(3):285–291.
  2. Darouiche RO. Spinal epidural abscess. N Engl J Med. 2006;355(19):2012–2020.

CBS_IMG_47 — Necrotising Fasciitis / Gas Gangrene of the Diabetic Foot

Imaging Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 66-year-old man with poorly controlled diabetes presents with a rapidly spreading, exquisitely painful, discoloured right lower limb and septic shock. Plain radiograph of the foot/leg is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): plain radiograph showing soft-tissue gas tracking along fascial planes of the lower leg.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "necrotising fasciitis gas gangrene" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What is the single most important management step, and how urgently should it occur?
  2. What is the diagnostic role and limitation of the LRINEC score in this scenario?
  3. What antimicrobial regimen is recommended, and why is clindamycin (or linezolid) specifically included?
Reveal model answer
  • Most important step: emergency surgical debridement — imaging or laboratory scoring should never delay surgery when clinical suspicion is high (pain out of proportion, rapidly spreading erythema, skin necrosis/bullae, crepitus, systemic toxicity). Debridement should occur within hours of presentation; each hour of delay is associated with increased mortality.
  • LRINEC limitations: the Laboratory Risk Indicator for Necrotising Fasciitis score (using CRP, WCC, haemoglobin, sodium, creatinine, glucose) has a reported sensitivity of only roughly 70–80% in validation studies and should never be used to rule out the diagnosis in a clinically suspicious patient; a low score does not exclude necrotising fasciitis, and reliance on it for exclusion is a recognised cause of fatal diagnostic delay.
  • Antimicrobials: broad-spectrum empirical therapy (e.g., piperacillin-tazobactam or a carbapenem plus vancomycin/linezolid for MRSA cover) plus clindamycin or linezolid specifically for its antitoxin effect — these agents suppress bacterial exotoxin (e.g., streptococcal superantigen) production via inhibition of protein synthesis, independent of their antibacterial killing effect, which is particularly relevant in toxic shock associated with Group A Streptococcus.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct urgency of surgery (4), correct LRINEC limitation (3), correct antimicrobial reasoning including antitoxin rationale (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Waiting for CT/MRI confirmation or a LRINEC score before calling surgery in a clinically obvious case. Imaging (gas on plain film, as shown, or fascial fluid/oedema on CT/MRI) can support the diagnosis but a normal or equivocal LRINEC/imaging must never delay surgical exploration when clinical suspicion is high — surgical exploration is itself the diagnostic gold standard.

References

  1. Stevens DL, Bryant AE. Necrotizing soft-tissue infections. N Engl J Med. 2017;377(23):2253–2265.
  2. Wong CH, Khin LW, Heng KS, et al. The LRINEC score. Crit Care Med. 2004;32(7):1535–1541.

CBS_IMG_48 — Emphysematous Pyelonephritis: Gas Beyond Reflexive Nephrectomy

Imaging Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 58-year-old woman with poorly controlled diabetes presents with septic shock and left flank pain. Non-contrast CT abdomen is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): non-contrast CT showing mottled gas within the renal parenchyma, characteristic of emphysematous pyelonephritis.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "emphysematous pyelonephritis" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Historically, emergency nephrectomy was considered mandatory. What does contemporary evidence say about first-line management?
  2. What CT classification helps guide the decision between percutaneous drainage and surgery?
  3. What predisposing factor is present in the overwhelming majority of cases?
Reveal model answer
  • Contemporary management: percutaneous catheter drainage combined with broad spectrum antibiotics and aggressive glycaemic/metabolic control is now the preferred first-line approach for most cases, achieving comparable or superior survival to primary nephrectomy while preserving renal function; nephrectomy is reserved for patients who fail to improve with drainage and antibiotics, have extensive parenchymal destruction, or are too unstable for a staged approach.
  • CT classification: the Huang and Tseng CT-based classification (class 1: gas in collecting system only; class 2: gas in renal parenchyma without extrarenal extension; class 3A: gas extending to perinephric space; class 3B: gas extending to pararenal space; class 4: bilateral disease or solitary kidney) helps predict which patients are likely to respond to drainage alone versus require more aggressive intervention — higher classes and larger areas of parenchymal destruction on CT correlate with worse outcomes and higher likelihood of needing nephrectomy.
  • Predisposing factor: diabetes mellitus is present in the large majority (over 90%) of cases, related to impaired tissue perfusion/immune response and a hyperglycaemic environment favouring gas-forming organisms (most commonly E. coli, followed by Klebsiella pneumoniae).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct contemporary drainage-first approach (4), correct classification concept (3), correct predisposing factor (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Answering "emergency nephrectomy" as the reflexive first-line treatment. This reflects outdated teaching; modern series and reviews (Somani et al., J Urol. 2008;179(5):1844–1849, and subsequent literature) support a kidney-preserving, drainage-first strategy as first-line in most patients, with nephrectomy reserved for treatment failure.

References

  1. Somani BK, Nabi G, Thorpe P, et al. Is percutaneous drainage the new gold standard in the management of emphysematous pyelonephritis? J Urol. 2008;179(5):1844–1849.
  2. Huang JJ, Tseng CC. Emphysematous pyelonephritis: clinicoradiological classification, management, prognosis, and pathogenesis. Arch Intern Med. 2000;160(6):797–805.

CBS_IMG_49 — ADPKD With Acute Cyst Haemorrhage

Imaging Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 41-year-old woman with known autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD) and hypertension presents with acute severe right flank pain and a falling haemoglobin. Non-contrast CT abdomen is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): CT showing innumerable bilateral renal cysts (ADPKD) with one hyperdense cyst consistent with acute intracystic haemorrhage.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "ADPKD cyst haemorrhage" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What is the initial management of uncomplicated cyst haemorrhage, and when should intervention (embolisation/surgery) be considered?
  2. What extrarenal manifestation of ADPKD must be considered before any invasive procedure/intubation manoeuvre involving Valsalva or significant blood pressure swings?
  3. What disease-modifying therapy has evidence to slow cyst growth, and what is its key adverse effect requiring monitoring?
Reveal model answer
  • Initial management: most cyst haemorrhages are self-limiting and managed conservatively with analgesia, blood pressure control, bed rest, and correction of any coagulopathy; transfusion for significant blood loss. Angioembolisation or surgical intervention is reserved for haemodynamically significant bleeding that does not settle with conservative measures, or evidence of ongoing active extravasation on CT angiography.
  • Extrarenal manifestation: intracranial (berry) aneurysms occur in roughly 8–10% of ADPKD patients (higher with a family history of aneurysm/subarachnoid haemorrhage); uncontrolled hypertension or Valsalva-associated pressure swings carry a theoretical risk of aneurysm rupture, so blood pressure should be carefully controlled and screening (MR angiography) considered in high-risk patients (family history of SAH, prior aneurysm, high-risk occupation) before elective major physiological stress.
  • Disease-modifying therapy: tolvaptan, a vasopressin V2-receptor antagonist, has randomised trial evidence (TEMPO 3:4 and REPRISE trials) for slowing the rate of increase in total kidney volume and decline in eGFR in rapidly progressive ADPKD; its key adverse effect is dose-dependent idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity, requiring mandatory monthly liver function monitoring for the first 18 months of therapy, alongside aquaretic side effects (polyuria, thirst).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct conservative-first management (3), correct aneurysm screening consideration (4), correct tolvaptan evidence and monitoring requirement (3).

Common error / drift trap: Forgetting to screen for or consider intracranial aneurysm risk in an ADPKD patient before procedures involving significant haemodynamic swings. This is a frequently tested cross-system link in EDIC vivas — ADPKD is never "just a renal disease."

References

  1. Torres VE, Chapman AB, Devuyst O, et al. Tolvaptan in later-stage autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (REPRISE). N Engl J Med. 2017;377(20):1930–1942.
  2. Pirson Y. Extrarenal manifestations of autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease. Adv Chronic Kidney Dis. 2010;17(2):173–180.

CBS_IMG_50 — Acute Cholecystitis: Ultrasound and Timing of Cholecystectomy

Imaging Gastrointestinal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 70-year-old man on the surgical HDU has right upper quadrant pain, fever, and a positive sonographic Murphy's sign. Abdominal ultrasound is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram (not an actual patient scan): ultrasound showing a thickened gallbladder wall, pericholecystic fluid, and an impacted stone at the neck.

Real reference images (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "acute cholecystitis ultrasound" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. What ultrasound criteria support this diagnosis (per the Tokyo Guidelines)?
  2. What is the current evidence-based timing recommendation for cholecystectomy in acute cholecystitis?
  3. What is the alternative for a patient too unstable for surgery, and what are its risks?
Reveal model answer
  • Tokyo Guidelines (TG18/TG13) ultrasound criteria: gallbladder wall thickening (>4mm), pericholecystic fluid, distended gallbladder, and a sonographic Murphy's sign, combined with local signs of inflammation and systemic signs (fever, elevated CRP/WCC), support the diagnosis when both local and systemic criteria plus imaging findings are present.
  • Timing: current evidence (including the ACDC trial and subsequent guidance) supports early laparoscopic cholecystectomy, ideally within 72 hours (and up to a broader window of about 7–10 days is still considered acceptable in many protocols) of symptom onset for suitable surgical candidates, rather than the older practice of a 6-week "cooling-off" period with initial antibiotics alone followed by interval cholecystectomy — early surgery reduces hospital length of stay without increasing complication rates when performed by experienced surgeons.
  • Alternative for unstable patients: percutaneous cholecystostomy (image-guided drainage) is the standard bridge or definitive option for patients too unstable for surgery (e.g., severe sepsis/organ dysfunction, prohibitive surgical risk); risks include catheter dislodgement, bile leak, and the eventual need for interval cholecystectomy once the patient stabilises, as cholecystostomy alone does not address gallstone disease definitively.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct Tokyo Guidelines criteria (3), correct early-surgery timing (4), correct cholecystostomy alternative and risks (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Recommending routine "antibiotics and cool down for 6 weeks, then interval surgery" as the default answer. This reflects older teaching; contemporary evidence and the Tokyo Guidelines favour early cholecystectomy for fit patients, reserving delayed/interval surgery or cholecystostomy for those unfit for early operative intervention.

References

  1. Gutt CN, Encke J, Koninger J, et al. Acute cholecystitis: early versus delayed cholecystectomy, a multicenter randomized trial (ACDC study). Ann Surg. 2013;258(3):385–393.
  2. Yokoe M, Hata J, Takada T, et al. Tokyo Guidelines 2018: diagnostic criteria and severity grading of acute cholecystitis. J Hepatobiliary Pancreat Sci. 2018;25(1):41–54.

CBS_CRV_01 — Ventilator Waveforms in Severe Asthma: Dynamic Hyperinflation

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 26-year-old man is intubated for status asthmaticus refractory to nebulised bronchodilators and IV magnesium. Ten minutes after intubation, on volume-control ventilation (tidal volume 400 mL, respiratory rate 22/min, no extrinsic PEEP set), the bedside monitor shows the flow–time and pressure–time waveforms below, and his blood pressure has fallen from 118/70 to 78/45 mmHg.

Fig. 2 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "dynamic hyperinflation auto-PEEP"

Search link only (direct/topic-page links were dropped — less reliable and not verifiable from this session). Check the site's licence before reusing or redistributing content.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the abnormality shown in each trace.
  2. What is the underlying pathophysiology, and how does it explain the fall in blood pressure?
  3. What is your immediate action given the hypotension, before any ventilator adjustment?
  4. Outline your subsequent ventilator strategy to prevent recurrence.
Reveal model answer

1. Description of the abnormality

  • Flow–time trace: expiratory flow (below baseline) fails to return to zero before the next mandatory breath is delivered — the hallmark of incomplete exhalation/gas trapping.
  • Pressure–time trace: progressive breath-to-breath rise in both baseline (end-expiratory) pressure and peak inspiratory pressure — consistent with build-up of intrinsic PEEP (auto-PEEP) and dynamic hyperinflation, i.e. breath stacking.

2. Pathophysiology and link to hypotension

  • Severe airflow obstruction (bronchospasm, airway oedema, mucus plugging) markedly prolongs the time constant for exhalation; at a fixed rate and inspiratory time, there is insufficient time for full lung emptying before the next breath is delivered.
  • Progressive air trapping raises end-expiratory lung volume and intrathoracic pressure (auto-PEEP), which impedes venous return to the right heart and compresses the pulmonary vasculature, reducing RV preload and increasing RV afterload — this produces obstructive-physiology hypotension that can mimic (or coexist with) tension pneumothorax or cardiac tamponade.
  • Reduced venous return and increased intrathoracic pressure both reduce cardiac output, which is magnified immediately post-intubation by sedative-induced venodilation and loss of sympathetic drive.

3. Immediate action

  • Disconnect the patient from the ventilator circuit and allow full passive exhalation (manually decompress the chest if needed) — this is the single most important immediate manoeuvre and should be done before troubleshooting the ventilator settings.
  • Simultaneously perform a rapid clinical assessment to exclude tension pneumothorax as an alternative or additional cause of peri-intubation hypotension (asymmetric chest movement, absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation) — the two can coexist and both cause obstructive shock.
  • If the patient improves with disconnection, this confirms dynamic hyperinflation as the (or a) cause; give IV fluid bolus to support preload while the ventilator strategy is corrected.

4. Subsequent ventilator strategy

  • Adopt a strategy that maximises expiratory time: reduce respiratory rate, reduce minute ventilation target, increase inspiratory flow rate to shorten inspiratory time (accepting a higher peak but not plateau pressure), and consider a lower tidal volume.
  • Accept permissive hypercapnia (target pH >7.15–7.20 rather than normocapnia) as a deliberate strategy to avoid dynamic hyperinflation.
  • Set extrinsic PEEP cautiously and only after auto-PEEP is controlled — in spontaneously triggering patients with severe obstruction, a modest extrinsic PEEP (roughly 70–85% of measured auto-PEEP) can reduce triggering work, but in a fully controlled, paralysed patient without spontaneous effort, extrinsic PEEP does not help emptying and should generally be kept low or zero until obstruction eases.
  • Optimise bronchodilator therapy (continuous nebulised beta-agonist, anticholinergic, IV magnesium already given, consider IV beta-agonist or ketamine infusion for bronchodilation/sedation), treat any pneumothorax if found, and monitor with serial plateau pressure and measured auto-PEEP (end-expiratory hold) rather than relying on peak pressure alone.
Marking guide

Award up to 10 marks across: correct description of both traces (2), correct pathophysiological explanation linking auto-PEEP to obstructive hypotension (2), correct immediate action — ventilator disconnection before troubleshooting settings (2), exclusion of pneumothorax as a differential/co-existing cause (1), coherent subsequent ventilator strategy including permissive hypercapnia (2), safe and appropriately cautious approach to extrinsic PEEP in this context (1).

Common error / drift trap: A frequent error is reflexively increasing extrinsic PEEP to "treat" high pressures without first recognising dynamic hyperinflation and disconnecting the circuit — this can worsen hyperinflation and hypotension. Extrinsic PEEP is a tool to offset the trigger workload of auto-PEEP in a spontaneously breathing patient, not a first-line treatment for breath stacking in a controlled, paralysed patient.

References

  1. Tuxen DV, Lane S. The effects of ventilatory pattern on hyperinflation, airway pressures, and circulation in mechanical ventilation of patients with severe air-flow obstruction. Am Rev Respir Dis. 1987;136(4):872–879.
  2. Leatherman J. Mechanical ventilation for severe asthma. Chest. 2015;147(6):1671–1680.
  3. Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA). Global Strategy for Asthma Management and Prevention. Available at: ginasthma.org
  4. Kollef MH, Levy NT, Ahrens TS, et al. The use of continuous i.v. sedation is associated with prolongation of mechanical ventilation. Chest. 1998;114(2):541–548. (general context on sedation and ventilator interaction)

CBS_CRV_02 — Pulsus Paradoxus on an Arterial Line Trace: Cardiac Tamponade

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old woman with viral pericarditis progressing to cardiac tamponade is spontaneously breathing. Her arterial line trace is shown below across several respiratory cycles.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "pulsus paradoxus arterial line"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Quantify the abnormality shown and state the threshold that defines it.
  2. What is the pathophysiological mechanism in tamponade?
  3. What is your differential for this finding?
  4. How would you confirm the diagnosis and manage this specific patient?
Reveal model answer
  • The trace shows systolic pressure varying from ~130 mmHg on expiration to ~105 mmHg on inspiration — a 25 mmHg swing. Pulsus paradoxus is defined as an inspiratory fall in systolic blood pressure >10 mmHg; this is markedly abnormal.
  • In tamponade, pericardial fluid restricts total cardiac volume; inspiration increases venous return to the right heart, which (constrained by the fixed pericardial volume) bulges the interventricular septum leftward, reducing LV filling and stroke volume — producing the exaggerated inspiratory fall in systemic systolic pressure.
  • Differential: cardiac tamponade, severe asthma/COPD exacerbation (large intrathoracic pressure swings), tension pneumothorax, and (less pronounced) constrictive pericarditis.
  • Confirm with echocardiography (effusion with diastolic RA/RV collapse, IVC plethora). Given this is viral/medical pericarditis (not post-surgical), pericardiocentesis is the appropriate first-line treatment — in contrast to post-cardiac-surgery tamponade (CCS_S16), where surgical re-exploration is generally preferred.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct quantification and threshold (3), correct pathophysiology (3), differential named (2), correct diagnosis and management including surgical-vs-percutaneous distinction (2).

Common error / drift trap: Pulsus paradoxus is a quantitative sign, not simply "present/absent" — relying on peripheral pulse palpation alone is insensitive. An arterial line trace allows precise quantification and trending, which matters because the degree of paradox can guide urgency.

References

  1. Spodick DH. Acute cardiac tamponade. N Engl J Med. 2003;349(7):684–690.

CBS_CRV_03 — ECG Progression of Hyperkalaemia

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Four sequential single-lead ECG strips from a patient with worsening renal failure are shown, with potassium levels rising over several hours.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracings constructed to represent the described progression for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "hyperkalaemia ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify and describe the changes at each stage shown.
  2. Which stage represents the most immediately life-threatening finding, and why?
  3. Does the ECG appearance reliably predict the exact serum potassium level?
  4. What is your action on seeing the final strip?
Reveal model answer
  • Progression: tall, peaked T waves → P wave flattening with PR prolongation → QRS widening → a sine-wave pattern (QRS merging with the T wave) — a pre-terminal rhythm immediately preceding ventricular fibrillation or asystole.
  • The sine-wave pattern is the most life-threatening — it signals imminent cardiac arrest and demands immediate treatment, not further diagnostic delay.
  • ECG changes do not correlate precisely or linearly with the measured serum potassium — the rate of rise, coexisting calcium/pH, and individual variation all affect the threshold at which changes appear, so some patients with severe hyperkalaemia show relatively subtle ECG changes and vice versa.
  • On seeing a sine-wave pattern: treat as an emergency immediately — IV calcium gluconate/chloride for membrane stabilisation, followed by the full shift/remove sequence (insulin-dextrose, salbutamol, urgent dialysis) as detailed in CCS_S08, without waiting for a repeat potassium result.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification of each stage (5), correct identification of sine wave as most critical (2), correct statement that ECG does not reliably predict exact potassium level (2), correct immediate action (1).

Common error / drift trap: ECG changes do not always correlate precisely with the measured serum potassium level — a "normal-looking" ECG should not be used to falsely reassure against a genuinely high lab potassium value. Treat the number and the trend, not the ECG appearance alone in isolation.

References

  1. Renal Association (UK). Clinical Practice Guideline: Treatment of Acute Hyperkalaemia in Adults, 2020.

CBS_CRV_04 — Complete (Third-Degree) Heart Block

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 78-year-old man presents with syncope. His rhythm strip shows a ventricular rate of 32/min with a wide QRS escape rhythm.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described rhythm for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "complete heart block ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the key diagnostic feature of this rhythm.
  2. What does the wide QRS morphology tell you about the escape focus, and why does this matter?
  3. Would you give atropine? Justify your answer.
  4. Outline your management plan.
Reveal model answer
  • This is complete (third-degree) heart block: AV dissociation — P waves occur at their own regular rate, QRS complexes occur at their own regular (slower) rate, but there is no fixed relationship between them (P waves "march through" independently of the QRS).
  • A wide QRS escape rhythm indicates a lower, infranodal (ventricular) escape focus — less reliable and at higher risk of sudden asystole than a narrow-complex junctional escape rhythm arising higher in the conduction system.
  • Atropine is unlikely to help and should not be relied upon — it acts at the AV node, but infranodal block (as suggested by the wide QRS) does not respond reliably to vagolytic therapy.
  • Management: prepare for immediate transcutaneous pacing, with isoprenaline or adrenaline infusion as a bridge if needed, and arrange urgent transvenous pacing followed by permanent pacemaker assessment. Investigate the cause (inferior MI — usually more benign, AV-nodal, often reversible; anterior MI — infranodal, higher risk; drug effect; degenerative conduction disease; consider Lyme disease/infiltrative causes in a younger patient).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct AV dissociation description (4), correct wide-QRS/infranodal significance (2), correct, justified atropine scepticism (2), correct pacing-focused management plan (2).

Common error / drift trap: Atropine is often reflexively given for any bradycardia, including complete heart block. It is typically ineffective for infranodal (wide-complex) complete heart block, which requires pacing rather than relying on atropine to work.

References

  1. Resuscitation Council UK / European Resuscitation Council. Adult Bradycardia Algorithm.
  2. Glikson M, Nielsen JC, Kronborg MB, et al. 2021 ESC Guidelines on cardiac pacing and cardiac resynchronization therapy. Eur Heart J. 2021;42(35):3427–3520.

CBS_CRV_05 — Pressure-Volume Loop in ARDS: Recruitment and Overdistension

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A static pressure-volume loop is recorded during a slow-flow inflation in a patient with moderate ARDS, to help understand the mechanics being discussed at the bedside.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described mechanics for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "pressure volume loop ARDS"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the lower and upper inflection points and explain what each represents physiologically.
  2. How does this concept inform PEEP and tidal volume selection?
  3. Is routine static PV-loop-guided PEEP titration current standard bedside practice? Explain.
  4. What is the risk of ignoring the upper inflection point?
Reveal model answer
  • The lower inflection point marks the pressure below which alveolar units are collapsed and above which recruitment/improving compliance begins. The upper inflection point marks the pressure beyond which compliance worsens again due to overdistension of already-recruited alveoli.
  • This underpins lung-protective ventilation: set PEEP above the lower inflection point to avoid cyclic atelectasis/recruitment-derecruitment injury, and avoid tidal ventilation extending above the upper inflection point to avoid volutrauma from overdistension.
  • In everyday practice, routine static PV-loop-guided PEEP titration is not standard — it is labour-intensive and technically demanding; most units instead use simpler protocolised approaches (e.g. ARDSNet PEEP/FiO2 tables) or driving-pressure-guided titration. The underlying physiological concept, however, remains foundational and directly informs why excessive PEEP or tidal volume can be harmful.
  • Ignoring the upper inflection point and continuing to increase PEEP/volume risks overdistension injury (volutrauma/barotrauma) without further recruitment benefit — more support is not always better.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/explanation of both inflection points (5), correct link to PEEP/tidal volume selection (2), correct, honest statement about current practice (2), correct overdistension risk (1).

Common error / drift trap: Some assume higher PEEP is always better for recruitment. The PV loop specifically shows a ceiling (upper inflection point) beyond which further PEEP/volume causes overdistension and harm rather than benefit — PEEP titration must balance recruitment against overdistension, not simply be maximised.

References

  1. Amato MB, Barbas CS, Medeiros DM, et al. Beneficial effects of the "open lung approach" with low distending pressures in acute respiratory distress syndrome. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 1995;152(6 Pt 1):1835–1846.
  2. Fan E, Del Sorbo L, Goligher EC, et al. An Official ATS/ESICM/SCCM Clinical Practice Guideline: Mechanical Ventilation in Adult Patients with ARDS. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2017;195(9):1253–1263.

CBS_CRV_06 — Capnography Waveform Patterns

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Four capnography traces are shown from different points in a resuscitation and airway management scenario.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "capnography waveform"
