Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify each of the four patterns.
  2. What immediate action does panel B demand?
  3. What does panel C tell you about CPR quality and prognosis?
  4. Why is continuous waveform capnography preferred over auscultation for confirming tube placement?
Reveal model answer
  • A: normal square-wave capnograph. B: absent/flat trace — oesophageal intubation (no CO2 being exhaled through the airway device). C: low-amplitude but present waveform during CPR, reflecting reduced but ongoing pulmonary blood flow from chest compressions. D: a sloped, "shark-fin" upstroke — bronchospasm/airway obstruction (e.g. severe asthma/COPD), reflecting uneven emptying of lung units with different time constants.
  • Panel B demands immediate recognition of oesophageal intubation and reintubation — this is a critical, time-sensitive airway emergency.
  • In panel C, end-tidal CO2 reflects pulmonary blood flow generated by compressions and can guide compression quality (aim to maximise EtCO2); a persistently very low EtCO2 despite good-quality CPR is associated with a poor likelihood of return of spontaneous circulation, while a sudden rise in EtCO2 during CPR can be an early sign of ROSC.
  • Continuous waveform capnography is the gold-standard method for confirming correct tracheal tube placement — auscultation alone can be misleading (e.g. transmitted breath sounds can be heard even with oesophageal intubation), whereas a persistent, physiological capnograph trace confirms ventilation of the lungs directly.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification of all four patterns (4), correct immediate action for panel B (2), correct CPR-quality/prognostic interpretation of panel C (2), correct rationale for capnography over auscultation (2).

Common error / drift trap: Auscultation alone is sometimes still relied upon to "confirm" correct tube placement immediately after intubation. Continuous waveform capnography is the gold-standard confirmation method and should be the primary, definitive check, not auscultation, which can be misleading.

References

  1. Soar J, Böttiger BW, Carli P, et al. European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2021: Adult advanced life support. Resuscitation. 2021;161:115–151.

CBS_CRV_07 — ICP Waveform: Lundberg A (Plateau) Waves

Curves / Waveforms Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe TBI and an intraparenchymal ICP monitor shows intermittent episodes on the trended ICP trace, shown below.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "Lundberg A waves ICP"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify and describe this waveform pattern.
  2. What is its pathophysiological significance?
  3. How does it differ from Lundberg B and C waves?
  4. What is your immediate response to seeing this on the monitor?
Reveal model answer
  • This is a Lundberg A (plateau) wave: a sudden, sustained rise in ICP to 50–100 mmHg, lasting roughly 5–20 minutes, followed by an abrupt fall back toward baseline.
  • Significance: plateau waves reflect a critical reduction in cerebral compliance and a cascading cerebral vasodilatory response — they are an ominous sign of impending decompensation, associated with transient but significant falls in cerebral perfusion pressure and neurological deterioration during the wave itself.
  • Lundberg B waves are shorter, more rhythmic, lower-amplitude oscillations related to vasomotor instability (a Cheyne-Stokes-like pattern), and are less severe. Lundberg C waves are rapid, low-amplitude oscillations related to normal blood pressure/respiratory fluctuation and are of little independent clinical significance.
  • Each plateau wave episode should be treated as an acute ICP crisis — urgently reassess and escalate ICP-lowering therapy per the tiered approach (see CCS_L04), actively look for and treat a precipitant, and consider whether the episode changes the threshold for decompressive surgery.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/description of plateau wave (4), correct pathophysiological significance (3), correct distinction from B/C waves (2), correct urgent response (1).

Common error / drift trap: Plateau (Lundberg A) waves are sometimes under-recognised as an isolated transient event not requiring escalation. They are a serious sign of failing cerebral compliance/autoregulation, and each episode should prompt urgent reassessment and escalation of ICP management, not be dismissed as a transient artefact.

References

  1. Lundberg N. Continuous recording and control of ventricular fluid pressure in neurosurgical practice. Acta Psychiatr Scand Suppl. 1960;36(149):1–193.
  2. Hawryluk GWJ, Aguilera S, Buki A, et al. SIBICC management algorithm for ICP monitoring. Intensive Care Med. 2019;45:1783–1794.

CBS_CRV_08 — CVP Waveform: Atrial Fibrillation and Severe Tricuspid Regurgitation

Curves / Waveforms Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Three central venous pressure waveforms are shown: a normal trace, and traces from two septic ICU patients with new haemodynamic instability.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "CVP waveform tricuspid regurgitation"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Label the components of the normal CVP trace.
  2. What is abnormal about the second trace, and why?
  3. What is abnormal about the third trace, and what does it suggest?
  4. What is the clinical value of examining CVP waveform morphology rather than just the mean number?
Reveal model answer
  • Normal components: a wave (atrial contraction), c wave (tricuspid valve bulging into the RA during isovolumic RV contraction), v wave (venous filling of the RA against a closed tricuspid valve), with an x descent (atrial relaxation) and y descent (tricuspid valve opening/rapid early ventricular filling).
  • The second trace shows loss of the discrete a wave with an irregular baseline — consistent with atrial fibrillation, where the lack of organised, coordinated atrial contraction abolishes the a wave.
  • The third trace shows a giant, fused "cv" wave replacing the normal x descent — the classic pattern of severe tricuspid regurgitation, with systolic regurgitant flow into the RA producing "ventricularisation" of the CVP trace.
  • Waveform morphology gives an immediate bedside diagnostic clue — prompting targeted echocardiography to confirm rhythm/valvular pathology — rather than treating CVP as a single numeric value alone, which discards this diagnostic information.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct labelling of normal components (4), correct AF pattern/mechanism (2), correct severe TR pattern/mechanism (2), correct statement of clinical value of waveform interpretation (2).

Common error / drift trap: The CVP waveform is often overlooked beyond the mean number. Specific patterns (absent a wave in AF, giant cv wave in severe TR) provide immediate diagnostic clues that should prompt targeted echo assessment, rather than treating CVP as a single numeric value alone.

References

  1. Magder S. Central venous pressure: A useful but not so simple measurement. Crit Care Med. 2006;34(8):2224–2227.

CBS_CRV_09 — ECG Changes of Hypokalaemia

Curves / Waveforms Renal / Metabolic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old woman on long-term diuretics with poor oral intake has potassium 2.6 mmol/L. Her ECG is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "hypokalaemia ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the changes shown.
  2. How do you distinguish a U wave from a notched/bifid T wave?
  3. What is the major arrhythmic risk, and what coexisting electrolyte abnormality increases it?
  4. What is a commonly missed step in correcting this abnormality?
Reveal model answer
  • Findings: flattened T waves, ST segment depression, and a prominent U wave — classic ECG changes of significant hypokalaemia.
  • U waves are best seen in leads V2–V3, appearing as a small deflection after the T wave (distinguishing them from a notched/bifid T wave, which is a single broader deflection without a distinct second component following a return toward baseline).
  • Major risk: ventricular arrhythmia, including torsades de pointes, particularly with QT/QU prolongation. Coexisting hypomagnesaemia substantially increases this risk and is commonly present alongside hypokalaemia (e.g. from diuretic use or poor intake).
  • Commonly missed step: checking and correcting magnesium alongside potassium — magnesium deficiency impairs renal potassium conservation and blunts the efficacy of potassium replacement, so refractory hypokalaemia despite replacement should prompt magnesium measurement and correction.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification of ECG changes (4), correct U-wave-vs-notched-T distinction (2), correct arrhythmia risk and magnesium link (2), correct emphasis on concurrent magnesium correction (2).

Common error / drift trap: Attempting to correct hypokalaemia without checking/ correcting coexisting hypomagnesaemia is a common error — magnesium deficiency impairs potassium replacement efficacy, so refractory hypokalaemia should prompt magnesium measurement and correction alongside potassium.

References

  1. Kardalas E, Paschou SA, Anagnostis P, et al. Hypokalemia: a clinical update. Endocr Connect. 2018;7(4):R135–R146.

CBS_CRV_10 — Ventilator Dyssynchrony: Double Triggering and Breath Stacking

Curves / Waveforms General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with ARDS and a high respiratory drive is ventilated in volume-control mode. The ventilator graphics below are noted during rounds.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "double triggering ventilator dyssynchrony"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the pattern shown and explain the underlying mechanism.
  2. What is the clinical risk if this is not addressed?
  3. Outline your management options, in order.
  4. What monitoring point is essential and easily overlooked here?
Reveal model answer
  • This is double triggering — the patient's neural inspiratory time exceeds the mechanically set inspiratory time, so a second breath is triggered before the first has been exhaled, resulting in breath stacking and a combined delivered tidal volume far exceeding the set target.
  • Risk: volutrauma/barotrauma from delivered volumes/pressures far exceeding the intended lung-protective targets, undermining the entire ventilation strategy despite an appropriately "set" tidal volume.
  • Management, in order: first address the mismatch directly — adjust ventilator settings to better match the patient's neural timing (e.g. increase inspiratory time/flow, reconsider trigger sensitivity, or change to a mode allowing more patient-determined inspiratory time such as pressure support, or neurally adjusted ventilatory assist in appropriate patients). Increasing sedation/ analgesia is often needed alongside this, but adjusting the ventilator match should not be skipped in favour of sedation alone. Reserve neuromuscular blockade for refractory cases, per the individualised, ROSE-trial-informed approach discussed in CCS_L01.
  • Essential, easily overlooked monitoring point: the displayed "set" tidal volume underrepresents the actual delivered tidal volume during double triggering — actively check delivered/measured tidal volume, not just the set value, to recognise this is happening at all.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification and mechanism (4), correct volutrauma risk (2), correctly-ordered management addressing the ventilator mismatch, not sedation alone (3), correct delivered-vs-set volume monitoring point (1).

Common error / drift trap: Simply increasing sedation is not always sufficient or the single correct answer — the underlying mismatch between patient neural inspiratory time and ventilator-set inspiratory time should be addressed by adjusting ventilator settings, since sedation alone may not resolve a fundamentally mismatched setting and unnecessarily deep sedation carries its own risks (delirium, prolonged ventilation).

References

  1. Pham T, Telias I, Piraino T, et al. Asynchrony Consensus Group. Recognition of inspiratory muscle effort during mechanical ventilation. Crit Care. 2022;26:31.
  2. National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute PETAL Clinical Trials Network. Early Neuromuscular Blockade in the Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ROSE). N Engl J Med. 2019;380:1997–2008.

CBS_CRV_11 — Flow-Volume Loop: Fixed Upper Airway Obstruction

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old woman with known post-intubation tracheal stenosis presents with stridor and exertional dyspnoea. Spirometry with a flow-volume loop is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "flow volume loop upper airway obstruction"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern and name it.
  2. How would a variable extrathoracic or variable intrathoracic obstruction differ on this loop?
  3. What further investigation would you arrange?
  4. What are the airway management implications if this patient needed emergency intubation?
Reveal model answer
  • Flattening of both the inspiratory and expiratory limbs, forming plateaus on each side — the classic pattern of fixed upper airway obstruction (e.g. tracheal stenosis, tracheal tumour).
  • Variable extrathoracic obstruction (e.g. vocal cord paralysis) flattens predominantly the inspiratory limb (worse on inspiration, when negative extrathoracic pressure narrows the airway further). Variable intrathoracic obstruction (e.g. intrathoracic tracheal tumour, tracheomalacia) flattens predominantly the expiratory limb (worse on expiration, when positive intrathoracic pressure compresses the airway).
  • Further investigation: CT neck/chest and direct laryngoscopy/bronchoscopy to visualise and characterise the stenosis.
  • Airway management implications: anticipate a difficult airway — may need a smaller endotracheal tube, early ENT/anaesthesia input, and consider an awake fibreoptic approach rather than standard rapid sequence induction.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern identification (3), correct distinction from variable extrathoracic/ intrathoracic patterns (4), correct further investigation (1), correct airway management implications (2).

Common error / drift trap: Fixed upper airway obstruction can be mistaken for asthma/ COPD given dyspnoea and wheeze-like stridor — the flow-volume loop pattern is a key distinguishing feature that should prompt specific upper airway investigation rather than empirical bronchodilator escalation for presumed lower airway disease.

References

  1. Miller RD, Hyatt RE. Obstructing lesions of the larynx and trachea: clinical and physiologic characteristics. Mayo Clin Proc. 1969;44(3):145–161.

CBS_CRV_12 — Wellens Syndrome ECG Pattern

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man had intermittent chest pain that has now resolved; he is pain-free at presentation. ECG is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "Wellens syndrome ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify this pattern and its significance.
  2. Why is this patient at high risk despite feeling well right now?
  3. What investigation is specifically contraindicated, and why?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Deeply inverted (or biphasic) T waves in V2–V3 in a pain-free patient with a history of recent chest pain — Wellens syndrome, indicating critical proximal LAD stenosis.
  • Despite looking reassuringly "pain-free with an abnormal-but-stable-looking ECG," these patients are at high risk of imminent extensive anterior MI if untreated — the pattern reflects critical, unstable coronary stenosis rather than resolved ischaemia.
  • Exercise/stress testing is contraindicated — it risks provoking an extensive anterior MI in a patient with critical proximal LAD disease.
  • Management: urgent cardiology referral for early invasive coronary angiography, treating this as a high-risk ACS pathway despite the absence of current pain or dramatic ECG changes elsewhere.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern recognition and significance (4), correct high-risk-despite-pain-free reasoning (2), correct contraindication to stress testing (2), correct urgent angiography-based management (2).

Common error / drift trap: A pain-free patient with a seemingly stable ECG can be falsely reassured against significant coronary disease. Wellens syndrome specifically identifies a pain-free patient at very high risk of imminent extensive anterior MI — exercise/stress testing is contraindicated and urgent invasive assessment is required instead.

References

  1. de Zwaan C, Bär FW, Wellens HJ. Characteristic electrocardiographic pattern indicating a critical stenosis high in left anterior descending coronary artery. Am Heart J. 1982;103(4):730–736.

CBS_CRV_13 — EEG Burst Suppression

Curves / Waveforms Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient on a high-dose propofol infusion for refractory status epilepticus has continuous EEG monitoring in place.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic EEG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "EEG burst suppression"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe this pattern.
  2. What are the two very different contexts in which this pattern occurs?
  3. How do you distinguish which context applies in a given patient?
  4. What caution applies to prognostication based on this pattern?
Reveal model answer
  • Burst-suppression: alternating periods of high-amplitude electrical activity ("bursts") with periods of electrical silence ("suppression").
  • Two very different contexts: (1) iatrogenic/therapeutic — a deliberate target when titrating anaesthetic infusions (e.g. propofol, thiopental) for refractory status epilepticus or severe ICP management; (2) pathological — seen in severe hypoxic-ischaemic brain injury, carrying a different, generally poor prognostic implication.
  • Distinguish by context: check the current anaesthetic/sedative infusion status and dose — if the patient is on high-dose sedative/anaesthetic infusion, the pattern may simply reflect the intended drug effect rather than underlying brain injury.
  • Caution: burst-suppression seen while a patient is still on high-dose sedative/anaesthetic infusion is confounded and should not be used for prognostication — assessment should be deferred or repeated after adequate drug clearance, consistent with general neuro-prognostication principles (see CCS_L10).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern description (3), correct identification of both contexts (3), correct method to distinguish them (2), correct prognostication caution (2).

Common error / drift trap: Burst suppression on EEG is sometimes taken as an automatically poor prognostic sign without considering whether it is iatrogenic (from ongoing high-dose anaesthetic infusion) or a true reflection of underlying brain injury — prognostication must account for confounding sedation.

References

  1. Ching S, Purdon PL, Vijayan S, et al. A neurophysiological-metabolic model for burst suppression. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A. 2012;109(8):3095–3100.

CBS_CRV_14 — Arterial Waveform Morphology: Distributive vs Cardiogenic Shock

Curves / Waveforms Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Two arterial line traces are shown from patients with undifferentiated shock, before treatment has been started.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "arterial waveform shock morphology"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the differences between the two waveforms and link them to the underlying haemodynamics.
  2. What is the clinical value of this pattern recognition?
  3. What limits your ability to rely on waveform morphology alone?
  4. How does this inform your initial management approach in undifferentiated shock?
Reveal model answer
  • Panel A (distributive/septic shock): wide pulse pressure with a rapid, "bounding" upstroke and a low dicrotic notch — reflects low systemic vascular resistance with preserved or high cardiac output. Panel B (cardiogenic shock): narrow pulse pressure with a slow upstroke and diminished amplitude — reflects low stroke volume/cardiac output with compensatory high SVR.
  • Waveform pattern recognition offers a rapid, at-a-glance clue at the bedside to support (not replace) clinical and echocardiographic assessment of undifferentiated shock.
  • Limitations: waveform appearance is affected by arrhythmia, catheter position/damping, and vasopressor use (which itself alters SVR independent of the primary pathology) — it should never be the sole basis for diagnosis.
  • This informs initial management by helping prioritise fluid responsiveness assessment and choice of vasoactive agent while the fuller clinical/echo picture is being obtained — not as a substitute for it.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct description/haemodynamic link for both panels (5), correct clinical value (2), correct limitations named (2), correct integration into initial management approach (1).

Common error / drift trap: Over-relying on arterial waveform morphology alone to diagnose the type of shock, without integrating the full clinical picture and echocardiography, risks misclassification — waveform analysis is a useful adjunct, not a standalone diagnostic test, particularly once vasopressors have already been started.

References

  1. Vincent JL, De Backer D. Circulatory Shock. N Engl J Med. 2013;369:1726–1734.

CBS_CRV_15 — Portal Vein Doppler Waveform: Acute Portal Vein Thrombosis

Curves / Waveforms GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with known cirrhosis develops sudden worsening ascites and abdominal pain. Doppler ultrasound of the portal vein is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • Radiopaedia — search: "portal vein thrombosis Doppler" (cases)

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the finding and name the diagnosis.
  2. What underlying causes should you consider in a cirrhotic patient, and how would you distinguish bland from tumoural thrombus?
  3. Would you anticoagulate this patient? Discuss the evolving evidence.
  4. What are the implications for transplant candidacy?
Reveal model answer
  • Absent flow signal within the portal vein lumen (versus normal continuous hepatopetal flow) — acute portal vein thrombosis (PVT).
  • In cirrhosis, PVT results from reduced portal flow velocity and a hypercoagulable state; also screen for hepatocellular carcinoma, as PVT can be tumour-related — an arterial signal within the thrombus on Doppler suggests tumoural rather than bland thrombus.
  • Anticoagulation is increasingly supported even in cirrhotic patients with acute/ symptomatic PVT — traditionally avoided due to bleeding concerns from coexisting varices, but current evidence shows anticoagulation improves recanalisation and outcomes in appropriately selected patients, provided variceal bleeding prophylaxis (endoscopic banding/beta-blockade) is addressed first.
  • PVT can complicate or preclude the standard surgical technique for liver transplantation — urgent specialist hepatology/transplant input is needed to assess implications for candidacy and timing.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnosis (2), correct bland-vs-tumoural differentiation (3), correct, evidence-based anticoagulation discussion (4), transplant implications (1).

Guideline-drift trap: Anticoagulation for portal vein thrombosis in cirrhotic patients was traditionally avoided due to bleeding concerns. Current evidence increasingly supports anticoagulation in acute/symptomatic PVT (with variceal prophylaxis addressed first) — reflexively withholding it solely because the patient is cirrhotic is now considered outdated in appropriately selected patients.

References

  1. European Association for the Study of the Liver. EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines: Vascular diseases of the liver. J Hepatol. 2016;64(1):179–202.

CBS_CRV_16 — Thromboelastography: Hyperfibrinolysis

Curves / Waveforms Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A trauma patient has ongoing bleeding despite initial transfusion. A TEG trace is obtained.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "TEG hyperfibrinolysis"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern and name it.
  2. What is its clinical significance in trauma?
  3. Does this change whether you give tranexamic acid, given CRASH-2 established early empirical use?
  4. What is the opposite pattern, and why does it matter?
Reveal model answer
  • Rapid initial clot formation followed by premature clot breakdown (reduced LY30/elevated fibrinolysis) — hyperfibrinolysis.
  • Hyperfibrinolysis in trauma is associated with significantly increased mortality and identifies patients who particularly benefit from antifibrinolytic therapy.
  • No — CRASH-2 established that tranexamic acid should be given early and empirically (within 3 hours of injury) in trauma with significant bleeding risk, regardless of TEG availability; TEG/ROTEM helps guide additional or ongoing haemostatic therapy beyond the initial empirical dose, not replace the decision to give it early.
  • The opposite pattern — "fibrinolysis shutdown" (hypofibrinolysis) — is a recognised phenotype associated with thrombotic complications rather than bleeding; further antifibrinolytic therapy would not be beneficial in this pattern and could theoretically be harmful.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern identification (3), correct clinical significance (2), correct answer preserving early empirical TXA per CRASH-2 (3), correct fibrinolysis-shutdown counterpart (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming TEG/ROTEM should gate the decision to give tranexamic acid is a misunderstanding — CRASH-2 established early empirical TXA regardless of TEG availability; viscoelastic testing guides additional/ongoing haemostatic therapy, not the initial TXA decision. Equally, the opposite "fibrinolysis shutdown" phenotype exists and should not be treated with further antifibrinolytics.

References

  1. CRASH-2 trial collaborators. Effects of tranexamic acid on death, vascular occlusive events, and blood transfusion in trauma patients (CRASH-2). Lancet. 2010;376(9734):23–32.
  2. Moore HB, Moore EE, Gonzalez E, et al. Fibrinolysis shutdown correlates with poor outcome in trauma patients. J Trauma Acute Care Surg. 2014;77(6):811–817.

CBS_CRV_17 — QTc Prolongation and Torsades de Pointes

Curves / Waveforms Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient on multiple QT-prolonging drugs (ondansetron, haloperidol, azithromycin) with hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia has a QTc of 550 ms, followed by a run of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "torsades de pointes ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the rhythm and describe the preceding abnormality.
  2. List the categories of risk factors/causes for this presentation.
  3. What is your immediate treatment, regardless of the measured magnesium level?
  4. What preventive strategy applies going forward?
Reveal model answer
  • A markedly prolonged QT interval precedes a run of polymorphic VT with a twisting QRS axis around the baseline — Torsades de Pointes.
  • Risk factor categories: drug-induced (antiemetics, antipsychotics, certain antibiotics/antifungals, antiarrhythmics — all present here), electrolyte derangement (hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, hypocalcaemia — both present here), congenital long QT syndrome, and bradycardia.
  • IV magnesium sulfate is first-line, regardless of the measured serum magnesium level — it stabilises the myocardium and suppresses early afterdepolarisations independent of correcting a magnesium deficit per se. Also correct potassium to high-normal, stop all QT-prolonging drugs, and use overdrive pacing or isoprenaline for bradycardia-dependent/recurrent episodes; defibrillate if it degenerates to VF/pulseless arrest.
  • Prevention: review the medication list for cumulative QT-prolonging drug burden, proactively correct electrolytes in at-risk patients, and use QT-monitoring tools when starting multiple QT-prolonging drugs concurrently.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct rhythm/preceding abnormality identification (3), risk factor categories named (3), correct magnesium-regardless-of-level answer (2), sensible prevention strategy (2).

Common error / drift trap: Magnesium is sometimes withheld if the measured serum magnesium level is "normal." IV magnesium is first-line treatment for Torsades de Pointes regardless of the measured level, since its mechanism is myocardial stabilisation, not simply correcting a deficiency.

References

  1. Al-Khatib SM, Stevenson WG, Ackerman MJ, et al. 2017 AHA/ACC/HRS Guideline for Management of Patients With Ventricular Arrhythmias. Circulation. 2018;138(13):e272–e391.

CBS_CRV_18 — Cardiotocography: Late Decelerations

Curves / Waveforms Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A labouring woman's CTG trace shows a pattern of decelerations with reduced baseline variability.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic CTG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "CTG late decelerations"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Classify this deceleration pattern and explain how timing relative to contractions distinguishes it from other deceleration types.
  2. Why is this pattern, combined with reduced variability, particularly concerning?
  3. What immediate conservative measures would you take?
  4. What is your escalation plan if it does not resolve?
Reveal model answer
  • Late decelerations: onset after the peak of the contraction, with a gradual decline and recovery, and the nadir occurring after the contraction peak — reflecting uteroplacental insufficiency. This contrasts with early decelerations (mirror the contraction, from head compression, generally benign) and variable decelerations (abrupt onset, variable timing, from cord compression).
  • Late decelerations combined with reduced baseline variability is a particularly concerning combination, suggesting a higher likelihood of fetal hypoxia/acidaemia than either feature alone.
  • Conservative measures: maternal repositioning (left lateral), stop oxytocin if running, IV fluid bolus, treat maternal hypotension, and consider tocolysis if uterine hyperstimulation is present.
  • If the pattern persists or worsens despite conservative measures: fetal scalp blood sampling for pH/lactate if available and appropriate, otherwise proceed to expedited delivery (instrumental or caesarean).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct classification and timing-based distinction from other deceleration types (4), correct significance of the combination with reduced variability (2), correct conservative measures (2), correct escalation plan (2).

Common error / drift trap: Late decelerations are sometimes mistaken for early decelerations if timing relative to the contraction peak is not carefully assessed, leading to a falsely reassuring interpretation of a genuinely concerning trace.

References

  1. National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. NG229: Fetal monitoring in labour.

CBS_CRV_19 — Cushing's Reflex

Curves / Waveforms Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe TBI has rising ICP. The trended arterial pressure, heart rate, and ICP are shown together over the same time period.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "Cushing reflex ICP"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Name this pattern and describe its three components.
  2. Explain the underlying pathophysiology.
  3. Is this an early warning sign or a late one? Explain the significance.
  4. Would you treat the hypertension directly? Justify your answer.
Reveal model answer
  • Cushing's triad/reflex: rising systolic blood pressure with widening pulse pressure, bradycardia, and irregular respiration, occurring as ICP rises toward/exceeds MAP.
  • Pathophysiology: a compensatory reflex — rising ICP threatens cerebral perfusion pressure, triggering increased sympathetic outflow that raises systemic BP to maintain CPP; this then triggers a baroreceptor-mediated reflex bradycardia.
  • This is a late, ominous sign of critically raised ICP/impending herniation, not an early warning — by the time it is evident, ICP is already critically elevated and urgent action is required.
  • No — treating the hypertension directly with antihypertensive medication would be dangerous, since the hypertension is compensatory to preserve cerebral perfusion in the face of critically raised ICP. The correct response is immediate escalation of ICP-lowering therapy (see CCS_L04) and urgent neurosurgical involvement, not treating the blood pressure.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct naming and description of all three components (4), correct pathophysiology (2), correct "late sign" significance (2), correct, justified refusal to treat BP directly (2).

Common error / drift trap: Treating the hypertension component of Cushing's reflex with antihypertensive medication is a dangerous error — the hypertension is compensatory, and lowering BP in this context would reduce cerebral perfusion pressure and worsen cerebral ischaemia. The correct response is urgent treatment of the underlying raised ICP.

References

  1. Fodstad H, Kelly PJ, Buchfelder M. History of the Cushing reflex. Neurosurgery. 2006;59(5):1132–1137.

CBS_CRV_20 — Cardiotocography: Variable Decelerations and Cord Compression

Curves / Waveforms Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A labouring woman's CTG shows abrupt decelerations that vary in timing, shape and depth relative to contractions.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic CTG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "CTG variable decelerations"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify this deceleration type and its typical mechanism.
  2. Distinguish "typical/uncomplicated" from "atypical/complicated" variable decelerations.
  3. Outline your immediate conservative management.
  4. When would you escalate?
Reveal model answer
  • Variable decelerations: abrupt onset/offset, variable in timing, shape and depth relative to contractions, often with a rapid "V" or "W" shape — reflecting cord compression.
  • Typical/uncomplicated: shouldering present, quick recovery to baseline. Atypical/ complicated features — loss of variability, slow return to baseline, a biphasic shape, or a prolonged deceleration >60 seconds — significantly raise concern for fetal compromise and should not be dismissed simply because variable decelerations are common and usually less concerning than late decelerations in general.
  • Conservative management: maternal repositioning to relieve cord compression, consider amnioinfusion if oligohydramnios is contributing, and address other reversible causes.
  • Escalate — similar to late decelerations — with persistent atypical features despite conservative measures: consider fetal scalp blood sampling or proceed to expedited delivery.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/mechanism (3), correct typical-vs-atypical distinction (3), correct conservative measures (2), correct escalation criteria (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming all variable decelerations are benign because they are common in labour is a mistake — atypical features significantly raise concern for fetal compromise and should not be dismissed simply because the deceleration type is usually less concerning in general teaching.

References

  1. National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. NG229: Fetal monitoring in labour.

CBS_CRV_21 — Arterial Line Waveform Damping: The Square-Wave Flush Test

Curves / Waveforms General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·4·5·11 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An arterial line trace looks abnormally rounded with a low amplitude. A square-wave (fast-flush) test is performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "arterial line damping square wave test"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe how to perform and interpret the square-wave (fast-flush) test.
  2. What effect does overdamping have on the displayed blood pressure values?
  3. What would underdamping ("ringing") look like, and what effect does it have?
  4. What are common causes of overdamping, and how would you correct them?
Reveal model answer
  • Rapidly flush the line and observe the trace's return to baseline. A normally damped system shows 1–2 oscillations before settling. An overdamped system shows a slow, non-oscillating return ("flat top"), as seen here.
  • Overdamping tends to underestimate systolic and overestimate diastolic pressure, narrowing the apparent pulse pressure.
  • Underdamping ("ringing") shows excessive oscillation before settling, causing overestimation of systolic and underestimation of diastolic pressure.
  • Common causes of overdamping: air bubbles in the line/transducer, a kinked catheter/tubing, a clot at the catheter tip, loose connections, or compliant/long tubing. Correction: flush the system, check for kinks, remove air, and consider catheter repositioning if the problem persists.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct test technique/interpretation (3), correct overdamping effect on BP values (2), correct underdamping description/effect (3), causes and correction named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Treating the numeric blood pressure value from a damped or ringing arterial line trace at face value, without checking waveform quality first, risks inappropriate clinical decisions — the damping issue should be corrected/recognised before trusting the displayed numbers.

References

  1. Saugel B, Kouz K, Meidert AS, et al. How to measure blood pressure using an arterial catheter: a systematic 5-step approach. Crit Care. 2020;24:172.

CBS_CRV_22 — ROTEM Pattern: Isolated Fibrinogen Deficiency

Curves / Waveforms Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A woman with major obstetric haemorrhage has ROTEM performed as part of her massive transfusion protocol.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "ROTEM FIBTEM fibrinogen deficiency"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this pattern.
  2. Why is fibrinogen often the first factor to become critically low in major haemorrhage?
  3. What is your targeted management, and why is this more efficient than FFP alone?
  4. How would you confirm adequate correction?
Reveal model answer
  • EXTEM parameters are relatively preserved (clotting factors present) but FIBTEM (which isolates the fibrin/platelet contribution by inhibiting platelets) shows markedly reduced maximum clot firmness — isolated hypofibrinogenaemia.
  • Fibrinogen is often the first factor to fall critically low in major haemorrhage (major obstetric haemorrhage especially) due to both consumption/dilution and its relatively low baseline reserve compared with other clotting factors — a low FIBTEM amplitude correlates with bleeding risk.
  • Targeted replacement with cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate is more efficient than FFP alone, which contains relatively dilute fibrinogen and may not correct hypofibrinogenaemia quickly or adequately enough. Target fibrinogen >1.5–2 g/L in major obstetric haemorrhage (see CCS_L09).
  • Confirm correction with repeat ROTEM/fibrinogen level, avoiding both under- and over-transfusion.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern interpretation (3), correct fibrinogen-depletion rationale (2), correct targeted cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen concentrate answer with rationale (3), correct reassessment plan (2).

Common error / drift trap: Giving FFP alone as the default response to abnormal coagulation in major haemorrhage, without recognising isolated fibrinogen deficiency on viscoelastic testing, risks inadequate/slow correction of the specific deficiency driving ongoing bleeding.

References

  1. Collins PW, Cannings-John R, Bruynseels D, et al. Viscoelastometric and standard laboratory tests to guide haemostatic treatment in postpartum haemorrhage (FIB-PPH). Br J Anaesth. 2017;119(3):411–421.

CBS_CRV_23 — ECG in Severe Hypothyroidism

Curves / Waveforms Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with myxoedema coma (see CCS_L14) has an ECG recorded on admission.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "hypothyroidism ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the ECG pattern shown.
  2. What mechanisms contribute to the low voltage?
  3. What specific investigation would you perform given one of these mechanisms, and why?
  4. What would you expect with treatment?
Reveal model answer
  • Sinus bradycardia with generalised low-voltage QRS complexes and flattened T waves — a classic pattern of severe hypothyroidism.
  • Mechanisms: myxoedematous infiltration of the myocardium, reduced metabolic rate/sympathetic tone, and — importantly — a pericardial effusion, which is common in severe hypothyroidism and can itself independently cause low voltage.
  • Perform echocardiography specifically to assess for a pericardial effusion — this is a separate, distinct finding from myocardial infiltration and rarely can progress to tamponade if large or rapidly accumulating, requiring different management.
  • These ECG changes should resolve with appropriate thyroid hormone replacement (with the hydrocortisone-first safety caveat from CCS_L14); persistent or worsening changes despite treatment should prompt reassessment for an alternative or additional cardiac pathology.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct ECG description (3), correct mechanisms including effusion (3), correct echocardiography rationale (2), correct expectation with treatment (2).

Common error / drift trap: Low voltage QRS complexes in severe hypothyroidism are sometimes attributed purely to "myxoedematous" myocardial infiltration without considering a coexisting pericardial effusion as a contributing or alternative cause — echocardiography should specifically assess for this, since a large effusion has its own separate tamponade risk.

References

  1. Ono Y, Ono S, Yasunaga H, et al. Clinical characteristics and outcomes of myxedema coma. Endocr J. 2017;64(7):679–685.

CBS_CRV_24 — Trended Intra-Abdominal Pressure During ACS Management

Curves / Waveforms GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe acute pancreatitis (see CCS_S23) has serial intra-abdominal pressure (IAP) measurements trended over 48 hours.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "intra-abdominal pressure trend"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this trend across the three phases shown.
  2. What should each phase trigger in your management?
  3. Why should IAP be correlated with organ dysfunction rather than interpreted as a number alone?
  4. What would you expect immediately after decompression, and what ongoing issues remain?
Reveal model answer
  • Initial rise toward the abdominal compartment syndrome (ACS) threshold, partial response/plateau with medical management, then a secondary rise prompting decompressive laparotomy.
  • Each phase should trigger action: escalating medical management at the first rise (sedation, NG decompression, fluid stewardship — see CCS_S23), close reassessment during the plateau, and decompressive laparotomy consideration at the secondary rise if accompanied by organ dysfunction.
  • A rising IAP without correlating organ dysfunction (urine output, airway pressures, lactate) may not yet mandate surgery — IAP should always be interpreted longitudinally alongside organ-dysfunction correlates, not as a single static threshold.
  • Expect a rapid fall in IAP immediately after decompression, but ongoing open-abdomen management issues remain — fluid losses, nutrition, and staged closure planning.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct three-phase interpretation (3), correct action at each phase (3), correct organ-dysfunction correlation rationale (2), correct post-decompression expectations (2).

Common error / drift trap: Acting on a single IAP measurement in isolation, without trending it against organ dysfunction parameters, can lead to premature surgery or delayed surgery — IAP should always be interpreted longitudinally alongside clinical/organ-dysfunction correlates.

References

  1. Kirkpatrick AW, Roberts DJ, De Waele J, et al. Intra-abdominal hypertension and the abdominal compartment syndrome: updated consensus definitions and clinical practice guidelines. Intensive Care Med. 2013;39(7):1190–1206.

CBS_CRV_25 — Ventilator Auto-Triggering from Cardiac Oscillations

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A deeply sedated patient with a very sensitive ventilator trigger setting shows regular, small, rhythmic triggered breaths on the ventilator graphics.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "ventilator auto-triggering cardiac oscillation"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify this pattern and explain the mechanism.
  2. Why does a deeply sedated, low-drive patient make this more likely?
  3. What are the consequences of failing to recognise this?
  4. What is your corrective action?
Reveal model answer
  • Triggered breaths occurring at a fixed rate matching the heart rate (not typical respiratory rate variability) — auto-triggering from cardiac oscillations. Small pressure/flow oscillations transmitted from cardiac contractions through the great vessels/lung parenchyma are sensed by an overly sensitive ventilator trigger, causing inappropriate triggering not representing true patient respiratory effort.
  • A deeply sedated patient with low or absent respiratory drive and a very sensitive trigger threshold provides the ideal conditions — there is little or no competing genuine respiratory effort to mask the cardiac-oscillation signal.
  • Unrecognised auto-triggering can lead to a false impression of adequate spontaneous respiratory drive, inappropriate assessment of sedation depth, inappropriate weaning trial decisions, and respiratory alkalosis if the triggered volume is significant.
  • Adjust trigger sensitivity (make it less sensitive) to eliminate cardiac-oscillation triggering while still allowing genuine patient effort to trigger, and correlate ventilator graphics with actual clinical assessment of respiratory effort/sedation depth rather than relying on the "triggered breath count" alone.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification and mechanism (4), correct explanation of why sedation/low drive matters (2), consequences of missing it (2), correct corrective action (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming a ventilator displaying "triggered" breaths automatically reflects genuine patient respiratory effort is a common error — cardiac oscillation auto-triggering can mimic patient triggering in a deeply sedated patient, leading to false reassurance about respiratory drive/readiness to wean.

References

  1. Pham T, Telias I, Piraino T, et al. Asynchrony Consensus Group. Recognition of inspiratory muscle effort during mechanical ventilation. Crit Care. 2022;26:31.

CBS_CRV_26 — Brugada Pattern ECG

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 35-year-old man with a family history of sudden cardiac death presents with a febrile illness and a syncopal episode. ECG is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "Brugada syndrome ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the pattern and distinguish it from the non-diagnostic variant.
  2. What is the clinical significance?
  3. What has likely unmasked this pattern now, and what other precipitants exist?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Type 1 Brugada pattern: coved ST elevation ≥2 mm in V1–V2 followed by an inverted T wave — the only pattern diagnostic on its own. Type 2 ("saddleback" morphology) is not diagnostic alone and requires provocation testing/additional criteria.
  • A channelopathy (often SCN5A mutation) associated with risk of malignant ventricular arrhythmia and sudden cardiac death, frequently presenting with syncope or cardiac arrest; family history is important.
  • Fever is a well-established, important precipitant that can unmask the Brugada pattern and its associated arrhythmic risk — this patient's febrile illness is the likely trigger. Other precipitants: certain antiarrhythmics/psychotropics/anaesthetic agents, and electrolyte disturbance.
  • Management: aggressively treat/correct the precipitant (antipyretics for fever, avoid Brugada-provoking drugs), urgent cardiology/electrophysiology referral, and consider ICD in symptomatic patients (syncope/aborted sudden death) or high-risk asymptomatic patients per risk stratification.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct Type 1 vs Type 2 distinction (3), correct clinical significance (2), correct identification of fever as the precipitant with other precipitants named (3), correct management (2).

Common error / drift trap: A Brugada ECG pattern found incidentally during a febrile illness is sometimes not recognised as being unmasked by fever specifically — fever is a well- established precipitant, and aggressive antipyretic treatment plus cardiology referral is warranted rather than dismissing the finding as incidental.

References

  1. Antzelevitch C, Yan GX, Ackerman MJ, et al. J-Wave syndromes expert consensus conference report. Europace. 2017;19(4):665–694.

CBS_CRV_27 — Nerve Conduction Study: Demyelinating Pattern in Guillain-Barré Syndrome

Curves / Waveforms Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with suspected Guillain-Barré syndrome (see CCS_S17) has a nerve conduction study performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "nerve conduction study Guillain-Barre"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this nerve conduction pattern.
  2. Contrast this with an axonal pattern, and note its prognostic relevance.
  3. Would a normal study done on day 1 exclude GBS? Explain.
  4. How should this result be integrated with your overall clinical management?
Reveal model answer
  • Reduced conduction velocity, prolonged distal motor latency, and conduction block/temporal dispersion — a demyelinating pattern (acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy, AIDP — the most common GBS subtype).
  • An axonal pattern (reduced amplitude with relatively preserved velocity — AMAN/ AMSAN variants) generally carries a worse/slower prognosis for recovery than the demyelinating (AIDP) pattern.
  • No — demyelinating changes can take time to develop, and an NCS performed very early in the disease course (within the first few days) can be normal or show only subtle changes even in genuine GBS. A normal early NCS does not exclude the diagnosis.
  • NCS supports but does not replace clinical assessment — respiratory monitoring decisions (serial FVC, per CCS_S17) should proceed based on clinical trajectory regardless of NCS timing or results, not be delayed awaiting a "confirmatory" study.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern interpretation (3), correct axonal contrast and prognostic relevance (3), correct "normal early NCS does not exclude" answer (2), correct integration with clinical management (2).

Common error / drift trap: A normal or equivocal NCS very early in the disease course is sometimes taken as evidence against a GBS diagnosis. Clinical diagnosis and respiratory monitoring decisions should not be delayed awaiting a "confirmatory" NCS if the clinical picture is convincing.

References

  1. Willison HJ, Jacobs BC, van Doorn PA. Guillain-Barré syndrome. Lancet. 2016;388(10045):717–727.

CBS_CRV_28 — Oesophageal Pressure and Transpulmonary Pressure in ARDS

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe ARDS and a raised BMI has an oesophageal balloon catheter placed. Airway and oesophageal pressure traces are shown together.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "oesophageal pressure transpulmonary pressure"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Explain the concept of transpulmonary pressure and how it is calculated.
  2. Why might this be particularly relevant in this patient?
  3. What does the key current trial evidence show?
  4. What is the current practical role of this technique?
Reveal model answer
  • Oesophageal pressure is used as a surrogate for pleural pressure. Transpulmonary pressure = airway pressure − oesophageal (pleural) pressure — representing the pressure actually distending the lung, as opposed to airway pressure, which reflects the whole respiratory system (lung plus chest wall).
  • In patients with high chest wall elastance (obesity, abdominal distension, oedema — relevant to this patient's raised BMI), airway/plateau pressure can overestimate the pressure actually stressing the lung. Oesophageal-pressure-guided PEEP titration aims to individualise PEEP based on the actual transpulmonary pressure rather than a fixed table.
  • The EPVent-2 trial (Beitler et al., JAMA 2019) compared oesophageal-pressure-guided PEEP titration against an empirical high PEEP-FiO2 table strategy in moderate-severe ARDS and found no significant difference in death or ventilator-free days.
  • The technique remains of physiological interest and is used in some specialised centres or challenging cases (e.g. very high chest wall elastance), but is not routinely recommended as standard-of-care PEEP titration given the neutral RCT evidence.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct concept/calculation (3), correct relevance to this patient (2), accurate EPVent-2 summary (3), correct current practical role (2).

Common error / drift trap: Physiological rationale for oesophageal-pressure-guided PEEP titration is compelling, and some candidates assume this constitutes proven clinical benefit. EPVent-2, a well-conducted RCT, found no significant outcome difference compared to empirical PEEP-FiO2 tables — present this as a technique of physiological interest without overclaiming a proven outcome benefit.

References

  1. Beitler JR, Sarge T, Banner-Goodspeed VM, et al. Effect of Titrating PEEP With an Esophageal Pressure-Guided Strategy vs an Empirical High PEEP-FiO2 Strategy (EPVent-2). JAMA. 2019;321(9):846–857.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines on acute respiratory distress syndrome. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_CRV_29 — ROX Index for High-Flow Nasal Oxygen Failure Prediction

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe pneumonia is on high-flow nasal oxygen at 60 L/min, FiO2 0.6. The ROX index is trended over the first 12 hours.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "ROX index high flow nasal oxygen"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. How is the ROX index calculated, and what does the trend shown indicate?
  2. What thresholds are used, and how should they be applied?
  3. What is the clinical utility of this index?
  4. What are its limitations?
Reveal model answer
  • ROX index = (SpO2/FiO2) / respiratory rate. The trend shown is falling toward the high-risk threshold over 12 hours, indicating likely HFNC failure and an increasing need for escalation.
  • Validated thresholds (Roca et al.): ROX ≥4.88 at 2, 6, or 12 hours is associated with a lower risk of needing intubation; ROX <3.85 is associated with a high risk of HFNC failure; intermediate values are indeterminate and warrant close monitoring. These are approximate cut-offs to use alongside clinical judgement, not a rigid rule.
  • Helps identify patients failing HFNC who need earlier escalation to NIV/intubation, rather than delayed recognition of deterioration — a practical bedside tool validated in pneumonia-related hypoxaemic respiratory failure, including during the COVID-19 pandemic.
  • Developed/validated primarily in pneumonia-related hypoxaemic respiratory failure, less validated in other aetiologies (e.g. exacerbation of pulmonary fibrosis, cardiogenic pulmonary oedema); should not delay clinically obvious escalation while awaiting a formal calculation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct calculation and trend interpretation (3), correct thresholds (3), correct clinical utility (2), limitations named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Relying on a single ROX index measurement rather than trending it over the first several hours risks premature reassurance or unnecessary escalation — the validated approach uses serial measurements at 2, 6, and 12 hours.

References

  1. Roca O, Caralt B, Messika J, et al. An Index Combining Respiratory Rate and Oxygenation to Predict Outcome of Nasal High-Flow Therapy. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2019;199(11):1368–1376.

CBS_CRV_30 — De Winter T-Wave Pattern: A STEMI Equivalent

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 50-year-old man with chest pain has an ECG showing upsloping ST depression at the J point with tall, symmetric T waves in the precordial leads, without overt ST elevation.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic ECG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "De Winter T wave ECG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify the pattern and its significance.
  2. What is the risk of misclassifying this?
  3. Outline your management.
  4. Name two other STEMI-equivalent ECG patterns.
Reveal model answer
  • Upsloping ST depression at the J point with tall, symmetric T waves in the precordial leads, without overt ST elevation — the De Winter pattern, a STEMI-equivalent indicating acute proximal LAD occlusion despite not meeting standard ST-elevation criteria.
  • Mistaking this for non-specific ST depression/ischaemia (an NSTEMI, watchful-waiting pathway) risks a dangerous delay to emergency reperfusion for a patient with an actively occluded LAD.
  • Treat as a STEMI-equivalent — activate the emergency primary PCI pathway immediately, without waiting for serial troponin as might apply to typical NSTEMI.
  • Other STEMI-equivalents: Wellens syndrome (pain-free, resolved-pain critical stenosis — see CRV_12, a distinct pattern from De Winter's active-occlusion picture), posterior STEMI (ST depression V1–V3 with tall R waves, confirmed with posterior leads V7–V9), and new LBBB with concordant changes (Sgarbossa criteria).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification/significance (3), correct risk of misclassification (2), correct emergency management (3), other STEMI-equivalents named (2).

Common error / drift trap: De Winter pattern is sometimes misclassified as simple ischaemic ST depression warranting an NSTEMI (troponin-driven) pathway. It is a STEMI-equivalent requiring the same urgency as classic ST elevation.

References

  1. de Winter RJ, Verouden NJ, Wellens HJ, Wilde AA. A new ECG sign of proximal LAD occlusion. N Engl J Med. 2008;359(19):2071–2073.
  2. Byrne RA, Rossello X, Coughlan JJ, et al. 2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(38):3720–3826.

CBS_CRV_31 — Pulmonary Artery Catheter Waveform Transitions

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A pulmonary artery catheter is being inserted at the bedside. The pressure trace is monitored continuously as the catheter is advanced.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described patterns for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "pulmonary artery catheter waveform"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Identify each waveform and the transition points.
  2. What waveform clue suggests the catheter has coiled in the RV rather than advancing?
  3. What is a dangerous "overwedged" trace, and how should you respond?
  4. What is the current role of PCWP-based assessment?
Reveal model answer
  • RA: low pressure with a/c/v waves. RV: higher systolic pressure, low diastolic pressure, sharp upstroke. PA: similar systolic pressure to RV but with a dicrotic notch and elevated diastolic pressure (reflecting downstream pulmonary vascular resistance). PCWP/wedge: lower pressure, resembling a damped left atrial trace with a/v waves.
  • A persistent RV trace that fails to transition to a PA trace suggests the catheter has coiled in the RV — carries arrhythmia risk and should prompt repositioning.
  • An "overwedged" trace is persistently high pressure that does not fall on balloon deflation — a warning sign of pulmonary artery rupture risk, requiring immediate recognition and catheter withdrawal, not simply reflating and re-checking.
  • PCWP as an LA pressure surrogate has largely been superseded by echocardiography and less invasive cardiac output monitoring in most modern ICUs, but is still used in select complex cases (e.g. severe pulmonary hypertension, mixed shock states, cardiac surgery); limitations include mitral valve disease, high PEEP affecting transmission, and tachycardia causing a/v wave fusion.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct identification of all waveforms/transitions (4), correct RV-coiling clue (2), correct overwedging recognition/response (2), correct current role of PCWP (2).

Common error / drift trap: PA catheter use has declined with the rise of echocardiography, and trainees may be unfamiliar with recognising a dangerously "overwedged" trace — persistent high pressure that doesn't fall on balloon deflation requires immediate recognition and catheter withdrawal.

References

  1. Vincent JL, Pinsky MR, Sprung CL, et al. The pulmonary artery catheter: in medio virtus. Crit Care Med. 2008;36(11):3093–3096.

CBS_CRV_32 — Transcranial Doppler and the Lindegaard Ratio

Curves / Waveforms Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with aneurysmal SAH, day 7, has transcranial Doppler (TCD) showing elevated MCA velocities.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic waveform tracings constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "transcranial doppler Lindegaard ratio"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Calculate and interpret the Lindegaard ratio.
  2. Why does the ratio matter more than the absolute MCA velocity alone?
  3. How should this finding be used clinically?
  4. What are the limitations of TCD?
Reveal model answer
  • Lindegaard ratio = MCA velocity / extracranial ICA velocity. A ratio >3 suggests true vasospasm rather than a globally hyperdynamic flow state; a ratio >6 suggests severe vasospasm.
  • Absolute MCA velocity can be elevated by a hyperdynamic circulation (e.g. from hypervolaemic therapy, anaemia, fever) without true vessel narrowing. The ratio normalises for overall flow state by comparing to the extracranial ICA, which is not affected by intracranial vasospasm.
  • TCD is a screening/trending tool, not fully diagnostic alone — correlate with clinical examination (new neurological deficit), and confirm with CT angiography or catheter angiography before invasive treatment (endovascular rescue therapy) if concerning.
  • Limitations: operator-dependent, technically difficult in some patients (poor temporal bone window), and most validated for the MCA — less reliable for distal/other vessel territories.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct ratio calculation/interpretation (3), correct rationale for using the ratio (3), correct clinical application (2), limitations named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Interpreting an elevated absolute MCA velocity as diagnostic of vasospasm without calculating the Lindegaard ratio can lead to false positives in a hyperdynamic circulatory state.

References

  1. Lindegaard KF, Nornes H, Bakke SJ, et al. Cerebral vasospasm diagnosis by means of angiography and blood velocity measurements. Acta Neurochir (Wien). 1989;100(1–2):12–24.
  2. Hoh BL, Ko NU, Amin-Hanjani S, et al. 2023 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Aneurysmal Subarachnoid Hemorrhage. Stroke. 2023;54(7):e314–e370.

CBS_CRV_33 — IVC Collapsibility and Fluid Responsiveness

Curves / Waveforms Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A spontaneously breathing patient with septic shock has a subcostal IVC M-mode trace recorded during assessment for fluid responsiveness.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "IVC collapsibility index fluid responsiveness"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the collapsibility index concept and interpret this trace.
  2. How does the technique and threshold differ in a mechanically ventilated, passive patient?
  3. What other factors limit reliability of this measurement?
  4. How would you use this in your overall assessment?
Reveal model answer
  • Collapsibility index = (max diameter − min diameter)/max diameter. In a spontaneously breathing patient, an index >50% (as shown) suggests low right atrial pressure and likely fluid responsiveness; a distended, minimally collapsing IVC suggests elevated right atrial pressure and reduced fluid responsiveness.
  • In a passive, mechanically ventilated patient with adequate tidal volume (≥8 mL/kg) and no spontaneous effort, the distensibility index is used instead — the physiology is opposite (positive pressure inspiration increases IVC diameter). Conflating the two indices/techniques is a common, important error.
  • Unreliable with irregular/weak spontaneous respiratory effort, with low tidal volumes in ventilated patients, with arrhythmias, and with intra-abdominal hypertension (falsely suggests fluid responsiveness).
  • Use as one of several parameters (alongside passive leg raise, pulse pressure variation where valid) correlated with the clinical fluid balance picture, rather than in isolation — particularly important in a patient already showing signs of fluid overload.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct index calculation/interpretation (3), correct spontaneous-vs-ventilated distinction (4), other limitations named (2), correct integration into overall assessment (1).

Common error / drift trap: Using the spontaneously-breathing IVC collapsibility index and its threshold in a mechanically ventilated, passive patient (or vice versa) is a common and important error — the underlying physiology and validated technique differ fundamentally between the two ventilatory contexts.

References

  1. Airapetian N, Maizel J, Alyamani O, et al. Does inferior vena cava respiratory variability predict fluid responsiveness in spontaneously breathing patients? Crit Care. 2015;19:400.
  2. Monnet X, Teboul JL. Prediction of fluid responsiveness. Ann Intensive Care. 2022;12:46.

CBS_CRV_34 — CRRT Circuit Pressures: Trending Toward Filter Clotting

Curves / Waveforms Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·5 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient on CVVHDF has circuit pressures trended over several hours.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "CRRT circuit pressure filter clotting"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Name the four standard CRRT circuit pressures and what each reflects.
  2. Interpret the trend shown.
  3. What proactive steps would you take on seeing this trend?
  4. What would a sudden, isolated change in access or return pressure suggest?
Reveal model answer
  • Access/pre-pump pressure — reflects adequacy of blood draw from the patient (becomes more negative if access is poor/kinked). Filter pressure — pressure within the filter. Return/venous pressure — pressure returning blood to the patient (rises if the return line is obstructed). Transmembrane pressure (TMP) — calculated from filter and effluent pressures, reflects the gradient across the filter membrane.
  • The trace shows a progressively rising, accelerating TMP — the classic pattern of impending filter clotting.
  • Proactively review anticoagulation adequacy (citrate levels/ionised calcium ratios, or systemic heparin dosing/APTT if used), consider flushing or increasing blood flow rate, and plan for a circuit change before complete filter loss, rather than waiting for a clotting alarm.
  • A sudden, very negative access pressure suggests an access problem (kinking, patient repositioning); a sudden high return pressure suggests a return-line issue (clot at the return port, line kinking, or patient movement).
Marking guide

10 marks: correct naming/meaning of all four pressures (4), correct trend interpretation (2), correct proactive response (2), correct isolated-pressure-change reasoning (2).

Common error / drift trap: Waiting until the filter completely clots before addressing anticoagulation or the circuit, rather than recognising and acting on a progressive TMP rise that typically precedes complete clotting by hours, leads to unnecessary treatment interruptions and gaps in renal support.

References

  1. Oudemans-van Straaten HM, Ostermann M. Bench-to-bedside review: Citrate for continuous renal replacement therapy, from science to practice. Crit Care. 2012;16(6):249.

CBS_CRV_35 — Triphasic Waves on EEG in Hepatic Encephalopathy

Curves / Waveforms GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A cirrhotic patient with worsening confusion has an EEG performed.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic EEG tracing constructed to represent the described pattern for teaching purposes.

Real reference waveforms (external, open-license)

  • LITFL — search: "triphasic waves EEG"

Search link only — not verified from within this session.

Structured questions

  1. Describe the pattern and its typical clinical associations.
  2. What important differential must you consider, and why does it matter?
  3. How would you grade this patient's presentation clinically?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Generalised, bisynchronous waveforms with a negative-positive-negative morphology, frontally predominant, often with anterior-to-posterior phase lag — triphasic waves, classically associated with metabolic encephalopathies including hepatic encephalopathy, though not specific to it.
  • Important differential: non-convulsive status epilepticus (NCSE) — triphasic waves can be difficult to distinguish from NCSE on EEG, and this has real management implications (triphasic waves from metabolic encephalopathy should be treated by addressing the underlying cause, not escalating anti-epileptic therapy; NCSE requires specific anti-seizure treatment). A trial of a short-acting benzodiazepine with EEG monitoring can sometimes help distinguish the two, though specialist neurology/EEG input is valuable.
  • Grade using the West Haven criteria (clinical grades 1–4) and identify/treat precipitants (GI bleed, infection, constipation, electrolyte disturbance, medication non-compliance, TIPS).
  • Management: lactulose (titrated to 2–3 soft stools/day), rifaximin as an add-on for recurrent/ refractory cases, treat the precipitant, and avoid sedatives/opioids that can worsen encephalopathy.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct pattern/associations (3), correct NCSE differential with rationale (3), correct grading (2), correct management (2).

Common error / drift trap: Triphasic waves are sometimes assumed to be diagnostic of hepatic/metabolic encephalopathy without considering NCSE, which can have a similar appearance but requires fundamentally different management.

References

  1. Vilstrup H, Amodio P, Bajaj J, et al. Hepatic Encephalopathy in Chronic Liver Disease: 2014 Practice Guideline by AASLD and EASL. Hepatology. 2014;60(2):715–735.
  2. Sutter R, Kaplan PW. Electroencephalographic criteria for nonconvulsive status epilepticus. Epilepsia. 2012;53(Suppl 3):1–51.

CBS_BIO_29 — Static/Dynamic Compliance: Localising a Rising Peak Pressure

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A ventilated ARDS patient's peak airway pressure has suddenly risen. Ventilator readings are recorded.

ParameterResult
Tidal volume450 mL
PEEP8 cmH2O
Plateau pressure26 cmH2O
Peak pressure34 cmH2O

Structured questions

  1. Calculate static compliance and interpret it.
  2. Calculate dynamic compliance and the peak-minus-plateau gradient. What does this isolate?
  3. Use this distinction to explain how you would troubleshoot a sudden rise in peak pressure.
  4. How does driving pressure relate to this calculation, and why does it matter?
Reveal model answer
  • Static compliance = Vt/(Pplat − PEEP) = 450/(26−8) = 25 mL/cmH2O (reduced; normal ≈50–80 mL/cmH2O) — consistent with stiff, ARDS-affected lungs.
  • Dynamic compliance = Vt/(Ppeak − PEEP) = 450/(34−8) ≈ 17.3 mL/cmH2O. The peak-minus-plateau gradient (34−26 = 8 cmH2O, elevated) isolates the airway resistance component — separate from the compliance (stiffness) problem.
  • A rising peak pressure with an unchanged plateau points to a resistance problem (bronchospasm, secretions, a kinked/blocked tube, ETT cuff herniation). A rising peak and plateau together points to a compliance problem (worsening ARDS, pneumothorax, abdominal distension, patient-ventilator dyssynchrony/breath stacking) — each requires a completely different troubleshooting pathway.
  • Driving pressure (Pplat − PEEP = 18 cmH2O here) is the parameter most closely linked to mortality risk in ARDS (Amato et al., 2015) — distinct from raw plateau pressure alone, and a useful individualised target alongside standard lung-protective settings.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct static compliance calculation/interpretation (3), correct dynamic compliance/ peak-minus-plateau gradient (3), correct troubleshooting logic (4), correct driving pressure link (2).

Common error / drift trap: Treating a rising peak pressure alone (without checking plateau pressure) as necessarily indicating worsening lung compliance/ARDS progression is a common error — the peak-minus-plateau gradient specifically isolates the airway resistance component and points to a completely different troubleshooting approach.

References

  1. Amato MB, Meade MO, Slutsky AS, et al. Driving Pressure and Survival in the Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome. N Engl J Med. 2015;372:747–755.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines on acute respiratory distress syndrome. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_BIO_30 — Driving Pressure: Association vs Proven Causal Benefit

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Two ARDS patients are ventilated with the same tidal volume and PEEP, but different plateau pressures due to different respiratory system compliance.
