ParameterPatient APatient B
Tidal volume420 mL420 mL
PEEP10 cmH2O10 cmH2O
Plateau pressure22 cmH2O32 cmH2O

Structured questions

  1. Calculate driving pressure for each patient and interpret the difference.
  2. Why might driving pressure be a more meaningful target than a fixed low tidal volume alone?
  3. Outline the practical target and how you would achieve it.
  4. What is the key evidentiary caveat about the driving pressure evidence base?
Reveal model answer
  • Driving pressure = Pplat − PEEP. Patient A: 22−10 = 12 cmH2O. Patient B: 32−10 = 22 cmH2O. Despite identical tidal volume and PEEP, Patient B has a substantially higher driving pressure, associated with worse prognosis.
  • Driving pressure reflects tidal volume normalised to individual respiratory system compliance — the same 6 mL/kg might be lung-protective in one patient but still injurious in another with much lower compliance, as seen here.
  • Aim to keep driving pressure <15 cmH2O where possible by adjusting tidal volume/PEEP, while still respecting basic lung-protective principles (Vt 4–8 mL/kg PBW, Pplat <30 cmH2O).
  • Amato's landmark finding was a secondary/mediation analysis of pooled existing RCT data showing association, not itself a prospective RCT testing a driving-pressure-targeted ventilation strategy against a control. While highly influential and incorporated into current guidance (including the 2023 ESICM ARDS guideline), it should be presented with this evidentiary caveat — strong association evidence, not proven causal benefit of a targeted strategy.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct calculations for both patients (4), correct rationale for individualised targeting (3), correct practical target (2), correct evidentiary caveat (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Driving pressure evidence is sometimes presented as though a prospective randomised trial demonstrated that targeting driving pressure improves survival. Amato's analysis was a secondary/mediation analysis of pooled RCT data showing association, not itself a prospective RCT of a driving-pressure-targeted strategy.

References

  1. Amato MB, Meade MO, Slutsky AS, et al. Driving Pressure and Survival in the Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome. N Engl J Med. 2015;372:747–755.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines on acute respiratory distress syndrome. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_BIO_31 — High-Sensitivity Troponin 0/1-Hour Rule-Out Algorithm

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old man presents with chest pain. High-sensitivity troponin T is measured at presentation and repeated at 1 hour.

ParameterResult
hs-cTnT (0h)8 ng/L
hs-cTnT (1h)9 ng/L
ECGNo ischaemic changes
SymptomsResolved, low ongoing suspicion

Structured questions

  1. Explain the ESC 0/1-hour hs-troponin algorithm.
  2. Apply it to this patient's values and state the pathway.
  3. What key caveat applies to the thresholds used?
  4. Does a "rule-out" result end the assessment regardless of clinical picture?
Reveal model answer
  • The algorithm has three pathways: rule-out (a very low baseline value below the assay-specific threshold, or a low baseline value with a small absolute 1-hour change); rule-in (a high baseline value, or an intermediate value with a large absolute 1-hour rise); and an "observe" zone for indeterminate cases needing further testing (e.g. a repeat at 3 hours).
  • This patient's very low, minimally-changing values place him on the rule-out pathway — appropriate to avoid admission for serial troponins/functional testing in this low-risk presentation.
  • Thresholds are assay-specific — different hs-troponin assays have different validated cut-offs, and numbers must never be mixed between assays.
  • No — a "rule-out" biomarker result still requires clinical correlation; persistent symptoms or high pre-test probability should prompt further testing regardless of a reassuring biomarker trajectory, since a minority of true ACS presentations can have a falsely reassuring early trajectory.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct explanation of the three pathways (5), correct application to this patient (3), correct assay-specificity caveat (2), correct clinical-correlation caveat (2).

Common error / drift trap: Applying threshold numbers from one hs-troponin assay to results from a different assay is a dangerous, genuine clinical error since validated thresholds are assay-specific and not interchangeable.

References

  1. Byrne RA, Rossello X, Coughlan JJ, et al. 2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(38):3720–3826.

CBS_BIO_32 — Residual Inflammatory Risk: hs-CRP and Colchicine in Secondary Prevention

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient recovering from MI is on a statin with well-controlled LDL cholesterol, but hs-CRP remains elevated.

Structured questions

  1. What does persistently elevated hs-CRP represent in this context?
  2. What is the current evidence for anti-inflammatory secondary prevention?
  3. What practical cautions apply to this therapy?
  4. How does this fit into the broader secondary prevention strategy?
Reveal model answer
  • hs-CRP is a marker of residual inflammatory cardiovascular risk, distinct from residual cholesterol risk — a concept established by earlier trials identifying inflammation as an independent therapeutic target.
  • Two trials established low-dose colchicine (0.5 mg daily) as an evidence-based option: COLCOT (Tardif et al., NEJM 2019, post-MI) and LoDoCo2 (Nidorf et al., NEJM 2020, chronic coronary disease) both showed reduced cardiovascular events with low-dose colchicine in patients with established coronary disease — now incorporated into some contemporary secondary prevention guidance as an option in appropriate patients.
  • Cautions: GI side effects, rare but serious myotoxicity (especially with concurrent statins/CYP3A4 inhibitors or renal impairment), and general avoidance in severe renal/hepatic impairment — patient selection and counselling are needed.
  • Colchicine is an emerging add-on, not a replacement for standard lipid-lowering (statin/LDL targets remain foundational), antiplatelet, and beta-blocker secondary prevention.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct inflammatory-risk concept (3), accurate COLCOT/LoDoCo2 summary (4), correct cautions (3), correct positioning as add-on not replacement (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Colchicine for cardiovascular secondary prevention is a genuinely newer (post-2019) practice that many older training resources won't mention — candidates unfamiliar with COLCOT/LoDoCo2 may not recognise anti-inflammatory therapy as an evidence-based adjunct in appropriately selected patients, an increasingly examinable area given its incorporation into contemporary secondary prevention guidance.

References

  1. Tardif JC, Kouz S, Waters DD, et al. Efficacy and Safety of Low-Dose Colchicine after Myocardial Infarction (COLCOT). N Engl J Med. 2019;381:2497–2505.
  2. Nidorf SM, Fiolet ATL, Mosterd A, et al. Colchicine in Patients with Chronic Coronary Disease (LoDoCo2). N Engl J Med. 2020;383:1838–1847.

CBS_BIO_33 — Neuron-Specific Enolase in Post-Cardiac Arrest Prognostication

Biochemistry / Data Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient remains comatose on day 3 after cardiac arrest, off sedation. Serum neuron-specific enolase (NSE) is measured as part of multimodal prognostication.

Structured questions

  1. What does an elevated NSE represent, and how should it be used?
  2. What technical pitfall commonly invalidates an NSE result?
  3. What other modalities should be combined with NSE, and why?
  4. What timing principles apply to this whole prognostication process?
Reveal model answer
  • NSE is a marker of neuronal injury; higher values (checked at ≥48 hours, ideally with a second sample to confirm the trend) are associated with worse prognosis when combined with other multimodal findings — it must never be used as a standalone prognostic test.
  • Haemolysis falsely elevates NSE (released from red cells) — a haemolysed sample must be excluded or repeated, a frequently tested practical point.
  • Combine with clinical examination (pupillary/corneal reflexes, myoclonus status), EEG (unreactive background, burst suppression, status epilepticus), imaging (diffuse anoxic injury on CT/MRI), and somatosensory evoked potentials (bilateral absence of the N20 response — a very specific poor- prognosis marker) — no single test is used alone.
  • Avoid prognostication before 72 hours post-arrest (longer if TTM/sedation confounds), require ≥2 concordant poor-prognosis indicators before considering withdrawal, and avoid the self-fulfilling prophecy of early pessimistic assessment (see CCS_L10).
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation/multimodal use (4), correct haemolysis pitfall (3), other modalities named (3), correct timing principles (2).

Common error / drift trap: Using a single elevated NSE value in isolation to guide a withdrawal-of-care decision, without excluding haemolysis or combining with other multimodal markers, risks incorrect and premature pessimistic prognostication — no single test, including NSE, should be used alone.

References

  1. Sandroni C, Cronberg T, Sekhon M. Brain injury after cardiac arrest: pathophysiology, treatment, and prognosis. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47:1393–1414.
  2. Nolan JP, Sandroni C, Böttiger BW, et al. European Resuscitation Council and European Society of Intensive Care Medicine Guidelines 2021: Post-resuscitation care. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47:369–421.

CBS_BIO_34 — Rapid Blood Culture Diagnostics and Antimicrobial Stewardship

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An ICU patient's blood cultures flag positive at 9 hours. Gram stain and MALDI-TOF identification become available progressively over the following hours.

TimeInformation available
9hCulture flags positive
~10hGram stain: Gram-negative rods
~12hMALDI-TOF: organism identified to species level
24–48hFull sensitivities available

Structured questions

  1. What does the time-to-positivity of a blood culture add clinically?
  2. What does rapid identification technology (MALDI-TOF) offer beyond conventional culture?
  3. How should you use the Gram stain result specifically?
  4. Outline your practical approach to antimicrobial adjustment across this timeline.
Reveal model answer
  • Shorter time-to-positivity generally correlates with higher bacterial burden/more severe infection — used as an adjunct to severity assessment, not a rigid diagnostic tool on its own.
  • MALDI-TOF mass spectrometry (and molecular/PCR-based rapid panels) can identify the organism to genus/species level within hours of a positive flag, well before traditional 24–48 hour culture/sensitivity results, enabling earlier targeted de-escalation or escalation.
  • The Gram stain (available ~1 hour after positivity) provides even earlier actionable information — Gram-negative vs Gram-positive — to refine empirical cover before final species identification/sensitivities are available.
  • Use each piece of information progressively — Gram stain, then rapid ID, then eventual sensitivities — to escalate/de-escalate/target therapy over the first 24–72 hours, rather than waiting for the complete conventional report to make any changes.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct time-to-positivity interpretation (2), correct MALDI-TOF value (3), correct Gram stain utility (3), correct progressive stewardship approach (4).

Common error / drift trap: Waiting for full conventional culture and sensitivity results before making any antimicrobial adjustments, despite earlier actionable information being available, represents a missed stewardship opportunity — each earlier data point should progressively refine therapy.

References

  1. Banerjee R, Özenci V, Patel R. Individualized Approaches Are Needed for Optimized Blood Cultures. Clin Infect Dis. 2016;63(10):1332–1339.
  2. Evans L, Rhodes A, Alhazzani W, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines 2021. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47:1181–1247.

CBS_BIO_35 — FENa vs FEUrea: The Diuretic Pitfall in AKI Classification

Biochemistry / Data Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with AKI has recently received loop diuretics. Urine and plasma electrolytes are sent to help distinguish pre-renal from intrinsic AKI.

ParameterResult
Urine sodium15 mmol/L
Plasma sodium138 mmol/L
Urine creatinine8000 µmol/L
Plasma creatinine250 µmol/L
Recent medicationIV furosemide

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the FENa and interpret it using standard thresholds.
  2. What limitation applies to FENa specifically in this patient?
  3. What index should be used instead, and why is it more robust here?
  4. What are the overall limitations of these indices?
Reveal model answer
  • FENa = (UNa × PCr)/(PNa × UCr) × 100 = (15×250)/(138×8000) × 100 ≈ 0.34% — a low value (<1%) that would normally suggest a pre-renal picture.
  • FENa is unreliable after diuretics — loop or thiazide diuretics cause natriuresis independent of renal perfusion status, which can falsely elevate FENa and mask a true pre-renal state (the opposite direction of error to what is seen here, but the result should still not be trusted given the recent diuretic).
  • Use FEUrea instead — urea handling is less affected by loop diuretics. FEUrea <35% suggests pre-renal; >50% suggests intrinsic/ATN, calculated the same way using urea in place of sodium.
  • Neither index is perfectly reliable — CKD, contrast nephropathy, some forms of glomerulonephritis, and the recovery phase of ATN can all cause false results. Use as adjuncts to the overall clinical assessment (history, examination, trend, other urinalysis findings), not as a standalone diagnostic test.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct FENa calculation (4), correct diuretic limitation (3), correct FEUrea substitution with thresholds (3), overall limitations (2).

Common error / drift trap: Calculating and relying on FENa in a patient who has recently received diuretics, without switching to FEUrea, is a common, recognised pitfall — diuretic- induced natriuresis can falsely elevate FENa into the "intrinsic AKI" range even when the process is truly pre-renal.

References

  1. Carvounis CP, Nisar S, Guro-Razuman S. Significance of the fractional excretion of urea in the differential diagnosis of acute renal failure. Kidney Int. 2002;62(6):2223–2229.
  2. Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury. Kidney Int Suppl. 2012;2:1–138.

CBS_BIO_36 — MELD-Na Score for Transplant Listing

Biochemistry / Data GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A cirrhotic patient is being assessed for liver transplant listing.

ParameterResult
Bilirubin45 µmol/L (2.6 mg/dL)
Creatinine150 µmol/L (1.7 mg/dL)
INR1.8
Sodium128 mmol/L

Structured questions

  1. What components make up MELD-Na, and why was sodium added to the original MELD formula?
  2. What is MELD-Na primarily used for?
  3. What are its limitations compared with Child-Pugh?
  4. Name two specific factors that can distort the MELD-Na calculation.
Reveal model answer
  • MELD-Na incorporates bilirubin, INR, creatinine, and sodium — sodium was added to the original MELD formula (bilirubin/INR/creatinine only) given its independent prognostic value, since hyponatraemia is common and prognostically significant in decompensated cirrhosis.
  • Primarily used for transplant organ allocation priority in most major systems — a higher score means higher priority, reflecting a higher estimated 90-day mortality risk without transplant.
  • Unlike Child-Pugh (which incorporates bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, and encephalopathy — more clinically holistic but more subjective), MELD-Na does not directly capture ascites or encephalopathy severity — a patient with severe refractory ascites/ encephalopathy but a modest MELD-Na may still have significant clinical burden not reflected in the score.
  • Distorting factors: anticoagulation (falsely raises INR), and renal replacement therapy (creatinine is capped/adjusted in the formula for patients on RRT); hepatocellular carcinoma also has a separate exception-point allocation pathway not captured by the raw score.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct components and rationale for sodium (4), correct primary use (2), correct Child-Pugh comparison/limitation (4), distorting factors named (2).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming MELD-Na captures overall clinical severity/quality of life in cirrhosis (including ascites and encephalopathy burden) is a misconception — unlike Child-Pugh, it does not directly incorporate these clinically important complications.

References

  1. Kim WR, Biggins SW, Kremers WK, et al. Hyponatremia and mortality among patients on the liver- transplant waiting list. N Engl J Med. 2008;359:1018–1026.

CBS_BIO_37 — Pseudothrombocytopenia vs True Immune Thrombocytopenia

Biochemistry / Data Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A routine FBC shows isolated severe thrombocytopenia in an otherwise well patient with no bleeding. Blood film is requested.

ParameterResult
Platelet count (EDTA tube)18 ×109/L
Blood filmPlatelet clumping seen
Clinical bleedingNone

Structured questions

  1. What does the blood film finding suggest, and what is the mechanism?
  2. How would you confirm this?
  3. How does this contrast with true ITP?
  4. Why does recognising this artefact matter clinically?
Reveal model answer
  • Pseudothrombocytopenia — an in-vitro artefact caused by EDTA-dependent platelet clumping, where antibodies cause platelets to aggregate in the EDTA-anticoagulated sample tube, leading the automated counter to undercount them. A common, benign, and frequently missed cause of an alarming isolated low platelet count with no clinical bleeding.
  • Confirm with a repeat count in a citrate or heparin anticoagulated tube (which doesn't promote the same clumping), or direct blood film examination showing platelet clumps — the true platelet count is normal.
  • True ITP shows isolated thrombocytopenia without clumping on film, with normal- appearing individual platelets, and clinical bruising/bleeding tendency correlating with the genuinely low count — a diagnosis of exclusion after ruling out other causes.
  • Recognising the artefact avoids unnecessary investigation (bone marrow biopsy), unnecessary treatment (steroids, IVIG), or inappropriate cancellation of necessary procedures/surgery based on a falsely low platelet count.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct identification/mechanism (4), correct confirmatory step (3), correct contrast with true ITP (3), correct clinical importance (2).

Common error / drift trap: Reflexively treating an isolated, asymptomatic low platelet count as ITP (or delaying necessary surgery because of it) without first excluding pseudothrombocytopenia via blood film review or a repeat citrate-tube count is a common, avoidable error.

References

  1. Lippi G, Plebani M. EDTA-dependent pseudothrombocytopenia: further insights and recommendations for prevention of a clinically threatening artifact. Clin Chem Lab Med. 2012;50(8):1281–1285.

CBS_BIO_38 — SIADH vs Cerebral Salt Wasting

Biochemistry / Data Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with aneurysmal SAH develops hyponatraemia. Biochemistry is sent to help classify the cause.

ParameterResult
Serum sodium124 mmol/L
Serum osmolality258 mOsm/kg
Urine osmolality420 mOsm/kg
Urine sodium65 mmol/L
Volume statusRequires clinical assessment

Structured questions

  1. List the biochemical similarities between SIADH and cerebral salt wasting (CSW). Can biochemistry alone distinguish them?
  2. What is the key discriminator, and what is the proposed mechanism of CSW?
  3. What happens if you fluid-restrict a CSW patient by mistake?
  4. What is the pragmatic approach to fluid management in this population given this diagnostic difficulty?
Reveal model answer
  • Both SIADH and CSW show hyponatraemia, low serum osmolality, inappropriately high urine osmolality, and high urine sodium — this biochemistry, as shown here, is essentially identical between the two and generally cannot reliably distinguish them alone.
  • The key discriminator is clinical volume status: SIADH patients are euvolaemic or mildly volume-expanded; CSW patients are volume-depleted despite natriuresis, from a presumed CNS-injury-mediated renal sodium-wasting process (mechanism debated, possibly involving brain natriuretic peptide-like factors).
  • Fluid-restricting a CSW patient (mistaking it for SIADH) — the opposite of what is needed — would worsen hypovolaemia and could precipitate cerebral ischaemia/vasospasm-related complications in a SAH patient already at vasospasm risk, a genuinely dangerous error.
  • Given the significant risk of incorrectly fluid-restricting a volume-depleted SAH patient, many neurocritical care units default to maintaining euvolaemia/mild positive fluid balance and avoiding fluid restriction in this population until the diagnosis is clarified, rather than restricting fluids empirically.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct recognition that biochemistry alone cannot distinguish (4), correct volume-status discriminator and CSW mechanism (3), correct danger of mistaken fluid restriction (3), correct pragmatic approach (2).

Common error / drift trap: SIADH and CSW have virtually identical routine biochemistry and are frequently confused, but require opposite management — the key differentiator is clinical volume status, not biochemistry alone, and incorrectly fluid-restricting a CSW patient risks worsening hypovolaemia and precipitating vasospasm-related cerebral ischaemia.

References

  1. Rahman M, Friedman WA. Hyponatremia in neurosurgical patients: cerebral salt wasting versus syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion. Neurosurgery. 2009;65(5):925–935.
  2. Hoh BL, Ko NU, Amin-Hanjani S, et al. 2023 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Aneurysmal Subarachnoid Hemorrhage. Stroke. 2023;54(7):e314–e370.

CBS_CRV_36 — Reverse Triggering on the Ventilator Waveform

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A deeply sedated patient with ARDS on volume-controlled ventilation shows a repetitive pattern on the pressure-time and flow-time waveforms: a passive-appearing breath is followed, at a fixed latency, by a small negative deflection and a second, smaller pressure spike — occurring in a fixed 1:1 or 1:2 ratio with the ventilator rate.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: pressure-time waveform showing reverse triggering — a mandatory breath followed by an entrained diaphragmatic contraction at fixed latency, producing an extra small pressure deflection (breath-stacking risk if it meets trigger threshold).

Structured questions

  1. What is reverse triggering, and by what proposed mechanism does it occur in deeply sedated patients with no apparent respiratory drive?
  2. Why is it clinically important to recognise, particularly in ARDS?
  3. What ventilator strategies can be used to manage it?
Reveal model answer
  • Definition/mechanism: reverse triggering is a form of patient-ventilator dyssynchrony in which the mandatory (ventilator-initiated) breath itself triggers a subsequent, entrained diaphragmatic contraction — the reverse of the usual sequence — thought to relate to a brainstem respiratory-pattern-generator entrainment phenomenon seen especially in deeply sedated patients; it can occur even without apparent volitional respiratory effort.
  • Clinical importance: if the entrained diaphragmatic contraction is strong enough and occurs during the ventilator's expiratory phase, it can trigger a second ("stacked") breath, delivering double the set tidal volume and driving pressure into the lung — a mechanism of patient self-inflicted/ventilator-induced lung injury that is particularly dangerous in ARDS, where lung-protective tidal volumes and driving pressures are essential; it also complicates neuromuscular blockade decisions, as it can occur even in apparently passive patients.
  • Management: deepening sedation may paradoxically worsen it in some patients (as entrainment can persist or intensify); options include adjusting ventilator rate/mode to move it out of phase with entrainment, neuromuscular blockade to eliminate the diaphragmatic contraction entirely (per the original ACURASYS-era evidence base for severe ARDS, contextualised by the more recent ROSE trial's neutral findings — reserved for selected severe cases with clear dyssynchrony/breath-stacking), or, where feasible, careful reduction of sedation to shift the patient toward a synchronous assisted mode instead.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct definition/mechanism (4), correct clinical significance including breath-stacking (3), correct management options (3).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming any extra waveform deflection in a deeply sedated patient represents an artefact or genuine volitional effort requiring more sedation. Reverse triggering is a distinct, under-recognised entity, and reflexively deepening sedation is not a reliable fix and may not address the underlying entrainment.

References

  1. Akoumianaki E, Lyazidi A, Rey N, et al. Mechanical ventilation-induced reverse-triggered breaths. Chest. 2013;143(4):927–938.
  2. National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute PETAL Clinical Trials Network (Moss M, et al.). Early neuromuscular blockade in ARDS (ROSE trial). N Engl J Med. 2019;380(21):1997–2008.

CBS_CRV_37 — Stress Index as a Bedside Marker of Tidal Recruitment/Overdistension

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with ARDS is ventilated in volume-controlled mode with constant flow. The airway pressure-time curve during the inspiratory phase is shown for two different PEEP settings.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: airway pressure-time curves during constant inspiratory flow, illustrating a concave-up curve (stress index <1, ongoing tidal recruitment) versus a concave-down curve (stress index >1, tidal overdistension).

Structured questions

  1. Describe how stress index is derived, and what a value of exactly 1.0 represents.
  2. What PEEP adjustment would you make in response to each curve shape shown?
  3. What are the practical limitations of this bedside tool?
Reveal model answer
  • Derivation: during volume-controlled ventilation with a constant (square-wave) inspiratory flow, the shape of the airway pressure-time curve reflects dynamic respiratory system compliance. The curve is fit to a power equation (Paw = a·t^b + c), and the exponent "b" is the stress index. A value of 1.0 indicates linear, constant compliance throughout inspiration (i.e., no ongoing recruitment or overdistension at the applied PEEP/tidal volume).
  • PEEP response: a concave-up curve (stress index <1, progressively increasing compliance) suggests tidal recruitment/derecruitment is occurring and more PEEP may be beneficial; a concave-down curve (stress index >1, progressively decreasing compliance) suggests tidal overdistension and PEEP should be reduced (or tidal volume reduced).
  • Limitations: requires constant-flow volume-controlled ventilation (not valid in pressure-controlled or spontaneous/assisted modes), is confounded by chest wall compliance abnormalities (obesity, abdominal compartment syndrome, chest wall oedema) which can mimic overdistension patterns, requires a fully passive patient (any respiratory effort distorts the curve), and has not been shown in large outcome trials to improve mortality when used to titrate PEEP — it remains a physiological/mechanistic bedside adjunct rather than a validated outcome-changing protocol.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct derivation and SI=1 meaning (4), correct PEEP response to both curve shapes (3), two or more correct limitations (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating stress index as a validated, mortality-reducing PEEP-titration protocol equivalent to ARDSNet low-PEEP/high-PEEP tables or the EPVent oesophageal pressure-guided approach. It remains a physiological bedside tool without dedicated large RCT mortality evidence and should complement, not replace, an evidence-based ventilation strategy.

References

  1. Grasso S, Terragni P, Mascia L, et al. Airway pressure-time curve profile (stress index) detects tidal recruitment/hyperinflation. Crit Care Med. 2004;32(4):1018–1027.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines: mechanical ventilation in ARDS. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_CRV_38 — Rapid Shallow Breathing Index Trend During a Spontaneous Breathing Trial

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient undergoes a 30-minute spontaneous breathing trial on a T-piece. Respiratory rate and tidal volume are recorded every 5 minutes, and the RSBI (f/Vt) trend is plotted.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: RSBI (f/Vt) trend during a 30-minute spontaneous breathing trial, showing a progressive rise from below to above the standard 105 breaths/min/L threshold.

Structured questions

  1. What threshold value of RSBI is traditionally used to predict extubation success, and what is its predictive performance?
  2. Why is a single early-trial RSBI value less informative than the trend shown here?
  3. What additional bedside assessments should accompany the RSBI before an extubation decision?
Reveal model answer
  • Threshold: RSBI <105 breaths/min/L is the classic threshold associated with a higher likelihood of extubation success (Yang and Tobin, 1991); however, its positive predictive value is only moderate, and it performs considerably better as a test to identify patients unlikely to succeed (high sensitivity for failure) than as a standalone confirmation of readiness — it should never be used in isolation to decide extubation.
  • Trend vs single value: a single measurement taken in the first few minutes of a trial may reflect transient anxiety/effort adjustment rather than sustainable respiratory mechanics; a rising RSBI trend across a full trial (as shown, progressing from <105 toward >105) indicates developing respiratory muscle fatigue and predicts a much higher likelihood of extubation failure than a single favourable early value would suggest.
  • Additional assessment: level of consciousness/ability to protect the airway, cough strength and secretion burden, cuff-leak test if prior traumatic/prolonged intubation raises concern for laryngeal oedema, haemodynamic stability, and overall clinical gestalt (work of breathing, accessory muscle use, diaphoresis) should all be assessed alongside the RSBI before extubation, consistent with contemporary weaning guidance emphasising a multi-parameter approach rather than a single index.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct threshold and predictive caveat (3), correct trend-vs-single-value reasoning (4), three or more additional assessments (3).

Common error / drift trap: Extubating based solely on a favourable RSBI measured in the first minute of a spontaneous breathing trial. The trend over the full trial duration, and a multi-parameter assessment, are what current weaning practice actually relies on — a single early number can be falsely reassuring.

References

  1. Yang KL, Tobin MJ. A prospective study of indexes predicting the outcome of trials of weaning from mechanical ventilation. N Engl J Med. 1991;324(21):1445–1450.
  2. Schmidt GA, Girard TD, Kress JP, et al. Official executive summary of an ATS/CHEST clinical practice guideline: liberation from mechanical ventilation in critically ill adults. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2017;195(1):115–119.

CBS_CRV_50 — Oesophageal Pressure Swings and Patient Self-Inflicted Lung Injury (P-SILI)

Curves / Waveforms Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A spontaneously breathing patient with moderate ARDS on high-flow nasal oxygen has an oesophageal balloon catheter placed to assess work of breathing. Large negative oesophageal pressure swings (ΔPoes of −20 cmH2O per breath) are recorded, well beyond the normal quiet-breathing range.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: oesophageal pressure tracing showing exaggerated negative deflections during spontaneous breathing, reflecting excessive inspiratory effort and risk of P-SILI.

Structured questions

  1. What is the proposed mechanism by which vigorous spontaneous effort can worsen lung injury in a patient not receiving invasive mechanical ventilation?
  2. What clinical and bedside signs, besides direct oesophageal manometry, suggest this is occurring?
  3. What are the evidence-based management options once P-SILI risk is recognised?
Reveal model answer
  • Mechanism: large negative pleural pressure swings generated by vigorous spontaneous effort increase transpulmonary pressure and can produce regional overdistension ("Pendelluft" — gas shifting from non-dependent to dependent lung regions without net inflation), increased pulmonary capillary transmural pressure promoting oedema, and patient-ventilator/patient- device asynchrony — cumulatively worsening lung injury through the same high-stress/high-strain mechanisms as ventilator-induced lung injury, but generated by the patient's own respiratory effort rather than the ventilator (hence "self-inflicted").
  • Bedside signs: visible accessory muscle use, tracheal tug, paradoxical abdominal motion, nasal flaring, tachypnoea with a high respiratory rate despite adequate oxygen delivery, and a swinging/thoraco-abdominal asynchrony pattern all suggest vigorous effort; rising respiratory rate combined with failure to improve oxygenation on high-flow/non-invasive support (the ROX index trending down) is also a recognised marker of impending P-SILI-driven deterioration warranting escalation.
  • Management: options include early recognition and escalation to invasive mechanical ventilation with controlled tidal volumes/driving pressure before overt respiratory failure develops (avoiding a prolonged trial of unsupported vigorous spontaneous breathing), judicious use of PEEP/positive pressure (CPAP or NIV) to reduce the required inspiratory effort, prone positioning in appropriately selected awake patients, and — where escalation to invasive ventilation occurs — early consideration of neuromuscular blockade or deep sedation in severe ARDS to eliminate the injurious effort, per the existing (if debated) evidence base discussed in earlier NMBA-related stations.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct P-SILI mechanism (4), two or more correct bedside signs (3), correct management options (3).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming that because a patient is "protected" from ventilator-induced lung injury simply by avoiding invasive mechanical ventilation, they cannot suffer significant iatrogenic lung injury. Vigorous, unsupported spontaneous effort in non-invasively supported patients with significant lung disease is an increasingly recognised and independently harmful mechanism requiring active monitoring and timely escalation.

References

  1. Brochard L, Slutsky A, Pesenti A. Mechanical ventilation to minimize progression of lung injury in acute respiratory failure. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2017;195(4):438–442.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines: mechanical ventilation in ARDS. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_CRV_39 — Digoxin Effect vs Digoxin Toxicity on the ECG

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An 80-year-old woman on long-term digoxin for atrial fibrillation presents with nausea, visual disturbance, and palpitations. Two ECG strips are shown: her routine outpatient ECG from last year, and today's rhythm strip.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: baseline "digoxin effect" (downsloping/scooped ST depression, unrelated to toxicity) versus today's bidirectional ventricular tachycardia, a near-pathognomonic sign of digoxin toxicity.

Structured questions

  1. Distinguish "digoxin effect" from digoxin toxicity — why does this distinction matter clinically?
  2. List three arrhythmias considered highly suggestive (though not exclusive) of digoxin toxicity.
  3. Outline the management of this presentation, including a specific antidote and dosing principle.
Reveal model answer
  • Effect vs toxicity: "digoxin effect" refers to the characteristic downsloping, scooped ("Salvador Dali moustache") ST-segment depression seen in many patients on therapeutic digoxin — it is a normal pharmacological finding, not evidence of toxicity, and should not by itself trigger toxicity management. Toxicity is a clinical and biochemical diagnosis (symptoms plus elevated level and/or characteristic arrhythmia), and conflating the two leads to either unnecessary treatment or, more dangerously, missed toxicity when the ECG is misread as "just digoxin effect."
  • Suggestive arrhythmias: bidirectional ventricular tachycardia (highly specific), atrial tachycardia with block (paroxysmal atrial tachycardia with 2:1 or variable AV block), and accelerated junctional rhythm — any combination of increased automaticity with impaired conduction is characteristically suggestive of digoxin toxicity.
  • Management: digoxin-specific antibody fragments (DigiFab) are the definitive antidote, indicated for life-threatening arrhythmias, severe bradycardia/heart block unresponsive to atropine, or hyperkalaemia (>5–5.5 mmol/L) in the setting of acute digoxin toxicity; dosing can be empirical (fixed dose for undefined ingestion amount) or calculated from the ingested dose/serum level using standard formulae. Correcting hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia (both potentiate toxicity) is also essential, while calcium administration for associated hyperkalaemia is traditionally avoided/given cautiously due to a historical (though contested) concern about precipitating "stone heart" — current toxicology teaching favours cautious calcium use only if truly life-threatening hyperkalaemia persists despite DigiFab.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct effect-vs-toxicity distinction (3), three correct arrhythmias (3), correct management including DigiFab (4).

Common error / drift trap: Interpreting scooped ST depression on a routine ECG as diagnostic of toxicity, or conversely dismissing a new bidirectional VT because "the patient looks well." Toxicity is defined by the clinical/biochemical picture, not by the presence of the classical but nonspecific ST-segment finding alone.

References

  1. Ma G, Brady WJ, Pollack M, Chan TC. Electrocardiographic manifestations: digitalis toxicity. J Emerg Med. 2001;20(2):145–152.
  2. Chan BS, Buckley NA. Digoxin-specific antibody fragments in the treatment of digoxin toxicity. Clin Toxicol (Phila). 2014;52(8):824–836.

CBS_CRV_40 — The ECG in Pulmonary Embolism: Why a Normal ECG Doesn't Exclude It

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old post-operative patient develops acute dyspnoea and pleuritic chest pain. Her ECG is shown.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: ECG showing the S1Q3T3 pattern (deep S wave lead I, Q wave and inverted T wave lead III), a classically taught but insensitive sign of right heart strain in acute PE.

Structured questions

  1. What is the most common ECG finding in confirmed acute pulmonary embolism, and how sensitive/ specific is the S1Q3T3 pattern?
  2. What ECG findings suggest a haemodynamically significant PE with right ventricular strain?
  3. What is the correct interpretation of a completely normal ECG in a patient with suspected PE?
Reveal model answer
  • Most common finding: sinus tachycardia is the single most common ECG abnormality in confirmed PE (present in a large proportion of cases), not S1Q3T3. The S1Q3T3 pattern is present in only around 10–20% of confirmed PE cases and is neither sensitive nor specific — it can occur in normal individuals and other conditions causing right heart strain.
  • Signs of RV strain/significant PE: new right bundle branch block (complete or incomplete), right axis deviation, T-wave inversion in the right precordial leads (V1–V4) and inferior leads (a finding correlating with RV strain severity), and the S1Q3T3 pattern together — the combination of precordial T-wave inversion and RBBB is more suggestive of significant right heart strain than any single sign.
  • Normal ECG interpretation: a normal ECG does not exclude PE — up to 30% or more of confirmed cases can have a normal or near-normal ECG, particularly smaller, haemodynamically insignificant emboli. ECG findings are used to support risk stratification (e.g., identifying higher-risk patients with RV strain who may need closer monitoring or escalation of therapy) rather than as a rule-out test; diagnosis and exclusion rely on validated clinical probability scores (Wells, Geneva) combined with D-dimer and/or CT pulmonary angiography.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct most-common-finding and S1Q3T3 sensitivity/specificity (4), three correct RV strain signs (3), correct normal-ECG interpretation (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating S1Q3T3 as either required for the diagnosis or, when absent, as evidence against PE. This is one of the most persistent misconceptions carried over from undergraduate teaching and is a frequent EDIC exam trap.

References

  1. Konstantinides SV, Meyer G, Becattini C, et al. 2019 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of acute pulmonary embolism. Eur Heart J. 2020;41(4):543–603.
  2. Shopp JD, Stewart LK, Emmett TW, Kline JA. Findings from 12-lead electrocardiography that predict circulatory shock from pulmonary embolism. Acad Emerg Med. 2015;22(10):1127–1137.

CBS_CRV_41 — ScvO2 Trend in Septic Shock: From Rivers to ProCESS/ARISE/ProMISe

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man is admitted with septic shock secondary to a urinary source. A central line ScvO2 trend over the first 6 hours of resuscitation, alongside lactate clearance, is plotted.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: ScvO2 rising above 70% in parallel with lactate clearance during the first 6 hours of resuscitation.

Structured questions

  1. Rivers et al. (2001) used ScvO2 ≥70% as a resuscitation target within early goal-directed therapy. What did the subsequent large multicentre trials find, and how has this changed practice?
  2. Given this, what is the current evidence-based role of ScvO2 monitoring in septic shock?
  3. What is a key confounder that can produce a falsely reassuring ScvO2 value?
Reveal model answer
  • Subsequent trials: the ProCESS (2014), ARISE (2014), and ProMISe (2015) trials — each large, multicentre, and pragmatic — found that protocolised early goal-directed therapy targeting ScvO2 (and central venous pressure) conferred no mortality benefit over usual care guided by clinical assessment and serial lactate, provided that early recognition, prompt antibiotics, and adequate fluid resuscitation still occurred. This led to protocolised ScvO2-targeted resuscitation being dropped as a mandatory element of the Surviving Sepsis Campaign bundles.
  • Current role: the Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021 guideline no longer mandates ScvO2 as a required resuscitation target; lactate clearance/normalisation (trend, not a single value) is the more consistently recommended and evidence-supported perfusion marker, alongside capillary refill time (per the ANDROMEDA-SHOCK-informed 2021 guidance) and clinical assessment. ScvO2 may still be used as an adjunctive physiological data point (e.g., to help distinguish causes of shock or to assess DO2/VO2 balance) but is no longer a mandated protocol target.
  • Key confounder: a falsely "normal" or elevated ScvO2 can occur despite ongoing tissue hypoperfusion in states of impaired oxygen extraction/utilisation (mitochondrial dysfunction in sepsis, arteriovenous shunting), meaning a reassuring ScvO2 does not guarantee adequate tissue oxygenation — this is a recognised limitation of relying on it in isolation.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct trial findings and practice change (4), correct current role/guideline position (4), correct confounder (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Citing Rivers' 2001 EGDT protocol as current standard-of-care without acknowledging that ProCESS/ARISE/ProMISe overturned mandatory ScvO2-targeted resuscitation, and that the Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021 guideline (Evans L, et al. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(11):1181–1247) reflects this. This is one of the most heavily tested "guideline drift" topics in EDIC.

References

  1. ProCESS Investigators. A randomized trial of protocol-based care for early septic shock. N Engl J Med. 2014;370(18):1683–1693.
  2. Evans L, Rhodes A, Alhazzani W, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2021. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(11):1181–1247.

CBS_CRV_42 — Pulse Pressure Variation: Validity Criteria and Pitfalls

Curves / Waveforms Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An arterial line waveform with a calculated pulse pressure variation (PPV) of 18% is used to justify a fluid bolus in a mechanically ventilated patient with hypotension. The waveform is shown alongside the ventilator settings (tidal volume 5mL/kg predicted body weight, patient making spontaneous triggering efforts, irregular rhythm noted on monitor).

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: arterial waveform with beat-to-beat pulse pressure variation used to derive PPV.

Structured questions

  1. List the validity criteria that must be met for PPV to reliably predict fluid responsiveness.
  2. Which criteria are violated in this vignette, and what is the practical implication?
  3. What are two dynamic alternatives that remain valid in a spontaneously breathing or low-tidal-volume patient?
Reveal model answer
  • Validity criteria: fully passive mechanical ventilation (no spontaneous triggering effort), regular cardiac rhythm (sinus, no significant arrhythmia), tidal volume ≥8mL/kg predicted body weight (lower tidal volumes reduce the cyclic intrathoracic pressure swing needed to generate a reliably interpretable PPV signal), closed chest, and absence of right ventricular failure/severe intra-abdominal hypertension.
  • Violations here: the patient is making spontaneous triggering efforts (invalidates PPV — spontaneous effort introduces variable intrathoracic pressure swings unrelated to the fixed mechanical breath), the tidal volume is only 5mL/kg PBW (below the ≥8mL/kg threshold, reducing reliability even if fully passive), and an irregular rhythm is noted (further invalidates the beat-to- beat pressure comparison). Practically, this PPV value cannot be relied upon to predict fluid responsiveness, and the fluid bolus decision should not be based on it — especially given that lung-protective ventilation (correctly used here at 5mL/kg PBW for ARDS) inherently and increasingly conflicts with the older assumption that PPV always requires high tidal volumes to be valid, meaning many contemporary ARDS patients simply cannot be reliably assessed with PPV.
  • Alternatives: passive leg raise combined with real-time cardiac output/stroke volume monitoring (valid regardless of ventilation mode, spontaneous breathing, or arrhythmia), and end-expiratory occlusion test (valid in ventilated patients making some spontaneous effort, does not require large tidal volumes) are both reasonable dynamic alternatives in this scenario.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct full validity criteria list (4), correct identification of violations and implication (3), two correct valid alternatives (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Using PPV uncritically in modern ARDS patients ventilated with protective low tidal volumes (as current 2023 ESICM ARDS guidance mandates) — PPV's validity was established using older, higher tidal volume ventilation strategies, and this conflict is increasingly relevant and increasingly tested.

References

  1. Michard F, Boussat S, Chemla D, et al. Relation between respiratory changes in arterial pulse pressure and fluid responsiveness. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2000;162(1):134–138.
  2. Monnet X, Marik PE, Teboul JL. Prediction of fluid responsiveness: an update. Ann Intensive Care. 2016;6:111.

CBS_CRV_43 — ICP Waveform Morphology: The P2 > P1 Sign

Curves / Waveforms Neurological CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with severe traumatic brain injury has an intraparenchymal ICP monitor in situ. The ICP pulse waveform morphology changes over 24 hours, shown alongside a rising mean ICP.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: ICP pulse waveform showing the normal triphasic pattern (P1>P2>P3) evolving to a pattern with P2 exceeding P1, indicating reduced intracranial compliance.

Structured questions

  1. What do the P1, P2, and P3 components of the ICP pulse waveform represent physiologically?
  2. What does a rising P2 relative to P1 indicate, and why does this precede a rise in mean ICP?
  3. What is the practical/clinical utility, and what is the key limitation, of relying on pulse waveform morphology at the bedside?
Reveal model answer
  • Components: P1 ("percussion wave") reflects arterial pulsation transmitted from choroid plexus/arterial pulsatility; P2 ("tidal wave") reflects intracranial compliance/the brain's viscoelastic response to the arterial pulse; P3 ("dicrotic wave") corresponds to aortic valve closure. Normally P1 > P2 > P3 in amplitude, reflecting normal intracranial compliance.
  • P2>P1 significance: as intracranial compliance falls (the brain moves up the steep, decompensated portion of the pressure-volume curve), the elastic/tidal component (P2) progressively increases relative to P1; this waveform change can precede a measurable rise in mean ICP, functioning as an early warning sign of deteriorating compliance before overt intracranial hypertension is captured by the mean value alone.
  • Utility and limitation: pulse waveform morphology provides continuous, real-time insight into compliance reserve that complements the single mean ICP number, and can prompt earlier intervention or closer monitoring; however, waveform interpretation is technically demanding, susceptible to artefact (patient movement, monitor drift/damping, transducer positioning), and there is no fully standardised, universally validated automated algorithm in routine clinical use — it remains a supportive, expert-interpreted adjunct rather than a stand-alone decision-making tool, and should never replace assessment of the mean ICP, CPP, and overall clinical trajectory.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct P1/P2/P3 physiology (4), correct P2>P1 significance and timing relative to mean ICP rise (3), correct utility/limitation (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating pulse waveform morphology as a fully automated, validated, stand-alone monitoring modality equivalent to mean ICP/CPP. It remains an adjunctive, expert-interpreted signal, not a replacement for standard ICP/CPP-directed management per the Brain Trauma Foundation guidelines.

References

  1. Cardoso ER, Rowan JO, Galbraith S. Analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid pulse wave in intracranial pressure. J Neurosurg. 1983;59(5):817–821.
  2. Carney N, Totten AM, O'Reilly C, et al. Guidelines for the management of severe traumatic brain injury, 4th edition. Neurosurgery. 2017;80(1):6–15.

CBS_CRV_44 — Pupillometry: Neurological Pupil Index Trend

Curves / Waveforms Neurological CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·1 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with a large middle cerebral artery infarct is monitored with serial automated pupillometry. The Neurological Pupil Index (NPi) trend over 12 hours for the affected (right) pupil is shown, alongside the unaffected (left) pupil.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: automated pupillometry NPi trend showing progressive decline in the affected-side pupil below the abnormal threshold, while the contralateral pupil remains normal.

Structured questions

  1. What does the Neurological Pupil Index measure, and what threshold is typically used to define an abnormal result?
  2. What is the clinical significance of a falling trend, as shown here, compared with manual penlight pupil assessment?
  3. What action should this trend prompt in a patient with a large hemispheric infarct?
Reveal model answer
  • NPi: a standardised, automated, algorithm-derived composite score (typically 0–5) from infrared pupillometry incorporating pupil size, constriction velocity, and latency in response to a calibrated light stimulus — designed to remove the substantial inter-observer variability inherent to manual penlight assessment. An NPi below approximately 3 is generally considered abnormal, correlating with impaired parasympathetic (cranial nerve III) function.
  • Trend significance: a progressively falling NPi on the affected side, especially preceding any obvious clinical change in pupil size or reactivity to a manual light source, provides earlier and more objective evidence of evolving mass effect/uncal or transtentorial herniation risk than manual assessment — automated pupillometry has demonstrated better sensitivity and reproducibility for detecting subtle, evolving changes than subjective bedside examination, particularly in the hands of less experienced examiners.
  • Action: this trend, in a patient with a large hemispheric infarct at risk of malignant oedema, should prompt urgent reassessment (repeat neurological exam, urgent CT to assess midline shift/herniation), escalation of osmotherapy if indicated, and early discussion of decompressive hemicraniectomy where appropriate (per trial evidence in patients under ~60 with malignant MCA infarction, e.g., DESTINY II) — it should not be dismissed as an isolated monitoring artefact given the high pre-test probability of evolving herniation in this clinical context.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct NPi definition/threshold (3), correct trend significance vs manual assessment (4), correct action (3).

Common error / drift trap: Waiting for an overtly dilated, unreactive pupil on manual bedside exam before escalating care. A falling NPi trend on automated pupillometry can precede this by hours and is an actionable early-warning signal in patients at risk of herniation.

References

  1. Couret D, Boumaza D, Grisotto C, et al. Reliability of standard pupillometry practice in neurocritical care. Crit Care. 2016;20:99.
  2. Jauch-Chara K, Kistermann J, Sopalla S, et al. Neurological pupil index for early detection of clinically relevant pupillary changes. Neurocrit Care. 2019;31(2):268–276.

CBS_CRV_45 — Capillary Refill Time Trend: Bayesian Response Guides Titration

Curves / Waveforms Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with septic shock has serial capillary refill time (CRT) recorded every 30 minutes during resuscitation, plotted alongside cumulative fluid volume administered.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: capillary refill time normalising over 3 hours of guided fluid resuscitation, used as a real-time bedside titration endpoint.

Structured questions

  1. What did the ANDROMEDA-SHOCK trial compare, and what were its headline findings?
  2. How should ANDROMEDA-SHOCK's findings be correctly represented — did CRT-guided resuscitation "prove superior" to lactate-guided resuscitation?
  3. What practical advantages make CRT an attractive bedside perfusion target regardless of the exact mortality-benefit interpretation?
Reveal model answer
  • ANDROMEDA-SHOCK (Hernández et al., JAMA 2019): a randomised trial comparing peripheral perfusion (capillary refill time)-targeted resuscitation versus serum lactate-targeted resuscitation in early septic shock. The primary outcome (28-day mortality) showed a numerically lower mortality in the CRT-targeted group (34.9% vs 43.4%) but this did not reach conventional statistical significance in the frequentist primary analysis (p=0.06).
  • Correct representation: the trial is frequently over-stated as having "proven" CRT superior. The pre-specified frequentist primary analysis was not statistically significant; a subsequent Bayesian re-analysis of the same data (Zampieri et al., Am J Respir Crit Care Med 2020) suggested a high probability that CRT-targeted resuscitation was either superior to or at least not worse than lactate-targeted resuscitation, and this Bayesian analysis is what much of current practice draws on. The accurate exam answer acknowledges both the neutral frequentist result and the favourable-leaning Bayesian reanalysis, rather than overstating either.
  • Practical advantages: CRT is immediate (no laboratory turnaround delay, unlike lactate), free, repeatable at the bedside every few minutes, avoids the confounders that affect lactate interpretation (e.g., reduced clearance in liver dysfunction, beta-agonist-driven aerobic glycolysis, epinephrine infusions), and the Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021 guideline gives it a weak/conditional recommendation as a reasonable adjunct to guide resuscitation alongside lactate, reflecting this nuanced evidence base rather than declaring it definitively superior.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct trial description/design (3), correct nuanced frequentist-vs-Bayesian interpretation (5), two correct practical advantages (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Stating flatly that "ANDROMEDA-SHOCK proved CRT-guided resuscitation reduces mortality." The primary frequentist analysis was not statistically significant; overstating this trial's conclusiveness is a commonly tested nuance-detection trap distinguishing candidates who have only read an abstract from those who understand the actual statistical result.

References

  1. Hernández G, Ospina-Tascón GA, Damiani LP, et al. Effect of a resuscitation strategy targeting peripheral perfusion status vs serum lactate levels (ANDROMEDA-SHOCK). JAMA. 2019;321(7):654–664.
  2. Zampieri FG, Damiani LP, Bakker J, et al. Effects of a resuscitation strategy targeting peripheral perfusion status: a Bayesian reanalysis of ANDROMEDA-SHOCK. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2020;201(4):423–429.

CBS_CRV_46 — Core-Peripheral Temperature Gradient in Shock

Curves / Waveforms Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with septic shock on noradrenaline has core (oesophageal) and peripheral (great toe) temperature continuously recorded. The gradient trend over 8 hours is plotted alongside noradrenaline dose.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: narrowing core-to-peripheral temperature gradient over 8 hours, paralleling successful de-escalation of vasopressor support.

Structured questions

  1. What does a widened core-peripheral temperature gradient signify physiologically?
  2. How does the trend in this gradient compare, in terms of evidence and practicality, with capillary refill time as a perfusion marker?
  3. What non-perfusion factors can confound this measurement?
Reveal model answer
  • Physiology: a widened core-peripheral gradient reflects peripheral vasoconstriction diverting blood flow away from the skin/extremities to preserve perfusion to vital organs — a compensatory sympathetic/vasopressor-driven response to inadequate cardiac output or excessive vasopressor dosing, and a narrowing gradient with clinical improvement suggests restoration of adequate peripheral perfusion.
  • Comparison with CRT: both are peripheral perfusion markers reflecting similar underlying physiology (skin/peripheral vasoconstriction), but capillary refill time has a considerably larger and more direct evidence base in septic shock resuscitation (notably ANDROMEDA-SHOCK) and requires no specialised equipment, whereas continuous core-peripheral gradient monitoring requires dedicated temperature probes and lacks equivalent outcome-trial evidence directly linking it to resuscitation targets — it remains a physiologically plausible, continuously trackable adjunct rather than a primary validated resuscitation target.
  • Confounders: ambient temperature, peripheral vascular disease, local vasodilators/warming devices, and the specific vasopressor used (agents with differing alpha-1 vs beta-2 receptor activity affect peripheral vascular tone independently of central haemodynamic status) can all alter the gradient independent of true systemic perfusion status.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct physiology (3), correct comparative evidence discussion (4), two or more correct confounders (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating core-peripheral gradient monitoring as an equally validated substitute for capillary refill time in guiding resuscitation. It is a plausible continuous physiological signal, but lacks the specific randomised outcome-trial evidence base that CRT now has.

References

  1. Lima A, Bakker J. Noninvasive monitoring of peripheral perfusion. Intensive Care Med. 2005;31(10):1316–1326.
  2. Evans L, Rhodes A, Alhazzani W, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(11):1181–1247.

CBS_CRV_47 — Renal Resistive Index Trend: Prognostic, Not (Yet) Treatment-Guiding

Curves / Waveforms Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with septic AKI has serial renal Doppler ultrasound performed, tracking the renal resistive index (RI) over the ICU admission alongside creatinine.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: intrarenal Doppler waveform used to calculate the renal resistive index, showing reduced end-diastolic flow consistent with elevated RI.

Structured questions

  1. How is renal resistive index calculated, and what threshold is generally associated with increased risk of persistent/worsening AKI?
  2. What is the key evidentiary limitation regarding using RI to guide specific interventions (e.g., fluid administration, vasopressor titration)?
  3. What is the appropriate, evidence-consistent clinical use of RI at present?
Reveal model answer
  • Calculation/threshold: RI = (peak systolic velocity − end-diastolic velocity) / peak systolic velocity, measured on intrarenal Doppler ultrasound. An RI >0.7–0.74 has been associated in several observational studies with a higher risk of persistent or worsening AKI and failure of renal recovery.
  • Key limitation: the association between elevated RI and AKI outcome is correlative, drawn largely from observational cohort studies — it has not been shown in adequately powered randomised trials that titrating fluids or vasopressors specifically to a target RI improves renal or patient-centred outcomes; RI is influenced by numerous factors besides renal perfusion (intra-abdominal pressure, vasopressor dose/type, heart rate, arterial compliance, positive-pressure ventilation), which complicates its use as a direct causal guide to therapy.
  • Appropriate use: RI is best regarded as a prognostic/risk-stratification tool (identifying patients at higher risk of AKI progression or non-recovery who may warrant closer monitoring or earlier nephrology involvement) rather than a validated treatment-titration target; management decisions (fluid balance, vasopressor choice, renal replacement therapy timing) should continue to be guided by the broader clinical and biochemical picture, consistent with current KDIGO AKI guidance, not by RI alone.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct calculation/threshold (3), correct evidentiary limitation (correlation vs causation) (4), correct appropriate use (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Using RI as a direct fluid-titration target ("RI is high, so give more fluid" or "give less fluid") without acknowledging that this represents an unproven extrapolation beyond the prognostic observational evidence base — association is not causation, and this is explicitly the kind of evidentiary overreach the exam tests for.

References

  1. Darmon M, Bourenne J, Hruska K, et al. Diagnostic performance of Doppler renal resistive index for predicting acute kidney injury. Intensive Care Med. 2011;37(1):68–76.
  2. Kellum JA, Lameire N; KDIGO AKI Guideline Work Group. Diagnosis, evaluation, and management of acute kidney injury: a KDIGO summary. Crit Care. 2013;17(1):204 (with subsequent KDIGO updates through 2024 unchanged on this point).

CBS_CRV_48 — Peritoneal Dialysis Effluent Cell Count Trend in Suspected Peritonitis

Curves / Waveforms Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 48-year-old woman on chronic peritoneal dialysis (PD) presents with cloudy effluent and abdominal pain. Serial effluent white cell count and percentage neutrophils are trended over 48 hours of empirical intraperitoneal antibiotics.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: PD effluent white cell count falling below the diagnostic threshold over 48 hours of treatment, indicating response to therapy.

Structured questions

  1. What effluent white cell count and differential defines PD-associated peritonitis?
  2. What is the recommended time frame to expect clinical/effluent improvement, and what does failure to trend down suggest?
  3. Under what circumstances is catheter removal mandatory rather than optional?
Reveal model answer
  • Diagnostic threshold: per the International Society for Peritoneal Dialysis (ISPD) 2022 guideline update, PD-associated peritonitis is defined by an effluent white cell count >100 cells/µL (after a dwell of at least 2 hours) with >50% polymorphonuclear cells, combined with cloudy effluent and/or abdominal pain.
  • Expected trend and failure implications: effluent cell counts should show a clear downward trend within the first 3–5 days of appropriate empirical/targeted antibiotics; failure to improve by day 5, or a WBC count that remains elevated or rises after initial response, should prompt reassessment for resistant/atypical organisms (including fungal peritonitis), an underlying abscess or catheter-related infection, or the need for imaging and early surgical/nephrology consultation.
  • Mandatory catheter removal: per ISPD 2022 guidance, catheter removal is mandatory (not optional) for fungal peritonitis (immediate removal on diagnosis, given very high mortality if the catheter is retained), refractory peritonitis (failure to respond after 5 days of appropriate antibiotics), relapsing peritonitis, and repeat peritonitis with the same organism — delaying removal in fungal peritonitis specifically is associated with markedly worse outcomes and is a frequently tested point.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct diagnostic threshold (3), correct expected trend/failure implications (3), correct mandatory removal criteria with emphasis on fungal peritonitis (4).

Guideline-drift trap: Attempting prolonged antifungal therapy with catheter retention in suspected/confirmed fungal PD peritonitis. Current ISPD guidance (Li PK, et al. Perit Dial Int. 2022;42(2):110–153) is explicit that fungal peritonitis requires prompt catheter removal — this is not a "try antifungals first" scenario.

References

  1. Li PK, Chow KM, Cho Y, et al. ISPD peritonitis guideline recommendations: 2022 update on prevention and treatment. Perit Dial Int. 2022;42(2):110–153.

CBS_CRV_49 — Continuous Glucose Monitoring and the Van den Berghe-to-NICE-SUGAR Shift

Curves / Waveforms Endocrine / Metabolic CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 10 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A critically ill patient has continuous subcutaneous glucose monitoring in place alongside an insulin infusion. The glucose trend over 24 hours is shown, with a target range band overlaid.

Fig. 1 — Original schematic diagram: continuous glucose monitor trend converging toward a moderate (not tight) glycaemic target band over 24 hours of insulin infusion.

Structured questions

  1. Summarise the Van den Berghe (2001) intensive insulin therapy trial and its original glucose target.
  2. What did the subsequent NICE-SUGAR trial find, and how did this change the accepted glucose target range in critical illness?
  3. What is the emerging evidence and rationale for continuous glucose monitoring in this context?
Reveal model answer
  • Van den Berghe 2001: a single-centre surgical ICU RCT showing that intensive insulin therapy targeting tight glucose control (4.4–6.1 mmol/L) reduced mortality compared with conventional control (target <11.9 mmol/L), generating widespread adoption of tight glycaemic control protocols in the following years.
  • NICE-SUGAR (2009): a much larger, multicentre, international RCT found that intensive glucose control (target 4.5–6.0 mmol/L) was associated with significantly increased 90-day mortality compared with conventional control (target <10 mmol/L), driven substantially by an increased rate of severe hypoglycaemia in the intensive-control arm. This overturned tight glycaemic control as standard practice; current guidance (including Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021 and general critical care consensus) recommends a moderate target of approximately 7.8–10 mmol/L, initiating insulin therapy for persistent hyperglycaemia above this range while avoiding hypoglycaemia.
  • CGM rationale: continuous glucose monitoring offers more frequent, near real-time glucose data than intermittent point-of-care testing, with growing observational and some randomised evidence (e.g., in the ICU setting) suggesting it can reduce hypoglycaemic events and glucose variability, and may improve time-in-target-range without increasing hypoglycaemia risk; however, CGM accuracy in critically ill patients (oedema, vasopressors, hypoperfusion affecting interstitial-to-blood glucose correlation) remains an active area of validation, and current evidence supports it as a promising adjunct rather than a fully established replacement for point-of-care glucose testing in all patients.
Marking guide

10 marks: correct Van den Berghe summary (3), correct NICE-SUGAR findings and practice change (4), correct balanced CGM evidence summary (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Citing Van den Berghe's tight glycaemic targets (4.4–6.1 mmol/L) as current standard practice. NICE-SUGAR (Finfer S, et al. N Engl J Med. 2009;360(13):1283–1297) is one of the most classic "guideline drift" trials in intensive care and is heavily tested — a candidate citing only Van den Berghe without NICE-SUGAR is demonstrating a significant knowledge gap.

References

  1. Van den Berghe G, Wouters P, Weekers F, et al. Intensive insulin therapy in critically ill patients. N Engl J Med. 2001;345(19):1359–1367.
  2. NICE-SUGAR Study Investigators (Finfer S, et al.). Intensive versus conventional glucose control in critically ill patients. N Engl J Med. 2009;360(13):1283–1297.

CBS_BIO_01 — High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis: Delta Ratio and Compensation

Biochemistry / Data Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 22-year-old woman with known type 1 diabetes is brought to the emergency department drowsy, tachypnoeic, and clinically dehydrated after 3 days of vomiting and reduced insulin intake during a viral illness.

ParameterResultReference range
pH7.127.35–7.45
PaCO22.9 kPa (22 mmHg)4.7–6.0 kPa (35–45 mmHg)
PaO213.8 kPa (104 mmHg) on air11–13 kPa
Bicarbonate (actual)8 mmol/L22–26 mmol/L
Sodium138 mmol/L135–145 mmol/L
Potassium5.6 mmol/L3.5–5.0 mmol/L
Chloride100 mmol/L98–106 mmol/L
Glucose32 mmol/L (576 mg/dL)4–7.8 mmol/L
Capillary/serum ketones5.8 mmol/L<0.6 mmol/L
Lactate1.3 mmol/L0.5–2.0 mmol/L
Measured osmolality305 mOsm/kg275–295 mOsm/kg
Urea10.2 mmol/L2.5–7.1 mmol/L
Creatinine118 µmol/L45–90 µmol/L

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this arterial blood gas systematically, stating the primary disturbance and whether compensation is appropriate.
  2. Calculate the anion gap and the delta ratio. What does the delta ratio tell you here?
  3. What is the diagnosis, and how would you distinguish it from hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state (HHS)?
  4. Outline your immediate management priorities for the first 60 minutes.
Reveal model answer

1. Systematic ABG interpretation

  • pH 7.12 → severe acidaemia.
  • Bicarbonate markedly low (8 mmol/L) with low PaCO2 (22 mmHg) → primary metabolic acidosis with respiratory compensation.
  • Check appropriateness of compensation using Winter's formula: expected PaCO2 (mmHg) = 1.5 × [HCO3−] + 8 ± 2 = 1.5 × 8 + 8 ± 2 = 20 ± 2 mmHg (range 18–22 mmHg). Measured PaCO2 of 22 mmHg falls within this range, i.e. respiratory compensation is appropriate — there is no independent respiratory disorder to explain further on top of the expected compensation.

2. Anion gap and delta ratio

  • Anion gap = Na+ − (Cl− + HCO3−) = 138 − (100 + 8) = 30 mmol/L (markedly elevated; normal ≈8–12 mmol/L) → high anion gap metabolic acidosis, consistent with ketoacidosis given the markedly raised ketones.
  • Delta ratio = (anion gap − 12) / (24 − HCO3−) = (30 − 12) / (24 − 8) = 18/16 ≈ 1.1.
  • A delta ratio between roughly 1 and 2 indicates a pure high anion gap metabolic acidosis with no significant concurrent normal anion gap (hyperchloraemic) process and no significant concurrent metabolic alkalosis. (A ratio <1 would suggest a co-existing normal anion gap acidosis; a ratio >2 would suggest a co-existing metabolic alkalosis or pre-existing elevated bicarbonate.)

3. Diagnosis and distinction from HHS

  • Diagnosis: diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) — hyperglycaemia, raised ketones, high anion gap metabolic acidosis with pH <7.3 and bicarbonate <15 mmol/L, in a patient with reduced insulin intake during intercurrent illness.
  • Key distinguishing features from HHS: DKA typically has significant ketosis/acidosis (as here) and glucose is often (though not always) lower than in HHS; HHS is characterised by profound hyperglycaemia (often >30 mmol/L, frequently much higher), markedly raised osmolality, minimal ketosis/acidosis (pH usually >7.3, bicarbonate usually >15 mmol/L), and a more insidious onset, typically in older patients with type 2 diabetes. This patient's significant ketosis and acidaemia clearly place her in DKA rather than HHS, even though her glucose and osmolality are also elevated — the two conditions exist on a spectrum and can overlap.
  • Calculate corrected sodium for the degree of hyperglycaemia (add ~2–2.4 mmol/L to measured sodium for every 5.5 mmol/L glucose above normal) — relevant to true free-water deficit assessment and to avoid missing a falsely low/normal sodium.

4. Immediate management (first 60 minutes)

  • IV fluid resuscitation with isotonic crystalloid (e.g. 0.9% saline), assessing haemodynamic status first — she is clinically dehydrated and drowsy, so cautious but prompt volume resuscitation is required.
  • Fixed-rate IV insulin infusion (e.g. 0.1 units/kg/hr) — do not withhold insulin despite the elevated potassium; insulin is essential to switch off ketogenesis.
  • Potassium monitoring and early replacement plan: although potassium is currently 5.6 mmol/L, total body potassium is depleted and insulin therapy will drive potassium intracellularly — plan to add potassium replacement to IV fluids once potassium falls below ~5.5 mmol/L, with frequent re-checks (hourly initially).
  • Do not give bicarbonate routinely — reserve for extreme, life-threatening acidaemia (e.g. pH <6.9) with senior/consultant input, as routine bicarbonate has not been shown to improve outcomes and may cause paradoxical CNS acidosis and worsen hypokalaemia.
  • Identify and treat the precipitant (here, likely intercurrent viral illness plus reduced insulin dosing) — septic screen if indicated, avoid missing a co-existing infective trigger.
  • Monitor for cerebral oedema risk (more relevant in younger patients/adolescents) with a cautious, gradual correction strategy; hourly neuro-observations, glucose, and venous gas/ketone monitoring per DKA protocol (e.g. JBDS-IP) until resolution criteria are met (ketones <0.6 mmol/L, venous pH >7.3, bicarbonate >18 mmol/L).
Marking guide

Award up to 12 marks: correct primary disturbance and compensation assessment with Winter's formula (2), correct anion gap calculation (1), correct delta ratio calculation and interpretation (2), correct diagnosis of DKA with justification (2), correct differentiation from HHS (2), fluids/insulin/potassium management (2), correct bicarbonate caveat (1).

Common error / drift trap: Candidates often withhold insulin because the potassium is "high" — this is incorrect; insulin is required to resolve ketoacidosis and hyperkalaemia here reflects a transcellular shift, not true total-body excess. Equally common is reflexive administration of bicarbonate for a low pH without applying the specific, narrow threshold (pH <6.9) supported by current DKA guidelines.

References

  1. Joint British Diabetes Societies for Inpatient Care (JBDS-IP). The Management of Diabetic Ketoacidosis in Adults. Available at: abcd.care/jbds
  2. Kitabchi AE, Umpierrez GE, Miles JM, Fisher JN. Hyperglycemic crises in adult patients with diabetes. Diabetes Care. 2009;32(7):1335–1343.
  3. American Diabetes Association. Hyperglycemic Crises in Adults with Diabetes: A Consensus Report. Diabetes Care. 2024;47(8):1257–1275.
  4. Berend K, de Vries AP, Gans RO. Physiological approach to assessment of acid–base disturbances. N Engl J Med. 2014;371:1434–1445.

CBS_BIO_02 — A Deceptively Normal pH: Triple Acid–Base Disorder

Biochemistry / Data Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old man with known COPD is admitted with septic shock secondary to a chest infection, complicated by several days of severe vomiting from associated ileus.

ParameterResultReference range
pH7.387.35–7.45
PaCO27.3 kPa (55 mmHg)4.7–6.0 kPa (35–45 mmHg)
Bicarbonate32 mmol/L22–26 mmol/L
Sodium140 mmol/L135–145 mmol/L
Potassium3.2 mmol/L3.5–5.0 mmol/L
Chloride85 mmol/L98–106 mmol/L
Lactate4.5 mmol/L0.5–2.0 mmol/L
Known baseline (from prior notes)PaCO2 ~6.0 kPa (45 mmHg), HCO3 27 mmol/L—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this gas at face value. Is anything obviously abnormal?
  2. Calculate the anion gap and delta ratio/delta-delta. What do they reveal?
  3. Using his known baseline, assess whether the bicarbonate is appropriate for chronic respiratory compensation. What does this tell you?
  4. Name all primary disorders present and explain why the pH looks deceptively normal.
Reveal model answer
  • At face value, the pH is near-normal (7.38), which can superficially suggest no significant acid-base disturbance — this is the central trap of this case.
  • Anion gap = Na − (Cl + HCO3) = 140 − (85 + 32) = 23 mmol/L (elevated; normal ≈8–12). Delta ratio = (23 − 12)/(32 − 24) ≈ 11/8 ≈ 1.4 — on its own this would suggest a fairly pure high anion gap acidosis, but this calculation is being distorted by the unusually high starting bicarbonate, so it must be interpreted alongside the compensation check below rather than in isolation.
  • Using his known chronic baseline (PaCO2 ~45 mmHg, HCO3 27 mmol/L, reflecting long-standing compensated COPD), expected further bicarbonate rise for his current PaCO2 of 55 mmHg (a 10 mmHg further rise) using the chronic compensation rule (~3.5 mmol/L HCO3 per 10 mmHg chronic PaCO2 rise) gives an expected HCO3 of about 27 + 3.5 ≈ 30.5 mmol/L. The measured HCO3 of 32 mmol/L is higher than expected — indicating an additional primary metabolic alkalosis (from vomiting) on top of his chronic respiratory acidosis.
