  • This is a triple acid–base disorder: (1) chronic respiratory acidosis (COPD), (2) metabolic alkalosis (vomiting/volume and chloride depletion), and (3) a high anion gap metabolic acidosis (septic lactic acidosis, lactate 4.5). The alkalosis and acidosis processes are raising and lowering bicarbonate in opposite directions, largely cancelling out and leaving a near-normal pH despite three significant, clinically important concurrent disorders.
Marking guide

12 marks: recognition that a normal pH does not exclude a significant disturbance (2), correct anion gap calculation (2), correct delta ratio calculation with correct caveat about its limitation here (2), correct compensation check against known baseline (3), correct identification of all three primary disorders (3).

Guideline-drift trap: A "normal" pH is sometimes taken to mean no significant acid-base disturbance. A normal or near-normal pH can result from counterbalancing disorders — a systematic anion gap and compensation/delta-delta calculation is required to unmask mixed or triple disorders, and skipping this step is a common, serious error in complex ICU patients with multiple concurrent processes.

References

  1. Berend K, de Vries AP, Gans RO. Physiological approach to assessment of acid-base disturbances. N Engl J Med. 2014;371:1434–1445.
  2. Seifter JL. Integration of Acid-Base and Electrolyte Disorders. N Engl J Med. 2014;371:1821–1831.

CBS_BIO_03 — Hypercalcaemia of Malignancy

Biochemistry / Data Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old woman with known squamous cell lung cancer presents with confusion, constipation, and polyuria.

ParameterResultReference range
Corrected calcium3.8 mmol/L2.20–2.60 mmol/L
Phosphate0.7 mmol/L0.8–1.5 mmol/L
PTH<0.5 pmol/L (suppressed)1.6–6.9 pmol/L
PTHrPElevatedUndetectable
Creatinine145 µmol/L45–90 µmol/L
ECGShort QT interval—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this biochemistry and identify the likely mechanism.
  2. Explain the ECG finding and contrast it with the ECG change seen in the opposite calcium derangement.
  3. Outline your immediate management, in the correct order.
  4. What longer-term treatment addresses the underlying process?
Reveal model answer
  • Severe hypercalcaemia with a suppressed PTH excludes primary hyperparathyroidism (where PTH would be elevated or inappropriately normal) and, combined with elevated PTHrP and known squamous cell lung cancer, is consistent with humoral hypercalcaemia of malignancy (PTHrP-mediated) — a classic association with squamous cell carcinomas.
  • A short QT interval is the classic ECG finding in hypercalcaemia (in severe cases, Osborn-like J waves and arrhythmia risk can also occur) — this contrasts with prolonged QT seen in hypocalcaemia.
  • Immediate management, in order: (1) aggressive IV fluid rehydration (0.9% saline, often several litres) first — dehydration is common and worsens hypercalcaemia; (2) IV bisphosphonate (e.g. zoledronic acid) — onset delayed 2–4 days, so not effective for immediate crisis alone; (3) calcitonin for a rapid but short-lived effect while awaiting bisphosphonate onset; (4) consider denosumab in bisphosphonate-refractory cases or significant renal impairment (denosumab is not renally cleared, unlike bisphosphonates); (5) haemodialysis for severe, refractory, life-threatening hypercalcaemia with renal failure.
  • Definitive longer-term management is treatment of the underlying malignancy, with oncology involvement; hypercalcaemia of malignancy is generally a poor prognostic marker.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation and PTHrP mechanism (3), correct short-QT/hypercalcaemia vs long-QT/hypocalcaemia contrast (2), correctly-ordered immediate management (5), correct definitive/ long-term management (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Loop diuretics were historically taught as a standard adjunct to "help excrete calcium." Current evidence does not support their routine use — they add no meaningful benefit once adequate rehydration is achieved and risk worsening volume depletion (and thus hypercalcaemia) if given before the patient is euvolaemic; they are reserved for a much narrower role rather than routine early combination therapy.

References

  1. Walker MD, Shane E. Hypercalcemia: A Review. JAMA. 2022;328(16):1624–1636.
  2. Mirrakhimov AE. Hypercalcemia of Malignancy: An Update on Pathogenesis and Management. N Am J Med Sci. 2015;7(11):483–493.

CBS_BIO_04 — Troponin Kinetics: Type 1 vs Type 2 Myocardial Infarction

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 72-year-old man in septic shock (tachycardic, hypotensive) has troponin sent as part of a general work-up. He has no chest pain and no ischaemic ECG changes.

ParameterResultReference range
High-sensitivity troponin T (0h)85 ng/L<14 ng/L (99th percentile)
High-sensitivity troponin T (3h)90 ng/L<14 ng/L (99th percentile)
ECGSinus tachycardia, no ST/T changes—
Heart rate128/min60–100/min
MAP58 mmHg (on noradrenaline)≥65 mmHg (target)

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this troponin pattern using the Fourth Universal Definition of MI framework.
  2. Distinguish Type 1 from Type 2 MI, and classify this patient.
  3. What are the management implications of this distinction?
  4. Does this mean the troponin result is unimportant? Explain.
Reveal model answer
  • The Fourth Universal Definition of MI requires a rising and/or falling pattern with at least one value above the 99th percentile plus evidence of myocardial ischaemia (symptoms, ischaemic ECG changes, imaging, or angiographic evidence). Here, the troponin is elevated but shows only a minimal rise (85→90 ng/L) with no symptoms or ECG changes — this is better classified as myocardial injury rather than acute MI.
  • Type 1 MI is due to spontaneous atherothrombotic plaque rupture/erosion. Type 2 MI is myocardial injury from oxygen supply-demand mismatch without plaque rupture (e.g. severe sepsis, tachyarrhythmia, severe anaemia, hypotension). This patient's picture — sepsis, tachycardia, hypotension, minimal troponin rise, no chest pain/ECG changes — is much more consistent with Type 2 MI/non-ischaemic myocardial injury than Type 1.
  • Management implications: focus on treating the underlying precipitant (sepsis) rather than routine anticoagulation, antiplatelet therapy, or urgent angiography as for Type 1 MI — unnecessary invasive work-up or anticoagulation could cause harm without benefit if this is Type 2/non-ischaemic injury; angiography is reserved for genuine diagnostic uncertainty or high suspicion of concurrent Type 1 pathology.
  • No — the result is not unimportant. An elevated troponin of any type in critical illness is a marker of worse prognosis/mortality risk and should prompt cardiac risk stratification and optimisation of the underlying critical illness, even though MI-specific therapy is not indicated.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct application of the Universal Definition framework (4), correct Type 1/Type 2 distinction and classification (4), correct management implications (2), correct prognostic significance point (2).

Common error / drift trap: An elevated troponin in a critically ill patient is sometimes automatically treated as acute (Type 1) MI, triggering dual antiplatelet therapy/ anticoagulation/urgent angiography. Without a clear rising/falling pattern and ischaemic evidence, and with an obvious supply-demand mismatch precipitant, this more likely represents Type 2 MI or non-ischaemic injury, where treating the underlying critical illness is the priority.

References

  1. Thygesen K, Alpert JS, Jaffe AS, et al. Fourth Universal Definition of Myocardial Infarction. Circulation. 2018;138:e618–e651.
  2. Chapman AR, Adamson PD, Mills NL. Type 2 myocardial infarction: evolving approaches to diagnosis and risk-stratification. Clin Chem. 2017;63(1):61–69.

CBS_BIO_05 — Alveolar-Arterial Gradient in Hypoxaemic Respiratory Failure

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man develops sudden dyspnoea. Arterial blood gas on room air is shown. Pulmonary embolism is suspected.

ParameterResultReference range
pH7.467.35–7.45
PaCO24.2 kPa (32 mmHg)4.7–6.0 kPa (35–45 mmHg)
PaO28.5 kPa (64 mmHg)11–13 kPa (on air)
Bicarbonate22 mmol/L22–26 mmol/L
Inspired O2Room air (FiO2 0.21)—
Age55 years—

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the alveolar-arterial (A-a) gradient using the alveolar gas equation.
  2. What does an elevated A-a gradient tell you clinically, and how does it help you here?
  3. What are the limitations of this calculation?
  4. How does this correlate with his likely diagnosis and next step?
Reveal model answer
  • Alveolar gas equation: PAO2 = FiO2 × (Patm − PH2O) − PaCO2/R. On room air: PAO2 = 0.21 × (760 − 47) − 32/0.8 ≈ 149.7 − 40 ≈ 110 mmHg. A-a gradient = 110 − 64 ≈ 46 mmHg. Expected normal gradient for age 55 (rule of thumb: age/4 + 4) ≈ 18 mmHg — this patient's gradient is markedly elevated.
  • An elevated A-a gradient indicates an intrinsic gas-exchange abnormality (V/Q mismatch, shunt, or diffusion limitation) rather than pure hypoventilation — this helps differentiate causes of hypoxaemia: a normal A-a gradient with hypoxaemia points to hypoventilation or low inspired oxygen, whereas an elevated gradient (as here) points to a parenchymal or vascular lung process such as PE, pneumonia, or ARDS.
  • Limitations: the normal range widens with age; the gradient is affected non-linearly by FiO2, complicating serial comparison on supplemental oxygen; it reflects a single time point and is dependent on the accuracy of the measured PaCO2.
  • His markedly elevated A-a gradient, respiratory alkalosis, hypoxaemia, and sudden onset are all consistent with acute PE — proceed to CTPA, with initial anticoagulation/supportive management pending confirmation.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct alveolar gas equation calculation (5), correct clinical interpretation/utility (3), limitations named (2), correct diagnostic correlation and next step (2).

Common error / drift trap: A common error is not accounting for PaCO2 (via the alveolar gas equation) when assessing hypoxaemia, or comparing PaO2 directly to inspired oxygen without calculating the A-a gradient — this can miss the distinction between hypoventilation (normal gradient) and true gas-exchange pathology (elevated gradient), risking inappropriate reassurance or a missed diagnosis of significant pulmonary pathology.

References

  1. Hardman JG, Aitkenhead AR. Estimating alveolar-arterial oxygen gradient by non-invasive means. Br J Anaesth. 2003;91(6):903–905.
  2. West JB, Luks AM. West's Respiratory Physiology: The Essentials. 10th ed.

CBS_BIO_06 — Liver Function Test Pattern: Cholestatic vs Hepatocellular Injury

Biochemistry / Data GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old man with a history of biliary colic presents with jaundice, right upper quadrant pain, fever and rigors.

ParameterResultReference range
ALT95 U/L<40 U/L
AST80 U/L<40 U/L
ALP620 U/L30–130 U/L
GGT480 U/L<60 U/L
Bilirubin (conjugated predominant)110 µmol/L<21 µmol/L
Temperature38.9°C, rigors—

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the R ratio and classify this pattern of liver injury.
  2. What is your differential for this pattern?
  3. Does the clinical picture raise any red flags requiring urgent intervention?
  4. Contrast this with the biochemical pattern you would expect in acute hepatocellular injury (e.g. paracetamol overdose or ischaemic hepatitis).
Reveal model answer
  • R ratio = (ALT/ULN) / (ALP/ULN) = (95/40) / (620/130) = 2.4 / 4.8 ≈ 0.5. An R ratio <2 indicates a cholestatic pattern (R >5 = hepatocellular; 2–5 = mixed).
  • Differential for a cholestatic pattern: intrahepatic (drug-induced liver injury, primary biliary cholangitis, sepsis/ICU cholestasis) versus extrahepatic obstruction (choledocholithiasis, malignant stricture, benign stricture) — his history of biliary colic points strongly toward choledocholithiasis. Ultrasound is the first-line imaging test for biliary dilation.
  • Yes — fever and rigors with jaundice and RUQ pain (Charcot's triad) suggest ascending cholangitis, a time-critical sepsis source requiring urgent biliary decompression (ERCP) plus antibiotics; watch for hypotension/confusion (Reynolds' pentad), indicating suppurative cholangitis and even greater urgency.
  • Acute hepatocellular injury (paracetamol overdose, ischaemic "shock liver") typically shows markedly elevated transaminases (often into the thousands) with only a modest ALP rise — the opposite proportional pattern to this patient — and follows a different diagnostic pathway (viral serology, drug/toxicology history, assessment for a hypoperfusion event) rather than biliary imaging.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct R ratio calculation and classification (4), correct cholestatic differential (3), correct recognition of cholangitis as a time-critical red flag (3), correct contrast with hepatocellular pattern (2).

Common error / drift trap: Interpreting an isolated modestly-elevated ALP or GGT without calculating the R ratio/pattern can lead to misclassification of hepatocellular vs cholestatic injury and delay in recognising a biliary obstruction requiring urgent decompression — the pattern of ALT/AST rise relative to ALP rise, not absolute values alone, should guide the diagnostic pathway.

References

  1. European Association for the Study of the Liver. EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines: Drug-induced liver injury. J Hepatol. 2019;70(6):1222–1261.
  2. Kiriyama S, Kozaka K, Takada T, et al. Tokyo Guidelines 2018: diagnostic criteria and severity grading of acute cholangitis. J Hepatobiliary Pancreat Sci. 2018;25(1):17–30.

CBS_BIO_07 — Coagulation Screen Interpretation: Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation

Biochemistry / Data Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 50-year-old woman with septic shock secondary to necrotising fasciitis is oozing from cannula sites.

ParameterResultReference range
Platelets45 ×109/L150–400 ×109/L
PT22 s (control 12 s)10–14 s
APTT55 s (control 30 s)25–35 s
Fibrinogen0.9 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
D-dimer>20 mg/L FEU<0.5 mg/L FEU

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this coagulation panel and formally score it.
  2. What is your differential, and what supports this diagnosis over the alternatives?
  3. Outline your management priorities, in order of importance.
  4. How would you monitor response to treatment?
Reveal model answer
  • Thrombocytopenia, prolonged PT and APTT, low fibrinogen, and markedly raised D-dimer, in a patient with a recognised trigger (sepsis) — this pattern, formally scored using the ISTH DIC score (platelet count, D-dimer/fibrin degradation products, PT prolongation, fibrinogen level), gives a score compatible with overt disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC).
  • Differential: liver failure (would typically show preserved factor VIII, unlike DIC where it is consumed), dilutional coagulopathy from massive transfusion, and isolated vitamin K deficiency (typically isolated PT prolongation). The clinical context (sepsis, both bleeding and thrombotic potential) supports DIC.
  • Priority 1, by far the most important: treat the underlying cause — source control and antibiotics for the necrotising fasciitis/sepsis; this is the only truly effective treatment of DIC. Priority 2: supportive blood product replacement guided by active bleeding and lab trends, not a fixed formula — FFP for factor replacement, cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen concentrate to target fibrinogen >1.5–2 g/L, platelets for active bleeding or very low counts. Avoid anticoagulation in this bleeding-predominant phenotype (a more nuanced role exists in a thrombosis-predominant DIC phenotype, a less common scenario).
  • Monitor serial platelets, coagulation screen, fibrinogen, and D-dimer trend to assess response to treating the underlying cause; recognise DIC as dynamic and capable of shifting between bleeding and thrombotic phenotypes.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation and DIC scoring approach (4), correct differential with rationale (3), correctly-prioritised management (underlying cause first) (3), sensible monitoring plan (2).

Common error / drift trap: DIC is sometimes treated with blood product replacement in isolation without addressing the underlying trigger — this treats the epiphenomenon, not the disease. Product replacement is supportive and should be reserved for active bleeding or very deranged parameters with bleeding risk; the definitive treatment of DIC is always treatment of the underlying cause.

References

  1. Levi M, Toh CH, Thachil J, Watson HG. Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of disseminated intravascular coagulation. Br J Haematol. 2009;145(1):24–33.
  2. Taylor FB, Toh CH, Hoots WK, et al. Towards a definition, clinical and laboratory criteria, and a scoring system for disseminated intravascular coagulation (ISTH). Thromb Haemost. 2001;86(5):1327–1330.

CBS_BIO_08 — Sick Euthyroid Syndrome vs True Thyroid Dysfunction

Biochemistry / Data Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old man, 10 days into ICU admission for severe sepsis, has thyroid function tests sent as part of a general work-up for persistent fatigue/hypotension.

ParameterResultReference range
TSH0.3 mU/L0.4–4.0 mU/L
Free T49 pmol/L9–25 pmol/L
Free T32.1 pmol/L3.5–6.5 pmol/L
Reverse T3ElevatedNormal-low
Prior thyroid historyNone—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this pattern. What is the most likely explanation?
  2. How would you distinguish this from true central (secondary) hypothyroidism?
  3. Should this be treated with thyroid hormone replacement? Justify your answer.
  4. When, if ever, would you retest?
Reveal model answer
  • Low free T3 (the classic "low T3 syndrome"), low-normal/low free T4, low-normal TSH, and elevated reverse T3 in the context of severe critical illness — this pattern is characteristic of sick euthyroid syndrome (non-thyroidal illness syndrome, NTIS), an adaptive response to severe illness (reduced peripheral T4→T3 conversion, altered binding proteins, hypothalamic- pituitary suppression) rather than primary thyroid disease.
  • Both NTIS and true central hypothyroidism can show a low/inappropriately-normal TSH with low T4. The clinical context favours NTIS here: acute severe illness, no prior thyroid history, and an expected pattern of recovery as the illness resolves. If there is strong suspicion of true pituitary/ hypothalamic pathology (other pituitary hormone deficiencies, a structural lesion, a more chronic presentation), further work-up (other pituitary axes, pituitary imaging) is warranted.
  • No — current evidence does not support routine thyroid hormone replacement for sick euthyroid syndrome. Trials of T3/T4 supplementation in critical illness have not shown consistent benefit and may cause harm (increased catabolism, cardiac stress); "correcting" the numbers reflexively is a common but incorrect response.
  • Defer definitive thyroid function assessment until after recovery from the acute illness (typically weeks later) if there is persistent clinical concern for true thyroid dysfunction, since the NTIS pattern typically resolves as the patient recovers.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct recognition of sick euthyroid pattern and mechanism (4), correct distinction from central hypothyroidism (3), correct, evidence-based decision not to treat routinely (3), correct retest timing (2).

Common error / drift trap: A common error is treating "abnormal" thyroid function tests found incidentally in a critically ill patient as true thyroid disease requiring hormone replacement. Sick euthyroid syndrome is generally an adaptive, self-limiting response, and current evidence does not support routine supplementation — treating the underlying illness, not the thyroid numbers, is correct.

References

  1. Fliers E, Bianco AC, Langouche L, Boelen A. Thyroid function in critically ill patients. Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol. 2015;3(10):816–825.
  2. Van den Berghe G. Non-thyroidal illness in the ICU: a syndrome with different faces. Thyroid. 2014;24(10):1456–1465.

CBS_BIO_09 — Lactate Interpretation: Type A vs Type B Lactic Acidosis

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old man, 2 days post cardiac surgery, has a lactate of 6.8 mmol/L. He is haemodynamically stable with warm, well-perfused peripheries, normal urine output, and no other evidence of shock. He is on high-dose nebulised salbutamol for bronchospasm and takes metformin at home.

ParameterResultReference range
Lactate6.8 mmol/L0.5–2.0 mmol/L
MAP78 mmHg (no vasopressors)≥65 mmHg
Urine output1.2 mL/kg/h≥0.5 mL/kg/h
Capillary refill<2 s<3 s
Creatinine142 µmol/L (baseline 90)45–90 µmol/L
Regular medicationMetformin; nebulised salbutamol 4-hourly—

Structured questions

  1. Classify this lactate elevation using the Type A/Type B framework.
  2. Explain the mechanism by which salbutamol can raise lactate.
  3. What is your approach to the metformin?
  4. Outline your general approach to an elevated lactate with an unclear cause.
Reveal model answer
  • Type A lactic acidosis reflects tissue hypoperfusion/hypoxia (shock, severe hypoxaemia, seizure). Type B occurs without tissue hypoperfusion (drugs/toxins — metformin, beta-agonists; liver failure impairing clearance; malignancy; thiamine deficiency). This patient's clinical stability with normal perfusion, urine output, and capillary refill, alongside beta-agonist and metformin exposure, points strongly to Type B.
  • Beta-2 agonism (salbutamol) stimulates glycolysis via increased cAMP, causing lactate production independent of tissue hypoxia — a well-recognised, usually benign and self-limiting phenomenon with high-dose nebulised/IV salbutamol.
  • Check renal function (already mildly impaired here) for metformin accumulation (metformin-associated lactic acidosis, MALA) — hold metformin, and consider renal replacement therapy if severe MALA develops with significant renal failure.
  • Do not automatically assume sepsis/hypoperfusion for an elevated lactate. Systematically assess perfusion status/clinical examination, review medications, and consider renal/hepatic function and other Type B causes before escalating to aggressive fluid resuscitation or antibiotics that may not be needed — trend the lactate and reassess rather than treating an isolated value in isolation.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct Type A/B classification with rationale (4), correct salbutamol mechanism (3), correct metformin/MALA reasoning (3), correct general systematic approach to unclear lactate elevation (2).

Common error / drift trap: An elevated lactate is often reflexively equated with tissue hypoperfusion/sepsis, triggering aggressive fluid resuscitation. In a haemodynamically stable, well-perfused patient, Type B causes must be considered — treating a Type B lactate rise as septic shock can lead to unnecessary fluid overload and inappropriate antibiotic use.

References

  1. Kraut JA, Madias NE. Lactic Acidosis. N Engl J Med. 2014;371:2309–2319.
  2. Andersen LW, Mackenhauer J, Roberts JC, et al. Etiology and Therapeutic Approach to Elevated Lactate. Mayo Clin Proc. 2013;88(10):1127–1140.

CBS_BIO_10 — CSF Analysis: Bacterial Meningitis Pattern

Biochemistry / Data Neurology / Neurocritical Care CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 25-year-old man presents with fever, neck stiffness, photophobia and altered consciousness. Lumbar puncture is performed after CT head excludes a contraindication.

ParameterThis patientTypical viral patternTypical TB pattern
Opening pressure32 cmH2O (raised)Normal/mildly raisedOften markedly raised
White cell count1200/µL, 90% neutrophils10–500/µL, lymphocyte-predominant50–500/µL, lymphocyte-predominant
Protein3.2 g/L (markedly raised)Normal/mildly raisedMarkedly raised
CSF:serum glucose ratio0.2 (serum 5.5, CSF 1.1)Normal (>0.5)Low (<0.3)

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this CSF result.
  2. How would you differentiate this from viral and TB meningitis, and what pitfall exists?
  3. What is your immediate management priority, and would you wait for these results before treating?
  4. What specific therapy timing point around antibiotics is critical, and why?
Reveal model answer
  • Neutrophilic pleocytosis, markedly raised protein, and a markedly reduced CSF:serum glucose ratio (<0.4–0.5 is abnormal), together with raised opening pressure, form the classic pattern of bacterial meningitis.
  • Viral meningitis typically shows a lymphocyte-predominant picture with normal or mildly raised protein and a normal glucose ratio; TB meningitis shows a lymphocytic picture but with very high protein, very low glucose, and a more gradual/subacute onset. Pitfall: early bacterial meningitis can occasionally show a lymphocytic picture, so an atypical CSF profile does not reliably exclude bacterial disease — clinical correlation and PCR/culture confirmation remain essential.
  • The immediate priority is time-critical empirical antibiotics — treatment should never wait for LP results, and if CT is required first (e.g. focal neurology, reduced GCS, papilloedema concern) or LP is otherwise delayed, blood cultures and empirical antibiotics should be given immediately beforehand.
  • Give dexamethasone with or just before the first antibiotic dose — evidence for reduced neurological sequelae (particularly in pneumococcal meningitis) applies to this timing; giving it after antibiotics have already been administered loses this benefit. Empirical antibiotics typically ceftriaxone (± vancomycin for resistant pneumococcus per local epidemiology), adding ampicillin if Listeria risk (age extremes, immunosuppression).
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation of bacterial pattern (3), correct differentiation with pitfall acknowledged (3), correct "do not wait for LP" priority (3), correct dexamethasone timing and antibiotic choice (3).

Common error / drift trap: Waiting for LP results before starting antibiotics in suspected bacterial meningitis — particularly when CT is required first — is a dangerous, common delay. Blood cultures and empirical antibiotics (± dexamethasone) should be given immediately whenever any delay is anticipated, rather than waiting for the "complete" diagnostic picture.

References

  1. van de Beek D, Cabellos C, Dzupova O, et al. ESCMID guideline: diagnosis and treatment of acute bacterial meningitis. Clin Microbiol Infect. 2016;22(Suppl 3):S37–S62.
  2. Tunkel AR, Hartman BJ, Kaplan SL, et al. Practice Guidelines for the Management of Bacterial Meningitis. Clin Infect Dis. 2004;39(9):1267–1284.
  3. National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. NG240: Meningitis (bacterial) and meningococcal disease.

CBS_BIO_11 — HELLP Syndrome: Biochemistry Panel

Biochemistry / Data Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A woman at 33 weeks' gestation with hypertension develops headache and right upper quadrant pain. Bloods are sent.

ParameterResultReference range
Haemoglobin95 g/L (falling)110–140 g/L (pregnancy)
Platelets65 ×109/L150–400 ×109/L
Blood filmSchistocytes presentAbsent
LDH850 U/L<250 U/L
HaptoglobinLowNormal
AST180 U/L<40 U/L
ALT150 U/L<40 U/L
Bilirubin28 µmol/L<21 µmol/L
Coagulation screenNormalNormal

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this panel using the components of its name.
  2. What other conditions in pregnancy could mimic this, and how would you differentiate them?
  3. Outline your management priorities.
  4. What complications would you specifically monitor for?
Reveal model answer
  • Haemolysis (raised LDH, low haptoglobin, schistocytes, falling Hb), ELevated Liver enzymes (AST/ALT), Low Platelets — HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of pre-eclampsia.
  • Differentiate from TTP (typically normal coagulation and more severe/isolated thrombocytopenia with prominent neurological involvement, not necessarily hypertensive/proteinuric) and acute fatty liver of pregnancy (a more overt liver-failure picture — significant coagulopathy, hypoglycaemia, hyperammonaemia — reflecting greater synthetic dysfunction). Overlap can occur, and correct differentiation matters because management differs (e.g. plasma exchange for TTP vs delivery for HELLP).
  • Management: as for severe pre-eclampsia/eclampsia (magnesium sulfate seizure prophylaxis, BP control — see CCS_S27); definitive treatment is delivery, timed according to gestation and severity. Corticosteroids (dexamethasone) are sometimes used to accelerate platelet recovery and fetal lung maturity, though evidence for a specific maternal outcome benefit in HELLP itself is not strong and remains debated.
  • Monitor for hepatic subcapsular haematoma/rupture (see CBS_IMG_12), DIC, AKI, pulmonary oedema, and eclamptic seizures.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct HELLP interpretation using all three components (4), correct differentiation from TTP/AFLP with rationale (4), correct management priorities (2), correct complication monitoring (2).

Common error / drift trap: HELLP, TTP, and AFLP can have overlapping presentations and are sometimes conflated — correct differentiation matters because management differs significantly, and clinicians should use a structured differential rather than assuming any thrombocytopenic pregnant patient with abnormal LFTs automatically has HELLP.

References

  1. Sibai BM. Diagnosis, controversies, and management of the syndrome of hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelet count. Obstet Gynecol. 2004;103(5 Pt 1):981–991.

CBS_BIO_12 — Euglycaemic Diabetic Ketoacidosis (SGLT2 Inhibitor)

Biochemistry / Data Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 58-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes on dapagliflozin (an SGLT2 inhibitor) has been fasting before elective surgery. She presents with nausea and malaise.

ParameterResultReference range
Glucose11 mmol/L4–7.8 mmol/L
Capillary/serum ketones4.2 mmol/L<0.6 mmol/L
pH7.227.35–7.45
Bicarbonate12 mmol/L22–26 mmol/L
Sodium138 mmol/L135–145 mmol/L
Chloride101 mmol/L98–106 mmol/L

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this panel. Does the glucose level exclude DKA?
  2. Explain the mechanism of this presentation.
  3. How does management differ from standard DKA protocols?
  4. What preventive measure could have avoided this presentation?
Reveal model answer
  • No — this is euglycaemic DKA: a high anion gap metabolic acidosis (anion gap = 138 − (101+12) = 25) with significantly raised ketones and acidaemia, despite glucose being only mildly elevated. The diagnosis rests on ketones/anion gap/pH, not glucose level.
  • Mechanism: SGLT2 inhibitors cause glycosuria independent of insulin, so blood glucose can remain deceptively low/normal despite significant ketogenesis driven by relative insulinopenia — here precipitated by fasting/reduced carbohydrate intake before surgery.
  • Management follows the standard DKA protocol (fixed-rate insulin infusion, fluid resuscitation, treat the precipitant) with one key modification: because glucose is not elevated, run a concurrent dextrose infusion alongside insulin from the outset, rather than withholding dextrose until glucose falls to a threshold as in standard DKA/HHS protocols. Stop the SGLT2 inhibitor.
  • Prevention: SGLT2 inhibitors should typically be withheld before elective surgery/ significant fasting (per specific perioperative guidance — commonly 3 days before surgery, longer for some agents) and during acute illness ("sick day rules").
Marking guide

12 marks: correct recognition that glucose does not exclude DKA, with anion gap calculation (5), correct mechanism (3), correct concurrent-dextrose modification (2), correct perioperative prevention point (2).

Guideline-drift trap: A relatively normal/mildly elevated glucose is sometimes used to exclude DKA, especially in a patient on an SGLT2 inhibitor. Euglycaemic DKA is a recognised, dangerous entity where glucose can be deceptively normal despite significant ketoacidosis.

References

  1. Goldenberg RM, Berard LD, Cheng AYY, et al. SGLT2 Inhibitor–associated Diabetic Ketoacidosis: Clinical Review and Recommendations for Prevention and Diagnosis. Clin Ther. 2016;38(12):2654–2664.e1.

CBS_BIO_13 — Massive Transfusion: Citrate Toxicity and Electrolyte Derangement

Biochemistry / Data Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A trauma patient has received 15 units of red cells plus FFP and platelets over 2 hours. He is now hypotensive with a prolonged QT interval and is cold.

ParameterResultReference range
Ionised calcium0.75 mmol/L1.15–1.35 mmol/L
Potassium5.8 mmol/L (rising)3.5–5.0 mmol/L
Temperature34.2°C36.5–37.5°C
ECGProlonged QT, reduced contractility—

Structured questions

  1. Explain the mechanism of the hypocalcaemia.
  2. Explain the mechanism of the hyperkalaemia in this context.
  3. Outline your management, including monitoring frequency.
  4. How does hypothermia interact with these derangements?
Reveal model answer
  • Citrate, used as an anticoagulant in stored blood products, chelates calcium, causing hypocalcaemia — worsened by rapid, large-volume transfusion and impaired hepatic citrate metabolism (itself worsened by hypothermia, liver dysfunction, and shock, all reducing citrate clearance).
  • Potassium leaches from stored red cells during storage (increasing with storage duration); rapid, large-volume transfusion — especially through central access — can cause transient but clinically significant hyperkalaemia, worsened by renal impairment, acidosis, or hypothermia reducing clearance.
  • Management: proactive calcium replacement during massive transfusion (empirical calcium chloride/gluconate boluses guided by ionised calcium, not waiting for severe symptoms), correct hyperkalaemia per the standard pathway, and actively rewarm the patient (hypothermia worsens both coagulopathy and citrate metabolism — part of the "lethal triad", see CCS_L20). Monitor ionised calcium and potassium frequently — every 30 minutes to hourly — during ongoing massive transfusion.
  • Hypothermia reduces hepatic citrate clearance (worsening hypocalcaemia), impairs the clotting cascade (worsening coagulopathy), and reduces renal/metabolic clearance of potassium — actively correcting temperature is an integral part of managing these electrolyte derangements, not a separate issue.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct citrate/hypocalcaemia mechanism (3), correct hyperkalaemia mechanism (3), correct proactive management and monitoring frequency (4), correct hypothermia interaction (2).

Common error / drift trap: Calcium and potassium derangements during massive transfusion are sometimes only checked/addressed reactively after symptoms develop, rather than being proactively monitored and empirically corrected as an integral, anticipated part of massive transfusion protocols.

References

  1. Rossaint R, Afshari A, Bouillon B, et al. The European guideline on management of major bleeding and coagulopathy following trauma: 6th edition. Crit Care. 2023;27:80.

CBS_BIO_14 — Base Deficit and Lactate: Occult Shock in Trauma

Biochemistry / Data Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A young, fit trauma patient has apparently reassuring vital signs (BP 110/70 mmHg, HR 95/min) after a significant mechanism of injury. A venous blood gas is sent as part of the trauma work-up.

ParameterResultReference range
Base deficit−8 mmol/L−2 to +2 mmol/L
Lactate5.2 mmol/L0.5–2.0 mmol/L
BP110/70 mmHg—
Heart rate95/min60–100/min

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this result in the context of the apparently reassuring vital signs.
  2. How would you classify shock severity using base deficit, and what does it correlate with?
  3. How should you use these values over time, rather than as a single snapshot?
  4. What does this result change about your immediate management?
Reveal model answer
  • Despite "normal-looking" vital signs, the significant base deficit and lactate indicate occult tissue hypoperfusion — compensatory mechanisms (particularly in young, fit patients) can maintain apparently normal BP and heart rate despite significant blood loss until decompensation occurs suddenly and late. "Vital signs lie" in compensated shock.
  • Base deficit severity roughly correlates with shock severity and transfusion requirement/mortality risk in the trauma literature (approximate categories: mild −2 to −5, moderate −6 to −9, severe <−10).
  • Serial trending of lactate clearance and base deficit over time is more useful than a single value — as a marker of resuscitation adequacy and response to treatment.
  • This result should heighten suspicion for ongoing occult haemorrhage and lower the threshold for further investigation (CT, repeat FAST) and close monitoring/escalation, rather than being reassured by the normal-looking vital signs.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation of occult hypoperfusion despite normal vitals (4), correct base deficit severity classification/correlation (3), correct emphasis on trending over single values (2), correct management implication (3).

Common error / drift trap: Relying on "normal" vital signs alone to exclude significant blood loss in trauma is dangerous — compensatory mechanisms can maintain apparently normal vitals despite significant occult haemorrhage. Base deficit and lactate are more sensitive markers and should be actively checked and trended, not overridden by reassuring-looking vital signs.

References

  1. Mutschler M, Nienaber U, Münzberg M, et al. The ATLS classification of hypovolaemic shock: a classification needing revision. Resuscitation. 2013;84(3):309–313.

CBS_BIO_15 — Digoxin Toxicity: A Hyperkalaemia Exception

Biochemistry / Data General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 78-year-old woman on digoxin for atrial fibrillation presents with nausea, vomiting, visual disturbance (yellow-green halos), and a bradyarrhythmia.

ParameterResultReference range
Digoxin level3.8 ng/mL0.8–2.0 ng/mL (therapeutic)
Potassium5.9 mmol/L3.5–5.0 mmol/L
Creatinine168 µmol/L45–90 µmol/L
ECGBradyarrhythmia with increased ectopic activity—

Structured questions

  1. Correlate the digoxin level and symptoms with the diagnosis.
  2. What is the specific significance of the hyperkalaemia in this context?
  3. What is definitive treatment for severe toxicity?
  4. What standard hyperkalaemia treatment should you specifically avoid here, and why?
Reveal model answer
  • An elevated digoxin level with GI symptoms, visual disturbance (xanthopsia/halos), and arrhythmia (digoxin toxicity can produce almost any arrhythmia, classically bidirectional VT or various degrees of AV block with increased ectopic automaticity) confirms digoxin toxicity, likely precipitated by renal impairment reducing clearance.
  • In acute digoxin toxicity specifically, the degree of hyperkalaemia correlates with severity and mortality — digoxin inhibits the Na+/K+-ATPase pump, shifting potassium extracellularly; this is a distinct, important prognostic marker unique to this context.
  • Definitive treatment for severe toxicity (life-threatening arrhythmia, severe hyperkalaemia, high levels) is digoxin-specific antibody fragments (DigiFab/Digibind), dosed based on ingested dose or a level/weight-based formula.
  • Avoid standard IV calcium for the hyperkalaemia — this is a widely-taught caution in digoxin toxicity, historically associated with concern for exacerbating cardiac toxicity (the "stone heart" phenomenon), though this has been debated in more recent literature. The correct approach is to treat the hyperkalaemia by treating the underlying digoxin toxicity with digoxin-specific antibody fragments, not standard calcium administration.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct diagnosis and correlation (3), correct hyperkalaemia-severity significance (3), correct DigiFab as definitive treatment (3), correct avoidance of calcium with rationale (3).

Common error / drift trap: Treating hyperkalaemia in digoxin toxicity with standard IV calcium (as per routine hyperkalaemia protocols) is a widely-taught caution/relative contraindication — the definitive treatment is treating the underlying digoxin toxicity with digoxin-specific antibody fragments, not standard calcium therapy.

References

  1. Chan BS, Buckley NA. Digoxin-specific antibody fragments in the treatment of digoxin toxicity. Clin Toxicol (Phila). 2014;52(8):824–836.

CBS_BIO_16 — Osmolar Gap and Toxic Alcohol Poisoning

Biochemistry / Data General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 40-year-old man is found confused with an empty bottle of antifreeze nearby.

ParameterResultReference range
Measured osmolality340 mOsm/kg275–295 mOsm/kg
Calculated osmolality295 mOsm/kg—
pH7.157.35–7.45
Anion gap28 mmol/L8–12 mmol/L
Sodium140 mmol/L135–145 mmol/L

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the osmolar gap and interpret it.
  2. How does the osmolar gap relate to the anion gap over the course of toxic alcohol metabolism?
  3. What specific clinical features would suggest ethylene glycol versus methanol?
  4. Outline your management.
Reveal model answer
  • Osmolar gap = measured − calculated osmolality = 340 − 295 = 45 mOsm/kg (markedly elevated; normal <10) — indicating an unmeasured osmotically active substance, consistent with a toxic alcohol (ethylene glycol, methanol, or less commonly isopropanol).
  • As toxic alcohol metabolism proceeds over time, the parent alcohol (contributing to the osmolar gap) is converted to toxic organic acid metabolites (contributing to the anion gap) — so the osmolar gap falls while the anion gap rises as time passes. A "normal" or falling osmolar gap late in the clinical course does not exclude toxic alcohol poisoning; the anion gap and clinical picture must be considered together with the timeline.
  • Ethylene glycol: calcium oxalate crystalluria, renal failure. Methanol: visual disturbance/ blindness, putaminal haemorrhage on imaging.
  • Management: fomepizole (alcohol dehydrogenase inhibitor, preferred over ethanol infusion for easier dosing and fewer side effects) to block toxic metabolite formation, sodium bicarbonate for severe acidosis, haemodialysis for severe poisoning/renal failure/refractory acidosis/very high levels, and cofactor therapy (thiamine/pyridoxine for ethylene glycol, folinic acid for methanol) to promote non-toxic metabolic pathways.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct osmolar gap calculation and interpretation (4), correct osmolar-gap-vs-anion-gap time-course relationship (3), correct specific features for each toxin (2), correct management (3).

Common error / drift trap: A "normal" osmolar gap late in the clinical course is sometimes used to exclude toxic alcohol poisoning. As metabolism proceeds, the osmolar gap falls while the anion gap rises — a normal or falling osmolar gap late in presentation does not exclude significant toxicity, and the timeline must be considered.

References

  1. Kraut JA, Mullins ME. Toxic Alcohols. N Engl J Med. 2018;378:270–280.

CBS_BIO_17 — Rhabdomyolysis: Biochemistry and Renal Protection

Biochemistry / Data Renal / Metabolic / Acid–base CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old man is found on the floor after a prolonged 12-hour "long lie." He has a swollen, tender leg and dark urine.

ParameterResultReference range
Creatine kinase55,000 U/L30–200 U/L
Creatinine210 µmol/L (rising)60–110 µmol/L
Potassium6.1 mmol/L3.5–5.0 mmol/L
Phosphate2.4 mmol/L0.8–1.5 mmol/L
Corrected calcium1.9 mmol/L2.20–2.60 mmol/L
Urine dipstickStrongly positive for bloodNegative
Urine microscopyNo red cells seen—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this panel, including the urine dipstick/microscopy discrepancy.
  2. Explain the mechanism of the AKI.
  3. Outline your management, including a therapy of debated benefit.
  4. What must you specifically avoid regarding the calcium, and why?
Reveal model answer
  • Markedly elevated CK with the classic associated pattern — hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hypocalcaemia (similar biochemical pattern to tumour lysis syndrome, but a different mechanism) — and a urine dipstick strongly positive for blood with no red cells on microscopy (the classic clue for myoglobinuria) confirm rhabdomyolysis with resulting AKI.
  • Mechanism: myoglobin causes tubular obstruction and direct nephrotoxicity, compounded by volume depletion from fluid sequestration ("third spacing") into the damaged muscle.
  • Management: aggressive IV fluid resuscitation is the mainstay of AKI prevention/ treatment, targeting good urine output. Urine alkalinisation (IV sodium bicarbonate) is historically taught as an adjunct (theoretically reducing myoglobin nephrotoxicity), but current evidence is weak/inconsistent and it has not been shown to be superior to saline alone in more recent analyses — it should never delay or substitute for aggressive fluid resuscitation. Correct hyperkalaemia per the standard pathway.
  • Avoid aggressive correction of asymptomatic hypocalcaemia — as in tumour lysis syndrome, calcium-phosphate precipitation risk applies, and calcium mobilises back out of damaged tissue during the recovery phase, sometimes causing rebound hypercalcaemia later — treat only symptomatic hypocalcaemia.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation including the dipstick/microscopy clue (4), correct mechanism of AKI (2), correct fluid-primary management with correct, cautious stance on bicarbonate (4), correct calcium caution with rebound-hypercalcaemia awareness (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Urine alkalinisation with IV bicarbonate is widely taught as standard adjunct therapy for rhabdomyolysis. Current evidence is weak/inconsistent for added benefit over aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation alone, and it should not delay or substitute for prompt fluid resuscitation, which remains the primary evidence-based intervention.

References

  1. Bosch X, Poch E, Grau JM. Rhabdomyolysis and Acute Kidney Injury. N Engl J Med. 2009;361:62–72.

CBS_BIO_18 — Procalcitonin-Guided Antibiotic Discontinuation

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

An ICU patient on antibiotics for presumed sepsis is clinically improving on day 4. Procalcitonin has trended from a peak of 8.2 ng/mL to 1.1 ng/mL.

ParameterResultReference range
Procalcitonin (day 1, peak)8.2 ng/mL<0.05 ng/mL (healthy)
Procalcitonin (day 4)1.1 ng/mL<0.05 ng/mL (healthy)
Clinical statusImproving — afebrile, weaning support—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this procalcitonin trend and its relevance to antibiotic duration.
  2. What is the evidence base for procalcitonin-guided antibiotic discontinuation?
  3. What are the limitations of procalcitonin as a diagnostic/monitoring tool?
  4. How would you practically apply this in your antibiotic stewardship decision?
Reveal model answer
  • A substantial fall from peak (here, an ~87% fall) toward a low threshold (commonly ~0.25–0.5 ng/mL), combined with clinical improvement, supports resolving bacterial infection and can support a decision to stop or de-escalate antibiotics.
  • Multiple trials and meta-analyses (e.g. the PRORATA and SAPS trials, and Cochrane reviews) show that procalcitonin-guided antibiotic discontinuation algorithms can safely reduce antibiotic duration/exposure without increasing mortality — a genuine antimicrobial stewardship tool.
  • Limitations: procalcitonin can be falsely elevated in non-infective states (major surgery, trauma, cardiogenic shock, some malignancies) and falsely low/normal in localised infections or early in the course — it should be used as an adjunct to, not a replacement for, clinical judgement.
  • Practical application: use the serial trend alongside clinical response to support (not dictate) antibiotic duration decisions — particularly useful for supporting earlier discontinuation in a clinically improving patient where the "right" fixed course length is uncertain.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation of the trend (3), correct evidence base named (3), limitations named (3), correct practical application as an adjunct (3).

Common error / drift trap: Procalcitonin is sometimes used as a standalone "rule in/ rule out infection" test in isolation, ignoring its false positives (surgery, trauma) and false negatives (localised infection). It should be interpreted as a trend alongside clinical assessment, not as a single definitive diagnostic value.

References

  1. Bouadma L, Luyt CE, Tubach F, et al. Use of procalcitonin to reduce patients' exposure to antibiotics in intensive care units (PRORATA). Lancet. 2010;375(9713):463–474.
  2. de Jong E, van Oers JA, Beishuizen A, et al. Efficacy and safety of procalcitonin guidance in reducing the duration of antibiotic treatment in critically ill patients (SAPS). Lancet Infect Dis. 2016;16(7):819–827.

CBS_BIO_19 — Ascitic Fluid Analysis: SAAG and Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis

Biochemistry / Data GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A cirrhotic patient develops new ascites and fever. Diagnostic ascitic tap is performed.

ParameterResultReference range / threshold
Serum albumin22 g/L35–50 g/L
Ascitic fluid albumin8 g/L—
Ascitic fluid neutrophil count320 cells/mm3≥250 cells/mm3 = diagnostic for SBP
Ascitic fluid total protein12 g/L<15 g/L = higher SBP risk

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the SAAG and interpret it.
  2. Would you wait for culture results before treating? Justify your answer.
  3. What specific adjunct therapy would you add, and what evidence supports it?
  4. What does the ascitic protein level tell you about longer-term management?
Reveal model answer
  • SAAG = serum albumin − ascitic albumin = 22 − 8 = 14 g/L. A SAAG >11 g/L indicates portal hypertension-related ascites (cirrhosis, cardiac, Budd-Chiari) — consistent with this patient's cirrhotic ascites.
  • No — an ascitic fluid neutrophil count ≥250 cells/mm3 is diagnostic for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) regardless of culture result (culture is frequently negative even in true SBP). Empirical antibiotics should start immediately once this threshold is met, without waiting for culture confirmation.
  • Add IV albumin (on day 1 and day 3) — landmark trial evidence (Sort et al., NEJM 1999) shows this reduces the risk of hepatorenal syndrome/renal impairment and improves survival in SBP, particularly with significant renal impairment or elevated bilirubin.
  • A low ascitic protein (<15 g/L, as here) identifies patients at higher risk of a first SBP episode, supporting consideration of prophylactic antibiotics (e.g. norfloxacin/ ciprofloxacin) in appropriate high-risk cirrhotic patients; after an episode of SBP, secondary antibiotic prophylaxis is indicated long-term (until resolution of ascites or transplant).
Marking guide

12 marks: correct SAAG calculation/interpretation (3), correct immediate-treatment answer without waiting for culture (3), correct IV albumin adjunct with evidence (3), correct prophylaxis reasoning from protein level (3).

Common error / drift trap: Waiting for ascitic fluid culture results before starting antibiotics for suspected SBP is a common, harmful delay — culture is frequently negative even in true SBP, and the neutrophil count threshold alone is diagnostic and should trigger immediate empirical therapy.

References

  1. Sort P, Navasa M, Arroyo V, et al. Effect of Intravenous Albumin on Renal Impairment and Mortality in Patients with Cirrhosis and Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis. N Engl J Med. 1999;341:403–409.
  2. European Association for the Study of the Liver. EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines for the management of patients with decompensated cirrhosis. J Hepatol. 2018;69(2):406–460.

CBS_BIO_20 — Drowning: Blood Gas Pattern and the Freshwater/Saltwater Myth

Biochemistry / Data Trauma / Burns / Environmental CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A man is pulled from a lake after submersion and intubated on arrival. Arterial blood gas is sent.

ParameterResultReference range
pH7.187.35–7.45
PaCO26.9 kPa (52 mmHg)4.7–6.0 kPa (35–45 mmHg)
PaO27.2 kPa (54 mmHg) on FiO2 1.011–13 kPa (on air)
Lactate6.1 mmol/L0.5–2.0 mmol/L
Sodium139 mmol/L135–145 mmol/L
Water sourceFreshwater lake—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this gas and correlate it with drowning-induced lung injury.
  2. Does the fact this was freshwater (rather than saltwater) change your expected electrolyte picture or management? Discuss.
  3. Outline your supportive management priorities.
  4. What factors would inform your approach to neuro-prognostication?
Reveal model answer
  • Severe hypoxaemic respiratory failure (markedly reduced PaO2 despite FiO2 1.0) with a mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis (raised PaCO2, raised lactate from hypoxia/hypoperfusion during submersion) — consistent with drowning-induced lung injury (surfactant washout and aspiration causing an ARDS-like picture).
  • No — this is a classic exam myth. The historical teaching that freshwater aspiration causes haemodilution/hyponatraemia/haemolysis while saltwater causes hypertonic pulmonary oedema/hypernatraemia derives from animal studies using massive aspirated volumes. In real human drowning victims, aspirated volumes are typically too small to cause clinically significant electrolyte or volume shifts, and management is essentially identical regardless of water type — sodium here is normal, consistent with this.
  • Supportive management: lung-protective ventilation (similar principles to ARDS), treat hypothermia if present (common in drowning), and avoid routine prophylactic antibiotics/steroids unless there is clear evidence of infection or aspiration of grossly contaminated water.
  • Neuro-prognostication should follow similar principles to post-cardiac arrest care: duration of submersion, water temperature, time to CPR/ROSC, and avoidance of premature prognostication — using a multimodal assessment after appropriate rewarming and an adequate waiting period.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct ABG interpretation/mechanism (4), correct dismissal of the freshwater/saltwater myth (4), correct supportive management (2), correct prognostication considerations (2).

Guideline-drift trap: The freshwater vs saltwater electrolyte distinction is a classic exam myth still taught in some sources. In real human drowning, aspirated volumes are generally too small to cause clinically significant electrolyte derangement, and management is essentially the same regardless of water type — this reflects outdated, animal-study-derived teaching rather than current evidence in human drowning victims.

References

  1. Szpilman D, Bierens JJLM, Handley AJ, Orlowski JP. Drowning. N Engl J Med. 2012;366:2102–2110.

CBS_BIO_21 — Peripartum DIC in Amniotic Fluid Embolism

Biochemistry / Data Obstetric / Peri-partum CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

Following return of spontaneous circulation after suspected amniotic fluid embolism (see CCS_S28), a coagulation panel is sent.

ParameterResultReference range
Platelets35 ×109/L150–400 ×109/L
PT28 s (control 12 s)10–14 s
APTT65 s (control 30 s)25–35 s
Fibrinogen0.4 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
D-dimer>20 mg/L FEU<0.5 mg/L FEU

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this panel and note its most striking feature.
  2. Explain why AFE causes a particularly severe coagulopathy compared with other obstetric DIC triggers.
  3. Outline your haemostatic resuscitation approach.
  4. How does the speed and severity of this coagulopathy change your practical approach compared with atonic postpartum haemorrhage?
Reveal model answer
  • Severe, rapid-onset DIC — thrombocytopenia, markedly prolonged PT/APTT, very high D-dimer. The most striking feature is the extremely low fibrinogen (0.4 g/L).
  • Amniotic fluid/fetal antigens entering the maternal circulation trigger both an anaphylactoid-type response and massive activation of the coagulation/complement cascades, causing a profound, rapid consumptive coagulopathy — mechanistically distinct from "classic" thromboplastin-driven obstetric DIC (e.g. from abruption), though the endpoint coagulopathy looks similar.
  • Aggressive, early, massive-transfusion-protocol-level factor replacement — cryoprecipitate/ fibrinogen concentrate targeting fibrinogen, FFP, and platelets — guided by viscoelastic testing where available (see CCS_L09 for general massive obstetric haemorrhage principles).
  • The severity and speed of coagulopathy in AFE characteristically exceeds that seen in many other obstetric haemorrhage triggers, meaning standard massive transfusion ratios/timing may need to be exceeded or accelerated given how rapidly fibrinogen and other factors are consumed — this should prompt very early, aggressive haemostatic resuscitation rather than reactive treatment.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct DIC interpretation with fibrinogen emphasis (4), correct AFE-specific mechanism (3), correct haemostatic resuscitation approach (3), correct recognition of AFE's disproportionate severity/ speed (2).

Common error / drift trap: The severity and speed of coagulopathy in AFE is sometimes underestimated compared to more common obstetric haemorrhage causes — standard massive transfusion ratios/timing may need to be exceeded or accelerated given how quickly fibrinogen is consumed in AFE specifically.

References

  1. Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine. Amniotic fluid embolism: diagnosis and management. Am J Obstet Gynecol. 2016;215(2):B16–B24.

CBS_BIO_22 — SOFA Score Calculation and Interpretation

Biochemistry / Data General / Miscellaneous CoBaTrICE 2·3·11 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with septic shock has the following parameters recorded on ICU day 1.

ParameterResult
PaO2/FiO2180 mmHg (ventilated)
Platelets85 ×109/L
Bilirubin28 µmol/L
Mean arterial pressure / vasopressorsNoradrenaline 0.15 micrograms/kg/min
GCS12
Creatinine / urine output220 µmol/L; urine output 0.4 mL/kg/h

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the SOFA score component by component.
  2. What is SOFA's role in the formal Sepsis-3 definition of sepsis?
  3. Is a single SOFA value or the trend over time more prognostically useful?
  4. Should SOFA be used as a standalone tool to triage or allocate ICU resources? Explain.
Reveal model answer
  • Working through each domain (0–4 points each): respiratory (P/F 180 on ventilation → 2), coagulation (platelets 85 → 2), hepatic (bilirubin 28 → 1), cardiovascular (noradrenaline <0.1 micrograms/kg/min would score 2, but at 0.15 → 3), neurological (GCS 12 → 2), renal (creatinine 220 with oliguria → 2–3) — giving an overall SOFA score reflecting significant multi-organ dysfunction (approximate total in the low-to-mid teens, exact banding per local scoring table).
  • Sepsis-3 defines sepsis as life-threatening organ dysfunction indicated by an acute change in SOFA score ≥2 points from baseline in the context of infection.
  • The trend ("delta SOFA") over time is generally more prognostically informative than a single value — worsening or improving trajectory better reflects response to treatment than one snapshot.
  • No — SOFA was designed and validated as a research/epidemiological tool for describing and tracking organ dysfunction, not as a standalone bedside triage or resource-allocation tool. It has limited accuracy for predicting individual patient outcomes, and using it in isolation to deny or withdraw treatment (as proposed in some crisis-standards-of-care protocols) is controversial and not how it was validated to be used — it should inform, not replace, holistic clinical judgement.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct component-by-component calculation (5), correct Sepsis-3 role (2), correct trend-over-single-value emphasis (2), correct, evidence-based refusal to endorse standalone triage use (3).

Common error / drift trap: SOFA is sometimes proposed as a standalone triage/ resource-allocation tool. It was validated as a research/epidemiological tool with limited accuracy for individual outcome prediction — using it alone for triage decisions is controversial and not how it was designed to be used.

References

  1. Singer M, Deutschman CS, Seymour CW, et al. The Third International Consensus Definitions for Sepsis and Septic Shock (Sepsis-3). JAMA. 2016;315(8):801–810.
  2. Vincent JL, Moreno R, Takala J, et al. The SOFA (Sepsis-related Organ Failure Assessment) score. Intensive Care Med. 1996;22(7):707–710.

CBS_BIO_23 — Acute Promyelocytic Leukaemia: Blood Count and Coagulation Pattern

Biochemistry / Data Haematology / Oncology CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 30-year-old woman presents with bruising, bleeding gums, and petechiae. Blood film shows abnormal promyelocytes with Auer rods.

ParameterResultReference range
White cell count3.2 ×109/L4.0–11.0 ×109/L
Haemoglobin78 g/L120–150 g/L
Platelets18 ×109/L150–400 ×109/L
PT22 s (control 12 s)10–14 s
APTT45 s (control 30 s)25–35 s
Fibrinogen0.8 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
D-dimerMarkedly elevated<0.5 mg/L FEU
Blood filmAbnormal promyelocytes, Auer rods—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this pattern and state the likely diagnosis.
  2. What makes the associated coagulopathy a medical emergency?
  3. Would you wait for genetic confirmation before starting definitive treatment?
  4. What supportive measures and treatment-related complication would you monitor for?
Reveal model answer
  • Pancytopenia with abnormal promyelocytes and Auer rods on film strongly suggests acute promyelocytic leukaemia (APL), a subtype of AML with t(15;17) PML-RARA translocation, combined with severe coagulopathy (low fibrinogen, prolonged PT/APTT, high D-dimer) — DIC, a hallmark, life-threatening complication of APL at presentation.
  • APL-associated coagulopathy carries high early mortality, largely from fatal haemorrhage (especially intracranial), if not treated immediately; it can be transiently worsened by chemotherapy-induced cell lysis if standard chemotherapy is used before differentiation therapy.
  • No — start ATRA (all-trans retinoic acid) immediately on clinical and morphological suspicion of APL, without waiting for genetic confirmation of the PML-RARA translocation. ATRA specifically targets the underlying leukaemic differentiation block and rapidly improves the coagulopathy — delaying treatment while awaiting confirmatory cytogenetics significantly increases early mortality risk.
  • Aggressive blood product support (platelets, cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen, FFP) to keep platelets and fibrinogen above defined thresholds during induction; monitor for differentiation syndrome (a separate ATRA-related complication — fever, dyspnoea, pulmonary infiltrates, fluid retention), managed with steroids and sometimes temporary ATRA interruption.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct interpretation/diagnosis (4), correct coagulopathy severity/emergency status (2), correct "start ATRA immediately, do not wait" answer (4), correct supportive care/differentiation syndrome monitoring (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Waiting for genetic/cytogenetic confirmation before starting ATRA is a dangerous, well-recognised error — ATRA should start immediately on clinical and morphological suspicion, because early treatment is what corrects the life-threatening coagulopathy, and any delay significantly increases early mortality.

References

  1. Sanz MA, Fenaux P, Tallman MS, et al. Management of acute promyelocytic leukemia: updated recommendations from an expert panel of the European LeukemiaNet. Blood. 2019;133(15):1630–1643.

CBS_BIO_24 — Phaeochromocytoma: Catecholamine Biochemistry and Perioperative Risk

Biochemistry / Data Endocrine / Metabolic-toxic CoBaTrICE 2·3·4·6 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old man has episodic headaches, palpitations, diaphoresis, and labile hypertension. Biochemistry is sent, and surgical resection is being planned.

ParameterResultReference range
Plasma free metanephrineMarkedly elevatedWithin normal limits
Plasma free normetanephrineMarkedly elevatedWithin normal limits
24-hour urinary catecholaminesElevatedWithin normal limits
Blood pressureLabile, episodes to 220/130 mmHg—

Structured questions

  1. Why are plasma free metanephrines the preferred first-line screening test?
  2. What factors could cause a false-positive result, particularly relevant in a critically ill patient?
  3. Outline the critical, correctly-sequenced perioperative pharmacological preparation.
  4. What happens if this sequence is reversed?
Reveal model answer
  • Plasma free metanephrines are produced continuously within the tumour regardless of episodic secretion, giving higher sensitivity than measuring catecholamines alone, which are released episodically and can be missed between episodes.
  • False positives: certain drugs (tricyclic antidepressants, some antipsychotics, levodopa), acute severe illness/critical illness itself (an important caveat for cases diagnosed incidentally in ICU), and improper sampling technique (should be supine, resting, avoiding caffeine).
  • Alpha-blockade (phenoxybenzamine or a selective alpha-1 blocker) must be established and adequately titrated first, with pre-operative volume expansion (chronic catecholamine excess causes volume contraction); beta-blockade is added afterward only if needed for reflex tachycardia.
  • Giving beta-blockade before adequate alpha-blockade leaves alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, precipitating a hypertensive crisis — a classic, dangerous prescribing error.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct rationale for plasma free metanephrines (3), false-positive factors named (3), correct alpha-then-beta sequence with volume expansion (4), correct consequence of reversal (2).

Common error / drift trap: Giving a beta-blocker before adequate alpha-blockade in a patient with known or suspected phaeochromocytoma is a classic, dangerous prescribing error — this leaves alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, precipitating a hypertensive crisis.

References

  1. Lenders JW, Duh QY, Eisenhofer G, et al. Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline. J Clin Endocrinol Metab. 2014;99(6):1915–1942.

CBS_BIO_25 — Severity Scoring in Acute Pancreatitis: Glasgow-Imrie Criteria

Biochemistry / Data GI / Hepatic / Pancreas CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

A 55-year-old man with acute pancreatitis (see CCS_S10) has bloods repeated at 48 hours for severity scoring.

ParameterResultAdverse threshold
Age58 years>55 years
PaO27.5 kPa<8 kPa
White cell count18 ×109/L>15 ×109/L
Corrected calcium1.9 mmol/L<2.0 mmol/L
Urea18 mmol/L>16 mmol/L
LDH720 U/L>600 U/L
Albumin28 g/L<32 g/L
Glucose12 mmol/L>10 mmol/L

Structured questions

  1. Calculate this patient's Glasgow-Imrie score and interpret it.
  2. What is a key practical limitation of this score?
  3. How does this relate to the revised Atlanta classification discussed in CCS_S10?
  4. What tools would you use for earlier risk stratification, before 48 hours?
Reveal model answer
  • All 8 criteria are met (age, PaO2, WCC, calcium, urea, LDH, albumin, glucose — mnemonic "PANCREAS") — a Glasgow-Imrie score of 8/8. A score ≥3 indicates severe pancreatitis with significantly increased complication/mortality risk; this patient scores at the very severe end.
  • Key limitation: Glasgow-Imrie (like Ranson's) requires 48 hours to fully calculate, delaying severity stratification when early risk assessment matters most.
  • Scoring systems support early risk stratification/triage decisions, but the revised Atlanta classification (based on persistent organ failure at 48 hours) remains the definitive severity classification once that information is available (see CCS_S10).
  • Earlier tools: APACHE II can be calculated immediately on admission and repeated, and BISAP is calculable within 24 hours — both are preferred for guiding initial triage/escalation decisions while awaiting the 48-hour score.

Common error / drift trap: Waiting for the 48-hour Glasgow-Imrie score before making any severity-related decisions delays care for genuinely severe patients — earlier tools (APACHE II, BISAP) should guide initial triage and escalation decisions.
