CBS_BIO_26 — Oxygenation Index and P/F Ratio in ARDS Severity Staging

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A ventilated ARDS patient is assessed before and after a session of prone positioning.

ParameterBefore proningAfter proning
FiO20.60.5
PaO29 kPa (68 mmHg)12 kPa (90 mmHg)
PEEP10 cmH2O10 cmH2O
Mean airway pressure14 cmH2O12 cmH2O

Structured questions

  1. Calculate the P/F ratio at both time points and classify ARDS severity per the current definition.
  2. Calculate the oxygenation index (OI) at both time points. What does it add beyond the P/F ratio?
  3. Interpret the trend shown and its clinical significance.
  4. In what context is OI particularly used to guide escalation decisions?
Reveal model answer
  • Before: P/F = 68/0.6 ≈ 113 mmHg (severe ARDS). After: P/F = 90/0.5 = 180 mmHg (moderate ARDS) — a meaningful improvement in severity category. This severity banding (mild 201–300, moderate 101–200, severe ≤100 mmHg on PEEP ≥5 cmH2O) was set by the 2012 Berlin Definition and is unchanged for intubated patients under the 2023 Global Definition of ARDS (Matthay et al.), which updated Berlin mainly by extending the diagnosis to non-intubated patients on high-flow nasal oxygen and by accepting lung ultrasound as an imaging modality — neither change alters this ventilated patient's classification, but citing only "Berlin" without acknowledging the 2023 update is itself a citation-currency error.
  • OI = (FiO2 × 100 × mean airway pressure) / PaO2 (mmHg). Before: (0.6×100×14)/68 ≈ 12.4. After: (0.5×100×12)/90 ≈ 6.7. OI incorporates the mean airway pressure required to achieve the oxygenation, giving a measure of the "cost" of oxygenation in ventilatory support intensity — two patients with the same P/F ratio but different PEEP/MAP requirements have different OI and arguably different severity/support burden.
  • Both metrics improve together after proning, supporting continued use of the intervention — both should be trended alongside clinical context rather than relying on a single number at a single time point.
  • OI is historically more used in paediatric/neonatal ARDS and in ECMO referral criteria in some settings, complementing P/F-ratio-based criteria such as those informing the EOLIA-based referral thresholds discussed in CCS_L01.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct P/F calculation and severity classification (4), correct OI calculation and added-value explanation (5), correct trend interpretation (2), correct context for OI use (1).

Common error / drift trap: Relying purely on P/F ratio without considering the level of ventilatory support required to achieve it can be misleading — two patients with an identical P/F ratio but very different PEEP/MAP requirements have different severity and prognosis, which OI helps capture.
Guideline-drift trap: Naming only the 2012 Berlin Definition without acknowledging the 2023 Global Definition of ARDS (Matthay et al.) that superseded it is a citation- currency error the exam expects candidates to avoid, even when — as here, for an intubated patient — the substantive severity thresholds are unchanged.

References

  1. Matthay MA, Arabi Y, Arroliga AC, et al. A New Global Definition of Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2024;209(1):37–47.
  2. ARDS Definition Task Force, Ranieri VM, Rubenfeld GD, et al. Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome: the Berlin Definition. JAMA. 2012;307(23):2526–2533. (superseded by the 2023 Global Definition above; retained here for historical/exam-recall context)

CBS_BIO_27 — BNP/NT-proBNP in Undifferentiated Dyspnoea

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 75-year-old woman with known CKD (eGFR 35) presents with acute undifferentiated dyspnoea. NT-proBNP is sent.

ParameterResultReference range
NT-proBNP8500 pg/mLAge-dependent; typically <125–450 pg/mL
eGFR35 mL/min/1.73m2>90 mL/min/1.73m2
Heart rhythmSinus—

Structured questions

  1. Interpret this markedly elevated NT-proBNP. Does it confirm acute heart failure?
  2. What confounders can cause a false or misleading elevation?
  3. Is natriuretic peptide testing more useful for ruling in or ruling out heart failure?
  4. How would you practically apply this result in her assessment?
Reveal model answer
  • An elevated NT-proBNP supports but does not confirm a cardiac cause of dyspnoea in isolation — it must be interpreted alongside the full clinical picture.
  • Confounders causing elevation independent of (or in addition to) heart failure: renal impairment (reduces clearance — directly relevant here given her CKD), age, atrial fibrillation, sepsis/critical illness, pulmonary embolism, and COPD with cor pulmonale.
  • Natriuretic peptides have their best diagnostic utility when low — a low value has a very high negative predictive value and effectively excludes acute heart failure as the cause in a patient with reasonable pre-test probability. A high value is less specific, especially with confounders present.
  • In this patient with significant CKD, interpret the elevated result cautiously — use age/renal-adjusted thresholds or trend where possible, and correlate with clinical examination, chest X-ray, and echocardiography for a confident diagnosis rather than treating the number as a standalone positive test.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct "supports but does not confirm" interpretation (3), confounders named including renal impairment (4), correct rule-out-vs-rule-in distinction (3), correct practical application (2).

Common error / drift trap: A markedly elevated NT-proBNP is sometimes interpreted as confirming acute heart failure in isolation, without considering confounders such as renal impairment — natriuretic peptides have their best utility for ruling OUT heart failure when low, and an elevated value in a patient with CKD requires cautious interpretation alongside the full clinical picture.

References

  1. McDonagh TA, Metra M, Adamo M, et al. 2021 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and treatment of acute and chronic heart failure. Eur Heart J. 2021;42(36):3599–3726.

CBS_BIO_28 — Critical Illness-Related Corticosteroid Insufficiency: The Cortisol Testing Controversy

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 2·3·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with septic shock remains on noradrenaline 0.4 micrograms/kg/min despite adequate resuscitation. The team debates whether to check a random cortisol or perform a short synacthen test before starting hydrocortisone.

Structured questions

  1. Explain the concept of critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency (CIRCI).
  2. Why is routine cortisol/synacthen testing before starting hydrocortisone no longer recommended?
  3. What should actually drive the decision to start hydrocortisone in this scenario?
  4. What is the practical implication for this patient right now?
Reveal model answer
  • CIRCI describes relative adrenal insufficiency/tissue glucocorticoid resistance during critical illness — historically diagnosed via the short synacthen (ACTH stimulation) test, looking for an inadequate cortisol rise.
  • Routine testing is no longer recommended by current guidelines (Surviving Sepsis Campaign, SCCM/ESICM CIRCI guidance) because total cortisol measurement is unreliable in critical illness (altered protein binding from low albumin, assay variability), free cortisol is not routinely available, and the synacthen test has poor reproducibility/predictive value for who benefits from steroids in septic shock.
  • The decision should be based on the clinical scenario — refractory shock with an ongoing significant vasopressor requirement (as discussed in CCS_S01) — not a biochemical test result.
  • Do not delay starting hydrocortisone in refractory septic shock while awaiting or performing a synacthen test; if a random cortisol is checked as part of a broader work-up, it should not gate the treatment decision.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct CIRCI concept (3), correct rationale for abandoning routine testing (4), correct clinical-scenario-based decision criterion (3), correct "do not delay treatment" practical answer (2).

Guideline-drift trap: Ordering and awaiting a short synacthen test or random cortisol level before starting hydrocortisone in refractory septic shock is outdated practice — current guidelines specifically recommend against routine adrenal function testing to guide this decision, since these tests are unreliable in critical illness.

References

  1. Evans L, Rhodes A, Alhazzani W, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2021. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47:1181–1247.
  2. Annane D, Pastores SM, Rochwerg B, et al. Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Management of Critical Illness-Related Corticosteroid Insufficiency (CIRCI). Crit Care Med. 2017;45(12):2078–2088.

CBS_BIO_39 — Dead Space Fraction (Vd/Vt) as an ARDS Prognostic Marker

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 50-year-old man with moderate ARDS has a calculated dead space fraction (Vd/Vt), derived from volumetric capnography, of 0.68 on day 2 of mechanical ventilation.

ParameterValueReference/interpretation
Vd/Vt (Bohr-Enghoff, corrected)0.68Normal ~0.3; elevated in ARDS
PaCO26.1 kPa (46 mmHg)Mildly elevated
P/F ratio145Moderate ARDS
Minute ventilation required12 L/minTo maintain permissive hypercapnia target

Structured questions

  1. How is dead space fraction calculated, and what does an elevated value represent physiologically?
  2. Summarise the evidence linking early elevated Vd/Vt to mortality in ARDS.
  3. How does an elevated Vd/Vt influence ventilator management decisions in a lung-protective strategy?
Reveal model answer
  • Calculation/physiology: Vd/Vt is calculated using the Bohr-Enghoff equation, (PaCO2 − PECO2)/PaCO2, where PECO2 is mixed expired CO2 (measured via volumetric capnography). It quantifies the proportion of each breath that does not participate in gas exchange (alveolar dead space plus anatomical dead space), reflecting ventilation-perfusion mismatch from microvascular thrombosis/obstruction, alveolar overdistension, and reduced pulmonary capillary blood flow — all prominent in ARDS pathophysiology.
  • Evidence/mortality link: Nuckton et al. (NEJM 2002) demonstrated in a prospective cohort that an elevated dead space fraction measured early in ARDS was an independent predictor of mortality, with each 0.05 increase in Vd/Vt associated with an increased odds of death; this remains one of the most robust physiological (non-oxygenation-based) prognostic markers in ARDS, reflecting the severity of the underlying vascular injury rather than just the degree of shunt/ hypoxaemia captured by the P/F ratio.
  • Ventilator implications: a high Vd/Vt means a larger minute ventilation is required to clear the same amount of CO2, which can create tension with lung-protective ventilation (low tidal volume, driving pressure <15 cmH2O) — this is one of the physiological drivers behind accepting permissive hypercapnia (targeting a higher, tolerated PaCO2/lower pH within accepted limits) rather than increasing tidal volume or plateau pressure to "chase" a normal PaCO2 in severe ARDS.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct calculation/physiology (4), correct evidence summary with named study (4), correct ventilator implications including permissive hypercapnia rationale (4).

Common error / drift trap: Increasing tidal volume above lung-protective limits specifically to normalise PaCO2 in a patient with a high dead space fraction. Permissive hypercapnia (accepting a higher PaCO2, monitoring pH) is the evidence-consistent approach when Vd/Vt is elevated, preserving lung-protective ventilation targets.

References

  1. Nuckton TJ, Alonso JA, Kallet RH, et al. Pulmonary dead-space fraction as a risk factor for death in the acute respiratory distress syndrome. N Engl J Med. 2002;346(17):1281–1286.
  2. Grasselli G, Calfee CS, Camporota L, et al. ESICM guidelines: mechanical ventilation in ARDS. Intensive Care Med. 2023;49(7):727–759.

CBS_BIO_40 — Carbon Monoxide and Cyanide in Smoke Inhalation: Why Pulse Oximetry Lies

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·2 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 34-year-old man is rescued from a house fire, found in an enclosed room with soot around the mouth and nose. He is confused with an SpO2 reading of 98% on room air. Co-oximetry arterial blood gas and lactate are shown.

ParameterValueReference range
Pulse oximetry SpO298%Falsely reassuring
Carboxyhaemoglobin (CO-Hb)32%<3% (non-smoker), <10% (smoker)
Lactate9.8 mmol/L0.5–2.2 mmol/L
pH7.187.35–7.45
PaO213.5 kPa (dissolved O2, on high-flow O2)Normal or high — measures dissolved O2 only

Structured questions

  1. Why is standard pulse oximetry unreliable in this scenario, and what test should be used instead?
  2. What does the markedly elevated lactate in the context of a normal-looking PaO2 suggest, and what is the specific antidote to consider?
  3. Outline the mechanism and management of carbon monoxide poisoning, including the role of hyperbaric oxygen.
Reveal model answer
  • Pulse oximetry unreliability: standard two-wavelength pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhaemoglobin from oxyhaemoglobin (both absorb light similarly at the wavelengths used) and therefore reports a falsely normal/high SpO2 despite significantly reduced functional oxygen-carrying capacity. Co-oximetry (multi-wavelength arterial or venous blood gas analysis, or a dedicated pulse CO-oximeter) is required to directly measure carboxyhaemoglobin and correctly identify significant CO poisoning.
  • Elevated lactate/antidote: a markedly elevated lactate (>8–10 mmol/L, "lactate gap" out of proportion to other findings) in a smoke-inhalation patient is a well-established marker of concomitant cyanide toxicity (from combustion of nitrogen-containing synthetic materials such as polyurethane), reflecting inhibition of mitochondrial cytochrome c oxidase and a forced shift to anaerobic metabolism; hydroxocobalamin is the first-line specific antidote (preferred over sodium thiosulfate/nitrites due to its more favourable side-effect profile and lack of methaemoglobin induction, which would further compromise oxygen-carrying capacity already reduced by carboxyhaemoglobin) and should be given empirically on strong clinical suspicion without waiting for a confirmatory cyanide level, given the delay in obtaining results.
  • CO poisoning management: CO binds hameoglobin with roughly 200–250 times the affinity of oxygen, shifting the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve leftward (impairing peripheral oxygen unloading) in addition to reducing oxygen-carrying capacity; high-flow 100% oxygen reduces the CO-Hb half-life from around 4–6 hours (room air) to roughly 60–90 minutes, and should be started immediately. Hyperbaric oxygen further reduces this half-life (to around 20–30 minutes) and may reduce delayed neurological sequelae; its use is considered for severe poisoning (CO-Hb >25%, loss of consciousness, significant neurological/cardiac features, or pregnancy with CO-Hb >15%, given fetal haemoglobin's higher CO affinity), though evidence for definitive outcome benefit remains mixed across trials and its use should be guided by local availability and toxicology/hyperbaric specialist input.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct pulse oximetry explanation and correct test (4), correct cyanide reasoning and antidote (4), correct CO management including hyperbaric considerations (4).

Guideline-drift trap: Reassuring a smoke-inhalation patient based on a normal SpO2 reading, or waiting for a confirmatory cyanide level before giving hydroxocobalamin. Both are recognised causes of preventable death in this scenario — pulse oximetry can be dangerously misleading, and cyanide antidote therapy should be empirical when suspicion is high.

References

  1. Weaver LK. Carbon monoxide poisoning. N Engl J Med. 2009;360(12):1217–1225.
  2. Baud FJ, Barriot P, Toffis V, et al. Elevated blood cyanide concentrations in victims of smoke inhalation. N Engl J Med. 1991;325(25):1761–1766.

CBS_BIO_41 — Methaemoglobinaemia: The Saturation Gap

Biochemistry / Data Respiratory CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·2 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 60-year-old man is treated with topical benzocaine spray for an awake fibreoptic intubation. Shortly afterward, he becomes cyanotic and hypoxic with a "chocolate brown" appearance to his arterial blood, despite a pulse oximeter reading of 88% that does not improve with 100% oxygen.

ParameterValueReference range
Pulse oximetry SpO288% (fixed, unresponsive to FiO2)Characteristic ~85% plateau
Co-oximetry SaO2 (calculated)99%Saturation "gap" vs SpO2 = key clue
Methaemoglobin28%<1–2%
PaO228 kPa (on 100% O2)Normal/high — dissolved O2 unaffected

Structured questions

  1. Explain the pathophysiology of methaemoglobinaemia and why pulse oximetry characteristically plateaus around 85% regardless of true oxygenation.
  2. What is the specific antidote, its mechanism, and an important contraindication to its use?
  3. Name two other common precipitants of acquired methaemoglobinaemia besides benzocaine.
Reveal model answer
  • Pathophysiology: methaemoglobin forms when the ferrous (Fe2+) iron of haemoglobin is oxidised to the ferric (Fe3+) state, which cannot bind oxygen and also shifts the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve of the remaining normal haemoglobin leftward, further impairing oxygen delivery. Standard two-wavelength pulse oximetry misinterprets methaemoglobin's absorbance characteristics and characteristically converges toward approximately 85% regardless of the true arterial oxygen saturation, producing a persistent "saturation gap" between the (falsely low/fixed) pulse oximetry reading and the true, typically much higher, co-oximetry-measured oxyhaemoglobin saturation.
  • Antidote: intravenous methylene blue (1–2 mg/kg over several minutes) acts as an electron acceptor via the NADPH-methaemoglobin reductase pathway, accelerating reduction of methaemoglobin back to functional haemoglobin. It is contraindicated (or requires extreme caution) in patients with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency, as methylene blue relies on NADPH generated via the pentose phosphate pathway and can precipitate severe haemolysis in G6PD-deficient patients; it should also be avoided/used cautiously with serotonergic drugs due to risk of serotonin syndrome (methylene blue is a monoamine oxidase inhibitor).
  • Other precipitants: dapsone, nitrates/nitrites (including inhaled nitric oxide and nitroglycerin at high doses), and other local anaesthetics (prilocaine, particularly notable) are all recognised causes of acquired methaemoglobinaemia; a careful medication history is essential in any patient with unexplained cyanosis and a normal PaO2.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct pathophysiology and saturation-gap explanation (5), correct antidote/mechanism/ contraindication (5), two correct other precipitants (2).

Common error / drift trap: Giving methylene blue without considering G6PD status in a patient from a population with higher G6PD deficiency prevalence, or attributing persistent hypoxia purely to a respiratory cause without recognising the classic "saturation gap" pattern pointing to methaemoglobinaemia.

References

  1. Ash-Bernal R, Wise R, Wright SM. Acquired methemoglobinemia: a retrospective series of 138 cases. Medicine (Baltimore). 2004;83(5):265–273.
  2. Skold A, Cosco DL, Klein R. Methemoglobinemia: pathogenesis, diagnosis, and management. South Med J. 2011;104(11):757–761.

CBS_BIO_42 — Venous-to-Arterial CO2 Gap as a Marker of Occult Perfusion Inadequacy

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 3·2 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A post-cardiac-surgery patient has a central venous catheter and arterial line. Despite a "normal" ScvO2 of 74% and a normal lactate, the Pv-aCO2 (central venous-to-arterial CO2) gap is elevated.

ParameterValueReference range
ScvO274%>70% (often considered reassuring)
Lactate1.6 mmol/L0.5–2.2 mmol/L
PvCO2 (central venous)7.2 kPa (54 mmHg)—
PaCO2 (arterial)5.2 kPa (39 mmHg)—
Pv-aCO2 gap2.0 kPa (15 mmHg)Normal <6 mmHg (~0.8 kPa)

Structured questions

  1. What does an elevated Pv-aCO2 gap indicate physiologically, and why can it be abnormal despite a "reassuring" ScvO2 and normal lactate?
  2. How can the Pv-aCO2/arteriovenous oxygen content difference ratio refine this interpretation further?
  3. What is the practical clinical utility of trending this gap during resuscitation?
Reveal model answer
  • Physiology/why it can be abnormal alone: the Pv-aCO2 gap widens when cardiac output/microcirculatory blood flow is inadequate to washout tissue-produced CO2, reflecting the Fick principle applied to CO2 clearance (CO2 production is relatively constant, so a low flow state increases the venous-arterial CO2 difference even before global oxygen delivery falls low enough to drop ScvO2 or raise lactate) — it can therefore identify subclinical or "occult" flow inadequacy that a single ScvO2/lactate value has missed, particularly relevant in patients where oxygen extraction can transiently normalise ScvO2 despite an underlying low-flow state.
  • Refinement with the ratio: the Pv-aCO2/C(a-v)O2 (arteriovenous oxygen content difference) ratio helps distinguish a purely low-flow (anaerobic/hypoperfusion) state from a predominantly aerobic hypermetabolic state — a ratio >1.4–1.6 suggests a significant anaerobic component to CO2 production (i.e., genuine tissue hypoperfusion), whereas a normal ratio despite an elevated absolute gap can reflect increased CO2 production without true anaerobiosis.
  • Practical utility: trending the gap (rather than relying on a single value) during fluid or inotrope titration provides an additional, complementary marker of adequacy of resuscitation, especially valuable in patients (as here) where ScvO2 and lactate appear falsely reassuring — a persistently elevated or rising gap should prompt reassessment of cardiac output/fluid status rather than premature de-escalation of monitoring or support.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct physiology and "occult" flow-inadequacy explanation (5), correct ratio refinement (4), correct practical utility (3).

Common error / drift trap: Declaring resuscitation adequate based on ScvO2 and lactate alone without recognising that these can be falsely reassuring in early or compensated low- flow states — the Pv-aCO2 gap is a useful complementary, not competing, marker.

References

  1. Mallat J, Lemyze M, Meddour M, et al. Ratios of central venous-to-arterial carbon dioxide content or tension to arteriovenous oxygen content difference. Ann Intensive Care. 2016;6:10.
  2. Vallet B, Pinsky MR, Cecconi M. Resuscitation of patients with septic shock: please "mind the gap"! Intensive Care Med. 2013;39(9):1653–1655.

CBS_BIO_43 — Copeptin in Acute Coronary Syndrome: An Emerging, Not Established, Adjunct

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 58-year-old man presents 90 minutes after onset of chest pain. A single high-sensitivity troponin and a copeptin level are drawn at presentation.

ParameterValueReference/interpretation
hs-Troponin T (presentation)8 ng/LBelow the assay's 99th-percentile cut-off
Copeptin9 pmol/L<10 pmol/L generally considered low-risk in combination rule-out
ECGNo ischaemic changes—

Structured questions

  1. What is copeptin, and what was its originally proposed clinical role in chest pain assessment?
  2. How has the emergence of high-sensitivity troponin 0/1-hour and 0/2-hour rapid rule-out algorithms affected copeptin's clinical role?
  3. What is the current, evidence-appropriate way to characterise copeptin's role in most modern emergency departments?
Reveal model answer
  • Copeptin/original role: copeptin is the C-terminal fragment of pro-vasopressin, released in a stress response and rising rapidly (within minutes) after myocardial injury, in contrast to the slower rise of troponin. It was proposed as an early adjunct biomarker to a single baseline (non-high-sensitivity) troponin to accelerate safe rule-out of myocardial infarction in patients presenting early after symptom onset, when a single conventional troponin might still be falsely negative.
  • Impact of hs-troponin algorithms: the widespread adoption of validated high-sensitivity troponin 0/1-hour and 0/2-hour rapid rule-out/rule-in algorithms (endorsed in the 2023 ESC ACS guideline) achieves very high negative predictive value using troponin kinetics alone, substantially reducing the incremental diagnostic benefit that combining copeptin with an older, less sensitive troponin assay used to provide — most contemporary evidence and guidelines now centre around hs-troponin-only algorithms as the primary rapid rule-out pathway.
  • Current characterisation: copeptin should be described as a historically studied, biologically plausible adjunct that offered clearer added value in the era of standard (non-high-sensitivity) troponin assays, but which does not have a routine established role alongside modern hs-troponin protocols in current major guidelines; it remains used in some centres and specific populations, but is not a mainstream recommended addition to hs-troponin-based algorithms and candidates should avoid presenting it as a currently mandated or first-line test.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct copeptin definition/original role (4), correct explanation of how hs-troponin algorithms changed its relevance (5), correct balanced current characterisation (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Presenting copeptin plus a single troponin as the current gold-standard rapid rule-out strategy. The 2023 ESC ACS guideline (Byrne RA, et al. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(38):3720–3826) centres on validated hs-troponin 0/1h or 0/2h algorithms; copeptin's evidence base predates and has been substantially superseded by these hs-troponin protocols in most settings.

References

  1. Byrne RA, Rossello X, Coughlan JJ, et al. 2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes. Eur Heart J. 2023;44(38):3720–3826.
  2. Möckel M, Searle J, Hamm C, et al. Early discharge using single cardiac troponin and copeptin testing in patients with suspected acute coronary syndrome (Biomarkers in Cardiology 8). Eur Heart J. 2015;36(6):369–376.

CBS_BIO_44 — Amiodarone-Induced Thyroid Dysfunction: Type 1 vs Type 2

Biochemistry / Data Cardiovascular / Endocrine CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·1 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 70-year-old man on long-term amiodarone for atrial fibrillation develops new tachycardia, weight loss, and agitation. Thyroid function tests and thyroid ultrasound with Doppler are performed.

ParameterValueReference range
TSH<0.01 mU/L0.4–4.0 mU/L
Free T452 pmol/L9–25 pmol/L
Free T3Markedly elevated—
Interleukin-6 (IL-6)Mildly elevatedHigher in Type 2 (destructive thyroiditis)
Thyroid Doppler vascularityReduced/absentIncreased in Type 1, reduced in Type 2

Structured questions

  1. Distinguish the pathophysiology of Type 1 versus Type 2 amiodarone-induced thyrotoxicosis (AIT).
  2. Based on this patient's Doppler findings, which type is more likely, and what does this mean for treatment?
  3. Should amiodarone be stopped in this scenario? Justify your answer.
Reveal model answer
  • Type 1 vs Type 2: Type 1 AIT results from iodine-induced excess thyroid hormone synthesis in an abnormal thyroid gland (underlying nodular disease or latent Graves'), driven by the large iodine load in amiodarone — the gland is overactive, with increased vascularity on Doppler. Type 2 AIT is a destructive thyroiditis caused by amiodarone's direct cytotoxic effect on thyroid follicular cells, releasing preformed hormone from a structurally normal gland, with reduced or absent vascularity on Doppler and typically a more pronounced inflammatory marker (IL-6) elevation. Mixed forms also occur and can complicate classification.
  • This patient/treatment implication: reduced/absent Doppler vascularity points toward Type 2 (destructive) AIT, which is primarily managed with high-dose corticosteroids (targeting the thyroiditis) rather than antithyroid drugs (thionamides), since there is no excess synthesis for thionamides to block; Type 1, in contrast, is managed with high-dose thionamides (which may need higher doses than usual due to the iodine load) ± potassium perchlorate to reduce iodine uptake, with corticosteroids of limited benefit as monotherapy. Mixed/indeterminate cases are often treated empirically with a combination of both therapies.
  • Stopping amiodarone: whether to stop amiodarone is individualised — if it is being used for a life-threatening arrhythmia with no safe alternative, continuing it while treating the thyrotoxicosis directly is reasonable, since stopping does not rapidly resolve the thyrotoxicosis (amiodarone and its active metabolite have a very long half-life, meaning its thyroidal effects persist for weeks to months after discontinuation regardless); if an alternative antiarrhythmic is feasible and thyrotoxicosis is severe/refractory, discontinuation is often pursued in conjunction with endocrinology input, but is not an urgent isolated fix.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct Type 1/Type 2 pathophysiology distinction (5), correct treatment implication based on findings (4), correct nuanced answer on stopping amiodarone (3).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming amiodarone must always be stopped immediately upon diagnosis of AIT, or that stopping it will rapidly resolve the thyrotoxicosis. Amiodarone's very long tissue half-life means its effects persist long after cessation, and the decision to stop is individualised against arrhythmia risk, not a reflexive step.

References

  1. Bartalena L, Bogazzi F, Chiovato L, et al. 2018 European Thyroid Association Guideline for the Management of Amiodarone-Associated Thyroid Dysfunction. Eur Thyroid J. 2018;7(2):55–66.
  2. Cohen-Lehman J, Dahl P, Danzi S, Klein I. Effects of amiodarone therapy on thyroid function. Nat Rev Endocrinol. 2010;6(1):34–41.

CBS_BIO_45 — Non-Hepatic Hyperammonaemia: A Diagnostic Trap

Biochemistry / Data Neurological CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·5 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 45-year-old woman on long-term valproate for epilepsy is admitted with acute confusion and reduced consciousness. Liver function tests are entirely normal. Ammonia and other relevant results are shown.

ParameterValueReference range
Ammonia135 µmol/L11–35 µmol/L
ALT/ASTNormal—
BilirubinNormal—
INR1.00.9–1.1
Valproate levelTherapeutic range—

Structured questions

  1. How does this biochemical picture distinguish this presentation from hepatic encephalopathy?
  2. What is the mechanism by which valproate causes hyperammonaemia despite normal liver function and a therapeutic drug level?
  3. What is the specific treatment, and what other important causes of non-hepatic hyperammonaemia should be considered in a critically ill adult?
Reveal model answer
  • Distinction from hepatic encephalopathy: hepatic encephalopathy requires significant hepatic synthetic dysfunction or portosystemic shunting, typically reflected by abnormal liver enzymes, coagulopathy (elevated INR), and/or known cirrhosis/portal hypertension. The entirely normal liver function tests and INR here argue strongly against a hepatic cause, and should prompt consideration of a non-hepatic aetiology despite the elevated ammonia — ammonia elevation is not synonymous with liver failure.
  • Valproate mechanism: valproate inhibits carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I and depletes hepatic carnitine and other urea-cycle intermediates (particularly N-acetylglutamate), impairing the urea cycle's capacity to detoxify ammonia even in the presence of structurally normal liver, and can occur at therapeutic (not just supratherapeutic) drug levels, particularly in patients with an underlying subclinical urea-cycle enzyme deficiency or on polytherapy with other enzyme-inducing anticonvulsants.
  • Treatment/other causes: stopping valproate, supplementing L-carnitine (which facilitates ammonia detoxification and mitochondrial fatty-acid metabolism), and supportive care (including consideration of haemodialysis for severe cases with very high ammonia and depressed consciousness) are the mainstays. Other important non-hepatic causes to consider in a critically ill adult include urease-producing urinary tract infections with obstruction/urinary diversion, post-liver-transplant "idiopathic" hyperammonaemia, total parenteral nutrition-related and gastrointestinal bleeding-related nitrogen loads, and rare late-onset inherited urea-cycle disorders unmasked by physiological stress (e.g., ornithine transcarbamylase deficiency).
Marking guide

12 marks: correct distinction from hepatic encephalopathy (4), correct valproate mechanism (4), two or more correct alternative non-hepatic causes plus treatment (4).

Common error / drift trap: Assuming any elevated ammonia in a confused patient implies hepatic encephalopathy and pursuing a hepatic workup while missing a reversible drug-induced cause. Normal liver function tests should actively prompt consideration of non-hepatic hyperammonaemia, especially with a relevant drug history.

References

  1. Lheureux PE, Hantson P. Carnitine in the treatment of valproic acid-induced toxicity. Clin Toxicol (Phila). 2009;47(2):101–111.
  2. Clay AS, Hainline BE. Hyperammonemia in the ICU. Chest. 2007;132(4):1368–1378.

CBS_BIO_46 — Presepsin as a Sepsis Biomarker: Promising but Not Yet Established

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with suspected early sepsis has procalcitonin, CRP, and presepsin measured on admission as part of a research-linked biomarker panel.

BiomarkerValueEvidence maturity
CRP210 mg/LLong-established, nonspecific
Procalcitonin4.2 ng/mLEstablished, guideline-referenced (antibiotic stewardship)
Presepsin (soluble CD14 subtype)1450 pg/mLEmerging — rises earlier, less validated at scale

Structured questions

  1. What is presepsin, and what is its proposed kinetic advantage over CRP and procalcitonin?
  2. Summarise the current strength of evidence for presepsin's diagnostic and prognostic utility compared with the more established biomarkers.
  3. What is the current, most defensible exam answer regarding presepsin's role in routine sepsis management?
Reveal model answer
  • Definition/kinetics: presepsin is a soluble fragment (soluble CD14 subtype, sCD14-ST) released from monocytes/macrophages during phagocytosis of bacteria, proposed to rise within 1–2 hours of infection onset — earlier than procalcitonin (which typically rises over 6–12 hours) and considerably earlier than CRP (which can take 24–48 hours to peak), offering a theoretical advantage for very early detection.
  • Evidence strength: presepsin has a growing but still comparatively limited evidence base relative to CRP and procalcitonin, with meta-analyses suggesting reasonable diagnostic accuracy for sepsis but significant heterogeneity across studies, assay standardisation issues, and confounding by renal function (presepsin is renally cleared and elevated in renal impairment independent of infection) — it lacks the large-scale randomised outcome-trial evidence base and guideline endorsement that procalcitonin has accumulated (e.g., for antibiotic de-escalation decisions).
  • Current defensible position: presepsin should be described as a biologically plausible, kinetically attractive, but not yet routinely established biomarker for guiding sepsis management decisions; current major sepsis guidelines (Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021) do not endorse it as a standard-of-care test, in contrast to the more established (though still imperfect) roles of lactate and, in some contexts, procalcitonin for antibiotic stewardship — candidates should present it as an area of active research rather than established practice.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct definition/kinetics (4), correct balanced evidence-strength discussion (5), correct defensible current-role statement (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Presenting presepsin as an established, guideline-endorsed sepsis biomarker equivalent to lactate or procalcitonin. Overstating the maturity of emerging biomarker evidence is a specific and recurring EDIC exam trap — candidates should explicitly qualify evidence strength rather than presenting all biomarkers as equally validated.

References

  1. Zhang J, Hu ZD, Song J, Shao J. Diagnostic value of presepsin for sepsis: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Medicine (Baltimore). 2015;94(47):e2158.
  2. Evans L, Rhodes A, Alhazzani W, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021. Intensive Care Med. 2021;47(11):1181–1247.

CBS_BIO_47 — Vancomycin AUC-Guided Dosing: The 2020 Consensus Shift

Biochemistry / Data Sepsis / Infection CoBaTrICE 1·2, 4·1 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A patient with MRSA bacteraemia is on vancomycin. Trough levels have consistently been in the "target" 15–20 mg/L range, yet a pharmacokinetic calculation reveals a low estimated AUC24 relative to the MIC.

ParameterValueReference/interpretation
Vancomycin trough17 mg/LWithin older "target" 15–20 mg/L range
Estimated MIC1 mg/L—
Estimated AUC24380 mg·h/LTarget AUC24/MIC 400–600
Serum creatinineRising over 48hNephrotoxicity risk with trough-only high-target dosing

Structured questions

  1. Summarise the shift in vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring recommended by the 2020 ASHP/IDSA/PIDS/SIDP consensus guideline.
  2. Why might trough-only monitoring, as used here, both under-treat and over-expose a patient simultaneously (as illustrated by this vignette)?
  3. What is the target AUC24/MIC ratio for serious MRSA infections, and what practical methods exist to estimate AUC at the bedside?
Reveal model answer
  • 2020 consensus shift: the 2020 ASHP/IDSA/PIDS/SIDP consensus guideline recommends area-under-the-curve (AUC)-guided dosing and monitoring (targeting an AUC24/MIC ratio of 400–600, assuming a broth microdilution MIC of 1 mg/L) as the preferred approach for serious MRSA infections, replacing the older practice of targeting a trough concentration of 15–20 mg/L alone — this shift was driven by evidence that trough-only high-target dosing was associated with significantly increased nephrotoxicity without proven superior efficacy compared to AUC-guided dosing.
  • Why trough-only monitoring can both under- and over-expose: trough concentration correlates imperfectly with total drug exposure (AUC) because it is affected by dosing interval and individual clearance in ways that don't map linearly onto AUC; a patient can have a trough within the old "target" range while their true AUC24/MIC is below the efficacy threshold (under-treatment risk, as in this vignette) or, conversely, be exposed to unnecessarily high total drug exposure and nephrotoxicity risk while trough alone looks acceptable — AUC-guided dosing (using two-level pharmacokinetic sampling or validated software/nomograms) more accurately captures true exposure than a single trough value.
  • Target and methods: AUC24/MIC ≥400 (up to ~600 to balance nephrotoxicity risk) is the target for serious MRSA infections; practical bedside methods include first-order pharmacokinetic equations using two levels (peak and trough, or two troughs at steady state) or Bayesian dosing software, which are now recommended over trough-only nomograms in centres with the necessary infrastructure.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct 2020 consensus shift summary (5), correct explanation of trough-only limitation (4), correct target ratio and bedside method (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Answering "target trough 15–20 mg/L" as the current standard for vancomycin monitoring in serious MRSA infection. The 2020 consensus guideline (Rybak MJ, et al. Am J Health Syst Pharm. 2020;77(11):835–864) has moved practice to AUC-guided dosing; trough-only targeting of 15–20 mg/L is now considered outdated and associated with excess nephrotoxicity.

References

  1. Rybak MJ, Le J, Lodise TP, et al. Therapeutic monitoring of vancomycin for serious methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus infections: a revised consensus guideline. Am J Health Syst Pharm. 2020;77(11):835–864.

CBS_BIO_48 — Urine Chloride in the Differential Diagnosis of Metabolic Alkalosis

Biochemistry / Data Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 68-year-old woman on a nasogastric tube for prolonged high-output gastric drainage develops metabolic alkalosis. Urine electrolytes are requested to characterise the process.

ParameterValueReference/interpretation
Arterial pH7.537.35–7.45
Bicarbonate34 mmol/L22–26 mmol/L
Urine chloride8 mmol/L<20 mmol/L = chloride-responsive
Urine sodium18 mmol/LCan be misleading if bicarbonaturia present

Structured questions

  1. Why is urine chloride preferred over urine sodium to classify metabolic alkalosis?
  2. What does a urine chloride <20 mmol/L indicate, and what is the appropriate treatment?
  3. Give two causes of chloride-resistant (urine chloride >20 mmol/L) metabolic alkalosis.
Reveal model answer
  • Chloride vs sodium: in metabolic alkalosis, the kidney may excrete sodium along with bicarbonate (bicarbonaturia) to maintain electroneutrality, even in a volume-depleted, chloride-deficient state — this makes urine sodium an unreliable marker of effective circulating volume/chloride status in this specific setting. Urine chloride is not affected by obligate bicarbonate excretion in the same way and more reliably reflects true volume/chloride depletion.
  • Interpretation and treatment: a urine chloride <20 mmol/L (as here, from gastric fluid loss via nasogastric drainage) indicates chloride-responsive metabolic alkalosis, driven by volume depletion and chloride deficiency (with associated hypokalaemia and secondary hyperaldosteronism perpetuating the alkalosis); treatment is straightforward volume and chloride repletion with 0.9% saline (± potassium chloride replacement), which corrects the alkalosis by allowing the kidney to excrete the excess bicarbonate once volume/chloride is restored.
  • Chloride-resistant causes: primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn's syndrome) and other mineralocorticoid-excess states (e.g., Cushing's syndrome, exogenous corticosteroid excess, renal artery stenosis, Bartter/Gitelman syndromes, licorice ingestion) cause a chloride-resistant metabolic alkalosis (urine chloride >20 mmol/L) that does not correct with saline administration and instead requires treatment of the underlying mineralocorticoid excess or specific tubulopathy.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct chloride-vs-sodium reasoning (4), correct interpretation and treatment (4), two correct chloride-resistant causes (4).

Common error / drift trap: Using urine sodium alone to assess volume status in a patient with metabolic alkalosis and significant bicarbonaturia — this is a classic source of diagnostic confusion, and urine chloride is the more reliable discriminator in this specific acid- base disorder.

References

  1. Galla JH. Metabolic alkalosis. J Am Soc Nephrol. 2000;11(2):369–375.
  2. Khanna A, Kurtzman NA. Metabolic alkalosis. Respir Care. 2001;46(4):354–365.

CBS_BIO_49 — Cystatin C and the 2021 Race-Free CKD-EPI Equations

Biochemistry / Data Renal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·6 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 55-year-old man with reduced muscle mass following prolonged critical illness has an estimated GFR calculated by both creatinine-based and cystatin C-based equations, which give discrepant results.

ParameterValueComment
Serum creatinine62 µmol/LLow, reflecting reduced muscle mass
eGFR (creatinine-based CKD-EPI 2021)98 mL/min/1.73m²Likely overestimates true GFR
Serum cystatin C1.9 mg/L0.5–1.0 mg/L
eGFR (cystatin C-based CKD-EPI 2021)42 mL/min/1.73m²Likely more accurate here

Structured questions

  1. Why might creatinine-based eGFR overestimate true renal function in this specific patient?
  2. Summarise the 2021 CKD-EPI equation update and the rationale for removing the race coefficient.
  3. In what other clinical scenarios is cystatin C-based (or combined creatinine-cystatin C) eGFR preferred over creatinine alone?
Reveal model answer
  • Overestimation mechanism: serum creatinine is a product of muscle metabolism (creatine phosphate breakdown); in a patient with significant sarcopenia/muscle wasting from prolonged critical illness, creatinine production is reduced independent of renal function, producing a falsely low creatinine and thus a falsely high (overestimated) creatinine-based eGFR that does not reflect true glomerular filtration.
  • 2021 CKD-EPI update: the 2021 CKD-EPI creatinine and creatinine-cystatin C equations (Inker et al., NEJM 2021) removed the previously included race (Black vs non-Black) coefficient, which had been shown to systematically overestimate eGFR (and thus potentially delay diagnosis, referral, and transplant listing) in Black patients without robust physiological justification for the correction factor; the update aimed to provide a more equitable, race-free estimate, with cystatin C-based and combined equations offering improved accuracy across populations independent of race and muscle mass.
  • Other scenarios favouring cystatin C: extremes of muscle mass (both sarcopenia, as here, and very high muscle mass, e.g., bodybuilders — both distort creatinine-based estimates), amputees, malnutrition, liver disease (reduced creatine synthesis), pregnancy, and situations requiring more precise GFR estimation (e.g., potential living kidney donors, chemotherapy dosing) are all scenarios where cystatin C-based or combined equations are preferred over creatinine alone.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct overestimation mechanism (4), correct 2021 update summary and rationale (5), two or more correct additional scenarios (3).

Guideline-drift trap: Using or citing the older race-adjusted CKD-EPI creatinine equation (2009/2012 versions) as current standard. The 2021 race-free equations (Inker LA, et al. N Engl J Med. 2021;385(19):1737–1749) are now the recommended standard, and failing to mention this update, or citing an outdated equation, is a specific and testable currency gap.

References

  1. Inker LA, Eneanya ND, Coresh J, et al. New creatinine- and cystatin C-based equations to estimate GFR without race. N Engl J Med. 2021;385(19):1737–1749.
  2. Delanaye P, Cavalier E, Pottel H. Serum creatinine: not so simple! Nephron. 2017;136(4):302–308.

CBS_BIO_50 — Faecal Calprotectin: Cannot Distinguish Flare From Superimposed Infection

Biochemistry / Data Gastrointestinal CoBaTrICE 1·2, 2·4 12 marks · 12 min

Clinical context

A 29-year-old woman with known ulcerative colitis, currently on maintenance azathioprine, presents with worsening bloody diarrhoea and abdominal pain. Faecal calprotectin is markedly elevated.

ParameterValueReference range
Faecal calprotectin1800 µg/g<50 µg/g (normal); >150–250 µg/g suggests active inflammation
CRP85 mg/L<5 mg/L
Stool Clostridioides difficile PCR/toxinPending—
Stool cultures/CMV PCRPending—

Structured questions

  1. What does faecal calprotectin measure, and what is its evidence-based utility in inflammatory bowel disease?
  2. Why can an elevated faecal calprotectin not, by itself, distinguish a true IBD flare from a superimposed infection in an immunosuppressed patient like this one?
  3. What specific infection must be actively excluded before escalating immunosuppression in this scenario, and why?
Reveal model answer
  • Definition/utility: faecal calprotectin is a calcium- and zinc-binding protein released by neutrophils, and its faecal concentration correlates with the degree of intestinal mucosal neutrophilic inflammation. It has good evidence for distinguishing inflammatory bowel disease from non-inflammatory functional bowel disorders (e.g., irritable bowel syndrome), for monitoring disease activity/mucosal healing, and for predicting relapse in patients in clinical remission.
  • Cannot distinguish flare from infection: faecal calprotectin is a nonspecific marker of any process causing neutrophilic mucosal inflammation — it rises comparably with a true IBD flare, an infectious colitis (bacterial, or Clostridioides difficile), and CMV colitis reactivation, all of which can coexist with or mimic an IBD flare, particularly in an immunosuppressed patient. A markedly elevated calprotectin here confirms active inflammation but does not tell the clinician its cause.
  • Specific infection to exclude: Clostridioides difficile infection must be actively excluded (stool toxin/PCR) before escalating immunosuppression, as patients with IBD (and particularly those on immunosuppressive therapy) have a significantly increased risk of C. difficile infection, which can mimic or precipitate a flare, and escalating immunosuppression (e.g., corticosteroids, biologics) in the presence of undiagnosed C. difficile colitis risks severe complications including toxic megacolon and perforation. CMV colitis reactivation is a second key differential warranting specific testing (colonic biopsy with immunohistochemistry/PCR) before escalating immunosuppression in a refractory or atypical flare.
Marking guide

12 marks: correct calprotectin definition/utility (4), correct nonspecificity explanation (4), correct identification of C. difficile (and CMV) exclusion with rationale (4).

Guideline-drift trap: Escalating immunosuppression (e.g., high-dose corticosteroids) based solely on an elevated faecal calprotectin without first excluding C. difficile/CMV infection in an immunosuppressed IBD patient. This is a specific, testable, and clinically dangerous pattern — nonspecific inflammatory markers must be interpreted alongside a targeted infective workup before escalating immunosuppressive therapy.

References

  1. Ricciuto A, Griffiths AM. Clinical value of fecal calprotectin. Crit Rev Clin Lab Sci. 2019;56(5):307–320.
  2. Rahier JF, Magro F, Abreu C, et al. Second European evidence-based consensus on the prevention, diagnosis and management of opportunistic infections in inflammatory bowel disease. J Crohns Colitis. 2014;8(6):443–468.
